                 PREFACE .




SINCE it may be in the recollection of some who

peruse the following pages, that the author professed

his intention of not involving himself in any defence

of his former enquiry, some explanation is perhaps

due for having abandoned that      resolution.   The

reason is this : when I wrote my former volume, I

did not anticipate objections of that character which
have now been brought against me.      Differences of

opinion there must naturally be upon so complicated

a question as that which I attempted to solve, —the
causes and end    of the twofold revolution which

has been taking place in the religious belief of Pro-

testant Germany.      Having been compelled in my

preface to express my dissent from what I imagined

to be Mr. Rose's opinions upon the causes of German
Rationalism, I was fully prepared for any criticisms

upon my own.     Objections then to my theory upon
this subject, I did not intend to answer.   I found,


vi                      PREFACE .


however, with surprise and pain, that the strictures

passed upon me by Mr. Rose related for the most

part, not to the historical portions, but to theological

opinions supposed to be contained in my book ;

that he had wholly mistaken my views on a number

of important religious questions, as well as the cha-

racter and design of my volume ; and had in conse-

quence felt it his duty to animadvert very severely

upon it, for advocating opinions which I hold as

little as himself.   No one probably, who read those

censures, would feel any inclination to peruse the

work which was the object of them ; and although

some might hope that charges so grave were in

some measure the effect of misconception, few pro-

bably would suppose that they were wholly without

foundation .    Even those who might be at the pains

to read my work, would naturally attach to my ex-

pressions the same meaning which had thus been

affixed to them.     As, moreover, I perfectly acquit

Mr. Rose of wilful misconception, I could not but

fear that my opinions might be mistaken by others

also.    It was moreover stated, that these my sup-

posed views were calculated to injure some of the

future    Ministers of our Church :    little influence,

therefore, as I myself suppose they would have had,


PREFACE .                      vii


it became a duty to the Church, since I did not hold

them, to disavow them .



   I will not deny, however, that private feelings

have had their influence.       No one would willingly

remain an object of suspicion ; nor, especially in a

period of some anxiety for the temporary welfare of

our Church, be unduly supposed to hold opinions

injurious to her well-being .        Yet having   mingled
but little in theological society out of my own uni-

versity, there are comparatively few to whom my

opinions are sufficiently known to enable them to
vindicate   me .   One    there was,     the   counsellor ,

friend, and guide of all who had the happiness to

come within his influence, to whom I owe a debt of

gratitude which could never have been repaid, who

had for six years shewn me the kindness of a second

father, and in the enjoyment of whose theological in-

struction my opinions had been principally formed .
With his counsels and his countenance I should not

have dreaded any reproach .      But a mysterious Pro-

vidence has withdrawn to his rest and reward, him

whom I, in common with others, venerated as one of

the ablest human supports of our Church ; and I

have lost the guardian friend, with whose guidance


viii                   PREFACE .


I had hoped to steer securely amid all the difficult

shoals, through which the course of a theologian

must in these days probably be held .



   It having thus become necessary to enter into some

explanation, I could omit no portion of the varied

points of attack.   Although I have thus been com-

pelled to enter into questions , which were but inci-

dentally connected with my Enquiry, none could be

omitted without the appearance of being abandoned

as incapable of defence.     Yet I have endeavoured

throughout, as far as the case admitted , to illustrate

my original Enquiry, and to furnish some additional

data, for those who may hereafter pursue the sub-

ject.     This was not, however, either the time or

place to attempt to fill up the brief outline which I

originally gave : others, I trust, may not be deterred

from considering a problem, in itself of the deepest

interest, and of practical importance, certainly to in-

dividuals in this country, possibly to our country

itself.   My own wish was to furnish a chart only,

not a picture of the land.    Those opportunities of

illustration which offered themselves I gladly seized ,

as enabling me to escape from continued controversy.

On the same ground I have throughout remained


PREFACE.                     ix


upon the defensive .   I have omitted also any notice
                       I

of the strictures upon Professor Sack, since an ex-

planation of the points in which he also has been

misconceived would have required much additional

controversy, and my valued friend, thinking such ex-

planation wholly unnecessary, wished me to abstain

from it any further than might be expedient for myself.



  Some apology is perhaps due to Mr. Rose for

the length of time which has elapsed, since his pub-

lication appeared, and the consequent protraction of
this discussion.   Most of the following pages were

indeed written within the two first months after the

appearance of his strictures : an enquiry into the

opinions of the Fathers upon Inspiration , which was

found to be of disproportionate length to be here
admitted, and other circumstances, into which I

need not enter, have unavoidably caused delay.



   Having already fully explained the use which was

made of the notes taken from Professor Tholuck's

lectures, I have only, in justice to my kind and

valued friend, to repeat, that as these notes were

taken by a student from his public lectures, not from

his MS. he cannot be responsible for them. Although


X                     PREFACE.


I am assured, from subsequent examination , that in

those parts which I employed there are no inac-

curacies affecting the general    results, there   may

be some among the minor points, which I have

not been able to trace.    Although I am not aware

of any such, it is but due to this eminent Christian

theologian, from whom I have experienced so much

kindness, to state, that his character ought not in

any case to be compromised by them.



    The following pages will, I trust, prove satisfac-

tory   yet it is possible that I may still have failed

fully to express my meaning upon some points : it

will, however, I hope, be borne in mind, that it is

often the very fullness of our conviction which pre-

vents our dwelling upon maxims and truths which

are generally acknowledged : and even such impu-

tations, as those to which I have lately been ex-

posed, cannot easily efface the habits of a period in

which I neither entertained, nor was the object of

suspicion.



    I trust that those who may undertake to judge

of the following pages, will form that judgment as

far as possible from the work itself, not fastening


PREFACE .                    xi


upon me the opinions of any party ; and I shall then

not dread that my views will again be held up as

pernicious to that Church, to whose service I have

pledged myself, and whom I love and admire.




  Christ Church,
 March 30, 1830.

AN


    HISTORICAL                      ENQUIRY,

                     CHAPTER I.


Explanation ofthe view taken by the Author of Mr. Rose's sup-
  posed theory on the Origin of German Rationalism - Objects
  of the Author in writing the Enquiry - Charges brought by
 Mr. Rose against the Author.


Ir is the most painful part of public discussion,
that some degree of mutual misconception is pro-
bably almost unavoidable.          Little acquainted as the
parties who engage in it generally are with each
other, the best commentary on the opinions which
each expresses, the knowledge of his private charac-
ter and sentiments, is generally wanting.             Each,
perhaps naturally, sees the sentiments of the other,
as they are reflected in the mirror of some party, to
whom he supposes his opponent to belong, rather
than as they stand in themselves ; and a few expres-
sions inadvertently used, or perhaps the observation
of a very limited coincidence of opinion in some por-
tions of any question, are sufficient to induce each
to rank the other with a class with whom he has in
other respects no connection .        The evil thus caused
                             B


2


to an individual is often very extensive.      Not to

 speak of the pain of being     separated from those
 whom he most respects, as promoting in a higher
degree the great objects to which he himself hopes
to devote whatever abilities God has given him, and
of being looked upon by them as an object of sus-
picion, all hopes of future usefulness are often at
once blighted ; any subsequent endeavour to benefit
the Church, to whose good he is bound to conse-
crate his exertions, is neutralized by the mistrust
thus attached to him.

   These painful misconceptions, however, have also
their use .  The very frequency of these mutual

errors has a tendency to repress any rising murmur at
mistakes, which in the consciousness of our own right
intentions, we may conceive might, and ought to have
been avoided ; and however we may have to drink
deeper than others of the bitter potion, we learn at
most to regret, not to complain.   The task becomes
easier when we have ourselves misconceived another.

And since, in my own case, my earnest wish to un-
derstand Mr. Rose's views in his book on German
Protestantism,   did not,   according to his present

statement of them, prevent my mistaking them in a
fundamental point, while it becomes necessary to
explain this misconception, it requires no great ex-
ertion of charity to think or speak mildly of any to
which I may in my turn have been exposed.
  It is, however, necessary, before I attempt to clear
my own opinions, shortly to state the origin of my
impressions with regard to those of Mr. Rose . The

theory, which it now appears I attributed erroneously
                         1


3


to him, was this ; that " there existed so great a ten-
dency in the human mind, notwithstanding the privi-
leges afforded to it by Christianity, to abandon that
very religion, as well as its fundamental doctrines, that
some external human authority was absolutely ne-
cessary to restrain it within these bounds." These

impressions, I must say, I shared with many in Ger-
many as well as in this country.     Here I frequently
saw the principles, which I supposed to be his, de-
fended and systematized ; on one occasion expanded
and illustrated by an appeal to the general history
of the present day in the different religious deno-
minations in our own country and in America, Mr.
Rose's work being referred to as establishing satis-
factorily the truth of the principle with regard to
Germany.     In Germany, the same idea of Mr.

Rose's views exposed him to very serious animad-
versions, and I had often to vindicate his character
and right-mindedness to men who had no taint of
Rationalism , but were themselves engaged in the
great work of restoring genuine Christianity.       The
same feelings which induced me to take every occa-
sion of removing the unfavourable impressions which
resulted from Mr. Rose's supposed views, would have
induced me to have formed, if possible, correct
notions of them.    Yet, such as I stated them, they
seemed to me to lie on the whole surface,            and
to be the very object of his book.       I found there
the fatal opinions of rationalism described as errors
" from which the most extensive learning, and (he
doubted not) the purest intentions could not rescue
their authors ." I found inferred from this " the
                           B 2


4


absolute necessity of some check and restraint over
the human mind in every religious society, and
especially over its ministers ," (accordingly not
limited to the clergy, but extending to the laity
also) .    In the same context, especial importance was
attached to " that particular part of the excellence of
our own Church, which consists in this very control .”
I found it stated that too many who acknowledged
" the wholesomeness of the general               effect of the
system , and of the general tone of the doctrine of our
Church, did not trace very narrowly the source
whence the blessing flowed," or observe that " the
benefit we reaped is attributable to the controlling
form of our peculiar system of Church government,
to the binding power of the Articles which guide
our faith, and the Liturgy which directs our devo-
tion 2."    To the spirit of control observable in each
part of the system, I found, according to the same
context, that " we owed the very existence of the
Church, and of all the blessings of her pure pri-

mitive form of Christianity ." I found, in p . 14,
the same three points mentioned as " the safe-

guards which must be requisite to the preservation
of any Church³. " In these passages, then, I did
think that the view which I ascribed to Mr. Rose,


   1 Discourses, p. 11.                       2 Ibid. p. 12.
     It is necessary to state that these passages, as I am glad to
see, are omitted in the second edition , which was not published
when I wrote the above explanation ; nor could I, under any
circumstances, have avoided in my own justification alluding to
the work from which alone any opinion could be formed when I
made the comments in question.


5


was implied, and all but expressed .           The very exis-
tence of our own Church was expressly ascribed to
these restraints ; the fall of the German Church was
considered as an illustration of their absolute neces-

sity ; they were said to be the necessary safe- guards
of any Church¹.         I scarcely see then how I could
avoid the error of supposing that Mr. Rose thought
that " the relaxation of the binding force of its arti-
cles" would in each Church " produce not merely
deviation from its doctrines within the bounds of

Christianity, but the abandonment of the principles
and authority of Christianity itself ."             These ex-
pressions of the absolute necessity of control appeared
to me still further illustrated by a subsequent state-
ment, " that not many years had elapsed after the
death of Calixtus ( 1656 ) ere the want of a sufficient
controlling power in the German Churches began to
display itself in the formation of a variety of opi-
nions 3." To what circumstances Mr. Rose here al-

ludes, is scarcely explained sufficiently ; yet, as the
death of Calixtus was a full century before the com-
mencement of Rationalism in the Church itself, I
know of no opinions over which the exertion of such
control would not exceed what any Protestant Church
ought to claim over its members.       Towards the

   ¹ It can hardly, after this, be necessary to say that I did not
make " a singular confusion between the preservation of Chris-
tianity and the welfare of particular Churches." (Letter, p. 41. )
If, as I understood Mr. Rose to say, these safeguards were abso-
lutely necessary to the existence of any Church, then, of course,
they were to the aggregate of Christian Churches, i. e. to Chris-
tianity itself.
   2
     My Preface, p. x.                     ³ Discourses, p. 35 .


6


Pietists not only were very severe measures of con-
trol exerted ' , but a very harsh and injudicious inter-
ference was often employed .
     I must beg then, once more, to disclaim having
attributed to Mr. Rose any other view than this, that
he seemed to me to regard the restrictions of human
formulæ as the bulwark without which Christianity

could not be maintained in any Church, and that by
this opinion he unintentionally " derogated from the
independence and the inherent power of the word of
God "."     Could it be proved that God had intended
that the maintenance of the revelation which he be-
stowed upon mankind should in each country or
Church depend upon the use of these human means,
no Christian, of course, could doubt the wisdom of
any condition which He annexed to the continuance
of the blessing. But unless this were shewn, I thought
it a dangerous supposition that these human means
were " absolutely necessary," not merely to the
well-being, but to the existence of Christianity, in each
Church, and consequently to the maintenance of our
holy faith itself; -that it tended to turn the attention
away from the real supports of our religion, while it
threw a stumbling-block in the way of the sceptic,
and gave a formidable weapon into the hands of the
unbeliever.     Mr. Rose, however, has now explicitly
declared that he does not hold this doctrine, and it
gives me the sincerest pleasure to find , that he at
least does not raise these outworks round the im-

pregnable fortress of Christianity to a height, which

 1                                        Preface, p. x.
     Enquiry, p. 87 and 98.


7


could only disguise its mighty structure, and might
perhaps give a vantage post to the enemy.
   I stand charged, however, not only with mistak-
ing Mr. Rose's views, but with carelessness in so
doing, and this then I must, in a few words, remove.
He says, 66 on recurring to my work to see what

grounds any carelessness of phrase might afford for
such imputations, I found there that I had not only
pointed out the withdrawing of the controlling su-
perintendence as the means only, not the cause, but
had absolutely (p. 10. 1. 8. ) printed the word ' means'
in Italics ."   Mr. Rose, however, has not observed
that the word means stood in his book opposed not
to the causes but to the effects of Rationalism .
He had said, indeed ,       in contrast to the effects
produced by Rationalism,       that it was " rather

to the means by which such dreadful results were
effected, to which he wished to direct attention."
The use of the word ' means' however, could furnish
no guide, since when standing in opposition to effects,
the means and cause would in common language
often coincide.     To use once more the illustration

originally employed, the breaking down of an em-
bankment might be equally said to have been the
cause of the inundation , or the means by which the
inundation was effected .    Supposing it to be true
that the river was uniformly so much above the level
of the surrounding country, that it had need of em-
bankments to prevent, not merely its doing occasional
damage, but its deluging the whole country, then


          Letter, p. 37.              2 Ibid. p. 10.


8


the breaking down the dam, or without metaphor,
the removal of the restraints must be the cause of

the inundation .   To employ an useful technical dis-
tinction, the accumulation of water , or, without a
metaphor, the tendency to go wrong would be the
ultimate cause of inundation ; its proximate cause,
or the means, would be the breaking of the embank-
ment, or the removal of control ; yet, wherever the
ultimate cause is in constant operation , it is naturally
the proximate cause or means, which is singled out
as the cause of the event.

  With these impressions, and with the knowledge
that Mr. Rose's book was looked upon as accounting
for the origin of German Rationalism, I shall hardly
need any further apology for having stated my

grounds for thinking that it could not answer that
purpose. I did not wish to impute to him as a fault,
that he had not satisfied this, which was not his
main object, but since I did infer from his book that
it was a collateral one, I could not, when about to
publish a new enquiry upon the subject, but express
an opinion on those views which, if correct, would
supersede any further investigation.
  In the Enquiry itself, not only was there no direct
reference to Mr. Rose's work, but to avoid a contro-
versial air, I did not even look into his volume from
the time that I commenced the immediate prepara-

tion for my own, until this portion was actually
printed.   Some few observations in it may have been
occasioned by the recollection of opinions expressed
or implied in Mr. Rose's work, with which mine did
not coincide, but these opinions were shared also by


9


others, nor do I think that any one remark had the
appearance of being directed against him .         My real
objects in making that enquiry I partly stated in
the commencement of the essay itself '.            The re-
cent revolutions in Germany are in themselves a
subject of intense though painful interest. To some
minds I found that they had become even appalling.
We had already seen the desolation of Christianity
in France, we had heard of the unbelief prevalent in
Spain and Italy, but painful as this was, there was
in it nothing astonishing.       In France political excite-
ment had drowned the voice of religion ; in Italy
and Spain, no resting place was known between an
irrational and superstitious belief, and an absolute
rejection of revelation .        Romanism had not only
been represented as the sole form of Christianity,
but the name of Protestant had studiously been made
equivalent to that of unbeliever.          It did, however,
become to these minds a more appalling source of
anxiety, when they learnt that the same fearful
changes had taken place in Germany also, the birth-
place of the Reformation : similar reports being cir-
culated with regard to North America, they knew
not amid the threatenings of these gathering storms ,
how to hope that they should not in their course
burst upon our own land. It was then , I thought, even
in this point of view, a legitimate object to attempt
to show the causes which had led to this defection ,

as well as to correct the exaggerated reports which
I had heard circulated of its extent, and to point to the

                   1
                       Enquiry, p. 1—3 .


10


cheering dawn which has now been for some time
breaking.      I thought too that this voluntary return
to pure Christianity, after such various deviations,
furnished a sublime instance of its inherent force,
and showed how deeply its dominion over the human
heart was founded .      Yet I felt also, as I stated, that
this history had both national and individual uses ;
to individuals, in that the events, which took place
in Germany are, on a small scale, often repeated in
the little world of a single mind ; nationally, be-
cause, though with " some similarities, there is, I
trust, still sufficient which is dissimilar (in the state
of the two countries) to justify the hope that we shall
be preserved from a similar visitation ' ," still no one
can behold all the appearances of the present day
without some disquietude, or some anticipations of
an approaching conflict, perhaps of unusual difficulty
or extent. In such times, I thought that an account
of the circumstances which led to the struggle in
Germany might not be without its use, and that
amid the various conflicting tendencies of mind now
prevailing among us, something might be learnt from
the evils incident to the several parties in Germany,
without any appearance of invidiously reflecting on
respectable divines, or bodies of men in England ;
and that especially we might see in the history of
another country, the dangerous effects of the un-
healthy excitement now widely reigning among the
laymen in this ?.
  Before I leave this subject, I must assure Mr.

    1                                2 Ibid. p. 12
        Enquiry, p. 3.                            0. sqq.


11


 Rose, that nothing could have been further from my
thoughts than the " holding him up to public con-
tempt for incompetence and ignorance '," nor can I
conceive what in my preface (in which alone I alluded
to the work) would be so interpreted .
   Since, however, I have involuntarily done Mr. Rose
injustice, by attributing to him the above view, (which
I cannot think was without apparent foundation , al-
though I gladly accept his present explanation, ) while
I wish to express my regret for an error which I was
unable to avoid, I complain not of the manner in
which all the opinions which he has selected out of
my book        have   been in their        turn   misconceived.
The mistakes are indeed numerous, and calculated,
under the stamp of his authority, and infinitely more
under the implied sanction of the very distinguished
Prelate to whom he has addressed his observations,

to render me an object of suspicion to many, whose
general views I share, and whose characters I admire.
   I am now indeed fully conscious of the obscurity
of parts of a book, written amidst harassing illness,
and   published       amidst    distress    and distraction of
mind ; the chief cause, however, of the misconcep-
tions under which I am suffering is, I imagine, what
I have mentioned as perhaps unavoidable in contro-
versy, that Mr. Rose, namely, has unintentionally
confounded me with some party of whose existence I
was wholly ignorant ; and ascribed to me their opi-
nions, although contrary to what I took much pains
to express .


                       Discourses, p. 16 , & c.


12

                        1
  It appears also           that the supposed views of my
Enquiry had been stated to him before it actually
appeared ; with a mind thus pre-occupied, miscon-
ception was natural, perhaps inevitable.
  The charges then which it has become necessary
to clear, relate in part to supposed theological opi-
nions incidentally expressed or implied in my En-
quiry, in part to matters of fact, upon which the
theory on the cause of German Rationalism has

been there supported .             Those charges, which bear
principally upon theological points are,                       1st, The
omission of any mention of the absence of Episco-
pacy among the causes of German Rationalism, im-
plying apparently indifference to it '.                  2nd, An in-
temperate opposition to all articles and all restraint ³,
as well as to all human safeguards and protections *.
3d, A hatred of all human systems 5 , as necessarily
producing a dead orthodoxism.                     4th, A supposed
statement that the inspiration of the historical parts
of Scripture , nay, that of the Gospels themselves ',
is a vulgar error.  5th, Great liberalism on theolo-

gical subjects, implied , I imagine, by the expression
of a ' new era of theology , so that I am described
as " zealous" indeed " for Christianity" but " ac-
cording to my own views of it "," and am asked to
which of the great lights of Rationalism (such as
 Paullus, Wegscheider, &c . ) I would refer for ex-
 amples of right principles in theology 10. 6th, A com-

mendation of the scattering doubts on the truth of
religion, or the genuineness of Scripture ' .        Those
which affect my Enquiry principally are, that, misled
by a hatred to orthodoxism, I have maintained a theory
untenable in itself , which I have propped up by the
exaggeration of all that was evil, and the suppres-
sion of what good was mingled with the evil during
the period of German orthodoxy³, while I am an
apologist for the Rationalists , nay, for some of the
worst features of their system 5.          Each of these
classes of charges bears in some respects upon the
other.   Although, therefore, I might not be justi-
fied in claiming the attention of any portion of the
public , much less of those who are engaged in pro-
fessional pursuits for the good of all, to the explana-
tion of opinions expressed by one so little known to
the theological world as myself, still since they affect
more or less a great subject, I may be permitted to
clear them as briefly as the case admits.          For my
own vindication it might have been sufficient to say
that I do value episcopacy, that I do consider articles
a blessing, that I regard systems of faith as benefi-
cial and even indispensable, that I do hold the inspi-
ration of the Gospels, that I do not refer to the Ra-
tionalists (Paullus, Wegscheider, &c.) for right prin-
ciples in theology, that I abhor the wanton circulation
of doubts on the truth of religion or of Scripture. Yet
such naked statements are generally disbelieved or at
most disregarded : it might still be said that it was
  1                            2
  ¹ Letter, p. 156-159.            Ib. p. 107-115, 129–144 .
  3 lb. p. 115-128.                    Ib. p . 18 , 104.
  5 Ib. P. 97, 98.


14


the tendency, if not the intention , of my volume, to
inculcate the opposite errors ; and the views of that
volume with regard to the origin of German Ration-
alism , which I cannot but think of practical import-
ance, might be compromised by these supposed ap-
pendages to them.
  It is indeed a painful task to have to clear away
imputations, foreign to every sentiment which I
cherish ; it is yet more painful that, ushered into the
world as these charges have been, I can scarcely
entertain a hope ever wholly to efface them : like the
separate counts against a criminal, each would appear
to the by-standers to throw a degree of probability
upon the remainder ;     no one could, it would be
thought, contract so much suspicion without some
share of guilt.

  I must indeed bid farewell thus early to those
hopes, with which every one sets out in public life,
to be free, in the opinions of all, " ab omni non solum
facto, verum opprobrio quoque turpi ;" where much
reproach is cast, however undeserved, some must
adhere ; yet had I not thus collected these charges,
it might have been thought that some portion of
them had been withheld or glossed over, and I trust
that if any will undertake the labor of going through
them with me, my opinions will appear neither un-
founded nor dangerous .


CHAPTER II .


 Omission of the absence of Episcopacy among the Causes of
                     German Rationalism .


 THOROUGHLY sensible of the real blessings of Epis-
 copacy in our own Church, and doubting not that
it is better adapted than any other form for all the
purposes of Church Government, I yet saw no
grounds for supposing that if it had existed in Ger-
many at the time of the commencing Rationalism,
it would have prevented the subsequent degeneracy.
Mr. Rose, however, complains of this omission as most
singular.    " Mr. Pusey," he says, " is a member of
an episcopal establishment, and yet he does not ap-
pear to consider the abrogation of this apostolical
form of church government in Germany a matter even
worth notice ¹."

   It is difficult to judge how far any system of
Church polity practically acts in a foreign country ;
                                                 2
in theory the General- Superintendents are stated to
have had the same offices and powers as our Bishops,

although in practice , probably, they were too much
fettered by the state.
  As far however as past experience, or the nature
of things could guide me, I could see no reason to
think that a different form of Church Government

would have changed the destinies of the German
Church.     Episcopal Denmark, as I understood , had

  ¹ Letter, p. 99, 100. and see note, p . 425.
  2 See Jablonski's Memor. on introducing Episcopacy into
Prussia, (Life of Abp. Sharp, ii . 205. sqq.)


16


suffered from   rationalism    equally with Germany,
while Scotland, although Presbyterian, had remained
nearly free from it, and Holland had suffered com-
paratively little.   In this country also, its attacks
were repelled not by Episcopal authority, but by the
efforts of Christian theologians.     The first ranks
among these indeed were occupied, as was to be ex-
pected, by many distinguished Bishops of that day,
but they acted as Christian divines, not in their
Episcopal capacity; the same weapons were employed
by all in the holy warfare.    I thought too, that the
difference of the form, which unbelief had in general
assumed in this and other Protestant countries, from
that in which it appeared in the land of Voltaire, La
Mettrie, &c. was in part indeed to be ascribed to
national character, in part also to the different form
of Christianity to which it was opposed, but in some
measure also to the greater freedom which here al-
lowed the evil free vent, while in Romanist countries
it had been pent up and confined, and had thus fes-
tered in the system .   The utmost which human

authority can avail in opposition to unbelief, is to re-
press its outward appearance. The melancholy state
of the Spanish and Italian clergy, where, amidst
the perfect external show of implicit belief, almost
all at the core is corruption and rottenness, so that
there is not even that earnestness of mind and ad-
herence to the truths of what is called natural reli-

gion, which are found among most of the German
Rationalists, is a proof that the requisition of out-
ward conformity is unequal to check the progress of
the disease, though it aggravates its character and
prevents its cure.


17


  But be this as it may, for nearly two centuries
and a half, i . e. up to the very commencement of the
Rationalist times, a very " sufficient" and absolute
"authority" was exercised in the German church ; in
every controversy which arose from the period of the
Reformation to that time, nay, in the Pietistic con-
troversies themselves, an uncompromising, and I
thought, often ill-placed discipline was employed . The
diminished exertion of that authority was, therefore,
I supposed, to be looked for in other causes ; not in
the name under which the authority was exercised .
   It is painful to be obliged, at every step, to protest
against being supposed to undervalue our own insti-
tutions. But it is necessary to remember the nature of
the question which I had to solve ; it does not relate
to the ordinary exercise of church government ; but
to its efficacy in extraordinary emergencies ; it is not,
whether one form be more adapted than another for
promoting, under favoring circumstances, the great
objects of the Redeemer's kingdom, but whether any
form be able to prevent not merely individual, but
general defection .The question, then , (and in these
days it cannot be invidious) " Quis custodiet ipsos
custodes ?" must recur alike under every form of
government.     It is, indeed, much more probable, in
ordinary cases, that where a government is vested
in a few, its members should be well selected, and
consequently that the institution should answer its
original design, than where the power is confided to
a larger number, and the selection becomes, conse-
quently, more difficult .   If one may use the expres-
                            C


18


sion, an aristocratical form would be more likely to
be well ordered than a republican.   Yet every Body

of men, however constituted , must partake, more or
less, of the general character of those from whom it
is formed ; and unquestionably very much so , when
the appointment is vested in any set of men external
to that Body.    There can then be no security in
times of prevailing degeneracy or coldness, that this
genuine aristocracy (whether of Bishops or of Gene-
ral Superintendents, ) should be perpetuated ; i. e.
that the higher order should be at all times selected
with reference to the principles on which it was con-
stituted   and consequently the form of church go-
vernment alone can furnish no sufficient safeguard.
In such times, I know not what ground there is to
conclude that those selected for the highest stations
of the Church, whether chosen by the Church, or,
as among ourselves, by laymen, would have been
more likely to repel the tide of innovation than the
rest of the Clergy.   It is, further, the misfortune,
not the fault of our Church, that so much power,

which originally and properly belonged to her Bi-
shops, has passed away ; but the fact is painfully
notorious, that not only the Scotch Church, but
every class of dissenters, has more power over unfit
ministers, whether in doctrine or in practice, than
ourselves.
  The whole history of the later German Church
might indeed have been different , had there been an
authority not only able, but willing, to distinguish
what was essential from what was unessential or un-


19


tenable in the previous system ; and which, while it
required from the     Ministers of the Church that
they should teach the great truths of Christianity,
should have allowed a freedom of opinion on other
points.     Had this been done, not only would there
have been more probability that such articles would
have been subscribed carefully and honestly, but a
great portion of the gathering opposition might have
been averted.     Many who, as Semler , saw that the
existing system was faulty, without possessing suffi-
cient clearness to discriminate between the human
additions and the original truth, might have been
rescued from their perplexities ; and many a theory,
which in itself had no tendency to affect any but the
unsound parts, might thus have been prevented from
communicating a shock to the whole.     Could the
delicate task have been performed of removing the
weakly portions of the building, and replacing them
with firmer materials, the edifice might have stood
unshaken, although the rains and the winds, and
the flood, by which it was assailed, had still been
permitted to try the firmness of its foundation.  I
cannot, indeed, but hope that hereafter the Confes-
sion of Augsburg, venerable as the first Protestant
Confession, as a monument of unshaken faith in that
period of difficulty and danger, no less than for the
sake of the great men who composed it, and dear to
us also, as the source of much in our own articles,
may hereafter be restored to its original eminence
as the sole symbolical book of the German church.
I also cannot but think that a genuine episcopal
form      of government,   combined with the synods,
                           C 2


20


which parts of the German Church already possess,
will in the better times, of which there seems now

an ample promise, be a permanent blessing to it.
Alterations, however, in a system, though often sug-
gested by experience,         are in general never less
thought of or acted upon, than in the hour                      in
which they are most needed.             When a system is
attacked, whether in a political or in an ecclesiastical
body, all of any eminence are generally engaged
either in maintaining or in opposing it as a whole ;
few can detach themselves sufficiently from the ob-
ject of immediate interest, to consider impartially
whether any portion of what as a whole they admire,
may be advantageously parted with.              Should even
such persons be found, and should they be suffi-
ciently gifted, to become under other circumstances
generally influential, it rarely happens, that amidst
the conflict of opinions, they are able to              induce
others to adopt measures which virtually condemn
both parties.      Of this character had been, in earlier
times , Melanchthon and his school, but they were
suspected of wishing to bring in, not merely the
Calvinistic, but the Zwinglian doctrines on the Lord's
Supper : a similar line had again been taken by
Musæus and Glassius in the Calixtine controversies,

but the consequence was, that all the errors imputed
to Calixtus were charged upon them.             An interme-
diate party ' again existed at the commencement of
the last crisis,    and had time been allowed, they
might ultimately have corrected the existing system ;


   ¹ That of Pfaff, Buddeus , &c.; see Enquiry, p. 110. sqq .


21


but they were themselves suspected of a leaning to
Pietism and heterodoxy ; nor were any of them of suf-
ficient calibre to produce a decided effect in such
troubled times. The ultra-orthodox party had, more-
over, been hitherto, as a body, triumphant, though their
system had received severe shocks from Calixtus,
Thomasius, Arnold , and the Pietists ; and these very
attacks, as was natural, increased their indisposition
voluntarily to yield what they had hitherto, at least
in appearance, successfully maintained .  Nor do I
see any probability that such combatants as Göze or
the rest of the ultra-Lutheran party would have
been more likely to listen to such a compromise,
much less to propose it, had they been invested with
episcopal authority. There were, moreover, other
impediments ; adherence to the Confessions of the
German Church was considered as the condition of
its enjoying the privileges of the religious peace ;
and this, whether the real ground or no, was assigned
as a reason against any such alteration , when it was
actually proposed.    I know not what weight this
last ground may really have had ; but for this ob-
stacle, however, I am not aware of any difficulty in
effecting such a change, arising in the constitution
of the German church, (supposing the wish to have
existed,) beyond what there would have been in a
parallel case in our own.


CHAPTER III.



Supposed Disparagement of Articles- Source of the Mistake-
  Value attached to English Articles ; and Grounds of it-
  Later German Articles alone condemned.



MR. ROSE's opinions on articles, as stated in his pre-
sent work, agree for the most part with those which
I took pains in two places in my original preface to
express¹ ; yet I am throughout his new work repre-
sented as almost a fanatical enemy of all Articles
and Confessions, and of all restraint.           The following
passage out of very many may shew the nature of
the charge.      " I never have denied, I do not now
deny, that evils may, or if Mr. Pusey pleases, must
arise from the use of Articles, as well as from
every other human institution. - The first and chief
of these evils is, that in Mr. Pusey's words   ' the
human mind can [could ] take no step except in the
leading-strings of authority ;' that it is [was] ac-
customed       to derive its Christian knowledge from
the mechanical study of the letter of a confused
[confined     form , that in short the use of articles

tends to repress all freedom of enquiry, and to
make men exalt a human system above Scripture , or
at any rate to the same level.            The fear of these

have yet had the easier task of examining an
existing system by the test of Scripture, instead
 of that of constructing it for ourselves from the
ruins of one deformed by human additions ."
  This is in truth but a modification of that which

I stated in my preface to be the use of articles to
individuals.    The process indeed here implied is the
converse of what was there supposed ; the individual
in the one case comparing the sum of doctrine which
he had collected from Scripture with the results of
the enquiries of pious and enlightened men in former
ages ; in the other setting out from these results and
comparing them with Scripture.      In practice both
probably are partially combined.   It were indeed one
of the vainest proposals of empiricism to recommend in
this momentous subject, that each should undertake
for himself, unassisted and unguided , the study of the
truths revealed in the Bible.   It were contrary to the
whole order of Providence which has made us so to

such an amazing extent indeed, but so wisely, de-
pendent upon each other ; and if consistently pur-
sued, it could not, as has often been remarked , stop
short of the rejection of all education and of all the
advances made in the several sciences by the united
efforts of ages. We must on such a plan be constantly
going back to the first principles, and never carrying
them on to perfection, and for the most part for
ever learning, and never coming to any knowledge
of the truth.    The benefits, indeed, to be derived
from this assistance in the examination of the com-
plex aggregate of doctrines contained in the Bible,
must   vary indefinitely   according to the    natural


26


strength, capacity, and character of the several indi-
viduals, to whom it is offered by the Church.    It
is undoubtedly impossible that among the very large

body, necessarily employed in giving religious in-
struction to a whole nation,           there should not be
very many, who would have been incapable of dis-
covering for themselves the whole system of Chris-
tianity from the Bible.      Nor is this any disadvantage,
since the means are provided to remedy the defi-
ciency. Few, probably, on the other hand , or none,
who would be called to such an office, are incapable
of examining such a scheme by Scripture, and satis-
fying themselves of its truth.          To this class the pos-
session of such a system, recommended not merely
by individuals but by the church to which they be-
long, is obviously an incalculable blessing .              Yet I
doubt much whether this be the class , upon whom
the existence of such a system confers the greatest
benefit.  These would bring probably few precon-
ceived notions to the examination , and the deficiency

would accordingly be rather a failure to perceive the
whole truth, than positive error .         It is , I should ima-
gine, to those of more powerful but irregular under-

relates to God, is comparatively the easiest, because
it is unfathomable.      These truths require indeed to
be ordered and systematized , but beyond this, the

most powerful and the humblest intellect have only
the common office of acquiescing. The other portion
of the divine disclosures, which relates to man, must

necessarily be obscure to those who can from their

of explaining that it was this practical knowledge of Scripture, aris-

I intended by the term experience' in my Enquiry. It was this
knowledge, upon which the earlier and excellent Pietists so much in-

rejected by their pseudo- orthodox opponents, and themselves cen-

truths of Christianity in the declining orthodox school, and ended

the systems by which it was opposed, but to which it had been

which are found upon a practical acquaintance with

Scriptures , are at an earlier stage of the Christian
career often unintelligible, at least in their full depth.
Imperfectly conceived as they must be, until an en-

comprehend them, both in themselves, and in refer-

to the difference of the natural disposition, either in-
adequately conceived or actually distorted .           Under
such circumstances, the value of a scheme of doctrine,
digested by eminent Christians, and containing the
results of their advanced Christian knowledge, will
be depreciated probably by no one who has ever
availed himself of its advantages.        Yet even indepen-
dently of these cases , the incalculable advantages of
such an aid in setting out upon the examination of
Scripture are sufficiently evident.      One cannot doubt,
indeed, that very many would in time arrive at that
scheme which we believe to have been truly formed
from the same sources. Still there would be but an
unnecessary expenditure of time and strength : the
human mind is less active when vaguely examining
in quest of an uncertain result, than when striving
to ascertain whether a given proposition be true .
It is every where of importance in enquiry to have
definite objects in view, and the more definite these

held and explained by a great and valuable early writer in our
own Church, Jackson. (Works, b. i. c. 31. sqe. on the Creed. )

Their public use in excluding from the ministry of

the first passage I contrasted Articles , which I pre-
ferred to those which I was condemning, with our
own.     " Until the Formula of Concord was adopted,

a certain latitude was still allowed by the symbo-
lical books, though not indeed equal to that per-
mitted by our own articles 2."                 In the German
church itself also I repeatedly contrasted the original
formulæ by which it was bound, with those which
were subsequently introduced .              Had the confession
of Augsburg alone, or other formulæ composed in


  1                                          2 Ib. p. 19.
    Enquiry, p. 144.
  ³ Ib. p. 18 , sqq. see especially note , p . 21 .


32


their spirit been employed, and the temperate and
discriminating adherents of Melanchthon maintained
their ground against the ultra-Lutherans, I antici-
pated a very different developement of the German
church.      The fatal step, however, appeared to me,
not the introduction of other writings of Melanchthon,
but that of the Formula of Concord , involving as it
did the sanctioning of the Articles of Smalcald .    It is
of that Formula, and of the narrowing effects which
it produced, of which I uniformly spoke in all the
subsequent passages.      The adherence which I con-
demned was that which clung not only to the letter
of Luther, but to every letter which even accidentally
escaped him in controversy, as long indeed as he did
not take larger views, which were ill suited to the
worshippers of his controversy.
  It can hardly be necessary to say in conclusion
that I am not fanatic enough to " think it wrong to
take any measures for the preservation of Chris-
tianity, because God alone can and will preserve it ¹."
I never excluded human agency.     I never expressed
any " indignation at the supposition that the Gos-
pel could not do all for itself " ."    I also did not
doubt, that " when mischief is afloat from whatever
causes," as far as individuals are concerned , " human
care" may (under God's blessing) check it ; human
carelessness may give it opportunity to work dreadful
evil ³ .   I never objected that it was " derogatory to
the independence and inherent power of the word
of God ," to imagine that it might receive tempo-
  1
      Letter, p. 51 .    2 Ib. p. 79.       3 Ib. p. 41 .
      Ib. p. 49. sqq .


33


rary injury from the neglect of precautions .        Chris-
tianity, as a privilege intrusted to man, may be of
course neglected or abused         by him ; nor is any
greater derogation from its power implied by the
existence of a theoretical unbelief of it, than by that
practical unbelief which we see every day in those
who, while they acknowledge its authority, live in
defiance of its precepts       and its principles.    As a
talent, it depends upon man to use it .        The only
supposition which I thought did derogate from its
power was, that it required not merely human means
to recommend, enforce, and illustrate it and the evi-
dence on which it rests, but also " the binding force
of human authority" to prevent our forsaking gene-
rally all those doctrines which constitute its essence ,
or even our religion itself.
  I may repeat, in order to obviate misconception,
that I also consider articles, absolutely necessary,

(as circumstances have long been) to the well- being
of a Church; that I regard a mere subscription to
Holy Scripture absolutely nugatory ; that, although
articles should be limited , as far as is practicable, to
essentials, the subscription must then not be left
vague and indeterminate, but ( in order to prevent, as
much as possible, dishonest evasion) must be abso-
lute and definite.   It is evident that every Church
has a right to require of its ministers, as long as they
profess to be ministers of that Church, to teach the
doctrines which she holds ; and that thereby no un-
due restraint, no hard yoke is placed upon them,
whenever the articles are good, and exceed not in
their definitions the just bounds .     There could even
be no question, (if it were necessary to choose, ) that
                           D


34


uniformity of doctrine in essentials, even with some
possible errors in points not fundamental (although
these, of course, should be corrected as soon as dis-
covered) is better for the religious interests of the
people at large, than an arbitrary and licentious ex-
position of Scripture truth. The only question which
I conceived to be between Mr. Rose and myself, was
one altogether abstracted from the practical prin-
ciples of Church polity.   I expressed my dissent

from what I conceived to be his opinion, because I
thought that by extending the uses and necessity of
articles too far,   or rather by producing      unreal
groundsfor that necessity, he did unintentional in-
jury to the cause of religion.    That articles are un-
der existing circumstances, and probably, now that
the Christian Church is so miserably divided, ever will
be necessary to the well- being of a Church, I am
as firmly persuaded as he is.     If I have dwelt more
upon the individual and personal benefits of articles,
it is because the general advantages appeared to me
too palpable to be questioned .    It has unfortunately
been too frequent a feeling, that articles , although
necessary to a Church, were rather individual evils :

assuming therefore their necessity, I have dwelt prin-
cipally upon the positive benefits which they confer
upon individuals also.

   More than enough has perhaps been said upon
this point ; but the charge recurs in so many shapes
and so many places, and articles are so constantly
exposed either to undue panegyric, founded on sup-
posed   uses which they were not      intended to an-
swer, or to disparagement, arising in want of expe-
rience, that I was anxious, if possible, to preclude
any further misconception of my meaning.


CHAPTER IV.


Human Systems - Sources of misconception- Traditionary Sys-
  tem not objected to- Advantages of unsystematic Form of
  Scripture- Consequent Necessity of System- Two Modes of
 forming it- Advantages of each- Dangers to be avoided-
  Difference of System of a Church and System in that Church
  -Instances of human Systems - Practical ill Effects of the
  Errors in human Systems .


THE third head, the supposed undue depreciation of
" human systems," is closely connected with the pre-
ceding, and the misconception appears to have been
derived from the same sources. What I have said

of the system which actually prevailed in Germany,
has been again understood as if spoken of " systems"
abstractedly, or of systems of faith in England ; and
I have again been blended with some class of oppo-
nents, of whose objects and opinions I am wholly
ignorant. To this I may add , that no distinction
has been made between the system prevailing in a
Church, and the system of that Church.
  So far, however, from joining with those who object
to systems formed from Scripture, whether from a
mistaken reverence for the mere word of Scripture,
or from want of sufficient learning, or from a hasty dis-
like to the attempt, occasioned by the errors to which
these systems are incidentally liable-so far from
this, I think, in common with most who have ever
given their minds to the subject, that such an under-
                            D 2


36


taking is absolutely necessary.    The circumstances
under which Christianity was given to the world ,—the
incapacity of those who first received    it, whether

during our Saviour's teaching or that of his Apostles,
to understand at first the whole compass of its truth ,
--the necessity of adapting the instruction to the
emergencies which occurred, of explaining, unfold-
ing, and enforcing its several parts, according to the
various misconceptions which arose in the different
minds to which it was proposed, would have been per-
haps sufficient reasons to prevent its being delivered
systematically.   A yet higher ground might be the
life and energy which are thus given to its teaching ;
in that the truths discovered to us, are not ab-
stractedly proposed as a sum of credenda (in which
case comparative coldness could perhaps hardly be
avoided, ) but come blended with the circumstances of
life, such as they were hereafter to influence.    The
doctrines or principles thus united with the feelings
and conduct and frame of mind which they were to
form , are naturally infinitely better calculated to act
upon the human heart, than any summary of doc-
trine , abstracted and disembodied as it were from
the vehicle in which they are conveyed.    Let any one

compare the influence of any doctrine, or of the
whole sum of doctrines, however admirably developed
from the books of Scripture, either in public formulæ
or by individual effort, with that which they produce
as they stand in Scripture, incorporated with the
minds of those through whom they were transmitted
to us, and he will be convinced that the original deli-
very of these truths in this      comparatively unor-


37


ganised form was the result of perfect wisdom , adapt-
ing itself to the nature of the beings to whom they
were imparted '.        Yet although the doctrines, when
understood, are much more influential in the form in
which they have been bestowed upon us, it is mani-
fest that in order to understand them fully, when the
authoritative teaching was withdrawn, it was neces-
sary that a comparison of the different statements
upon each doctrine should be made.                  Thus alone
could the partial and incorrect impressions be pre-
vented, which would arise from the insulated study
of the various passages, in which such statements oc-
curred, even though each should in itself be correctly
interpreted.      Now when this is done, a system is al-
ready commenced. It is again impossible that the se-
veral parts of Christian doctrine, centreing as they do
in one common object, and given for one end, should
not be better understood when viewed in relation to
each other, than when isolated ; and it is even ante-
cedently probable that the omission to survey them
in this relation, would prevent their being adequately
understood, or cause them to be positively miscon-
ceived ; as the withdrawing of one of two impelling
forces would alter the direction of the object.                So
far from there being any ground for not attempt-
ing to systematize religious truth, because it has been


  ' Qui vellent Christum Theologiam systematicam hominibus
tradidisse parum prudentes sunt æstimatores rerum . Ars vivendi
familiaribus colloquiis, datisque, prout res ferunt, documentis
longe felicius traditur, quam operoso illo definitionum et arguta-
rum quæstionum artificio, quod systema nuncupatur. - Turretin
Cogitat. de Relig. § 67.


38


delivered to us unsystematically, it may be that one
object of thus giving it was to excite our diligence
in the research after these truths, to influence the
heart by the occupation of the understanding , and
to increase our interest by the very effort of collect-
ing them .
  It being then not only a legitimate object , but
a bounden duty to       systematize   the   substance   of
revealed truth, two ways of effecting it would in
different periods obviously present themselves .    The
first perhaps would be to collect these several truths,
compare them with, and shew their bearing upon
each other, and correct, by views resulting from the
whole,   those   conceptions    which we    might   have
formed from the     separate    portions, or from lan-
guage occasionally adapted to human infirmity ; and,
this being done, to present the whole in the language
of Scripture. The other method, which does not con-
fine itself to the word of Scripture, would naturally
commence when the doctrines of Scripture had been
misunderstood or cavilled at, or the documents in
which they are contained , had been supposed to be at
variance with themselves.     The endeavour would

then be made to harmonise and order the whole by
means of what is not contained in that word, but is
derived from it by legitimate and necessary deduc-
tion.    Even to this further system no objection could
in itself be raised ;   truths which are thus derived

from revelation, are in fact virtually contained in it,
and form a part of it ; and as soon as we have satis-
fied ourselves of the legitimacy of the deduction, we
are as certain of them as of the truths from which


39


they are derived .   No one for instance can have any
right to object to the truth of the definitions introduced
into the first part of the Athanasian creed, in oppo-
sition to different human speculations, as soon as he is
convinced that these statements are legitimate infer-
ences from the appellation of God, applied in Scrip-
ture to the Son and the Holy Spirit.           This freer
mode of exhibiting Scriptural truth has also a fur-
ther advantage, in that it can present to us the ideas
of Scripture in the language of our own day, and thus
bring them nearer to us.      It must indeed be con-

fessed that both systems may produce incidental evil ;
evil, however, which is but incidental to an undertak-
ing, can of course be an argument only for extreme
caution in its execution, not for abandoning it.     It is
essential, indeed, that we should be alive, in both
these processes, to the dangers which lie close upon
their employment ; and to these dangers it was a
collateral object of the observations in my original
essay to direct attention. The principal risk, perhaps,
arising from that which confines itself to the word
of Scripture is, lest when a doctrinal word often
occurring in Scripture has had a definite sense given
to it, and has thus become (if one may use the ex-
pression without offence) a technical word, this mean-
ing, and this alone, should present itself to the mind
in the subsequent study of Scripture, and thus the
fuller and freer language of the sacred writers be
confined by a narrowing interpretation.           In the
other plan, there is, with more extensive usefulness,
more extensive danger also ; since it in a manner
re-translates Scripture, the meaning of the sacred


40


writers may receive incidental modifications ; or points
may be supposed to be legitimate and necessary con-
sequences, which do not of necessity follow : or tem-
porary views of Scripture doctrines may be substi-
tuted for the doctrines themselves.            The corollaries

to Scripture thus formed will naturally become more
numerous in controversy ; in which the positions ori-
ginally maintained or attacked are supported or op-
posed by means of such further deductions, as (e. g .)
the Lutheran mode of conceiving the presence of

Christ in the Sacrament, gave rise to the further
definitions of the relation of the two natures in
Christ '.     There will also be the obvious danger
that, under such circumstances, inferences will be
made, which have only a precarious connection with
that from which they are inferred .               In whatever
degree either of these classes of incidental defects
occur, more or less of a " human system" is intro-
duced into theology ; and since the adoption of the
second plan does not necessarily exclude the first,
there is nothing to prevent the errors of both being

united .   Both, I found, and I thought very exten-
sively, in the earlier theologians of Germany, espe-
cially in the 17th century ; the first producing an
interpretation of Scripture in unconscious subser-
viency to the system of Doctrinal         Theology,   the
other ending in an " over-refined human system, " or
as I elsewhere expressed it, " a system in itself partly
untenable, and the untenable points of which were
developed to their utmost extent by other deductions
and hypotheses¹."       It is also a natural evil of con-
troversy in such a state of things, that these deduc-
tions, which were originally introduced for the sake
of other points, should in time acquire an intrinsic
importance ; that the relative situation which they at
first occupied in the system should be forgotten ;
that from having been first valued as serviceable,
they should next be regarded as essential to other
doctrines, and at last, as of importance for their own
sakes ; and thus a " human system" be virtually
placed upon a level with Scripture.
  Those abuses, however, of a " human system"
are so entirely distinct from the legitimate object of
systematising the doctrines of Scripture, and that
object is so essential to the right understanding of
those doctrines , that I did not think it necessary in
my original enquiry to enter into any explanations ;
and the less, since I was speaking historically of a
given system and a specific period in Germany.
  It does not, however, follow that such a system ,
though prevailing at any period in a certain church,

                    1
                        Enquiry, p. 50.


42


can be properly called the system of that Church.
The system of the Church is of course contained in
its Articles   and other authentic documents ; and

until it has pronounced its approbation of any doc-
trines by receiving them into this code, its actual
character may indeed vary according to the tenets
of the majority of its members, but the Church it-
self is not responsible for them.     Such a supple-
mental system,     however, always probably exists ;
sometimes to a very great extent : and it may at
times be adopted by such a decided majority within
the Church, as virtually to become a portion of its
system, although no public opinion has been pro-
nounced upon it.      Such a secondary scheme is cer-
tainly not in itself necessarily objectionable , and may
often be requisite .     It existed undoubtedly to a

great extent at the time of the formation of our own
Articles ; although our compilers,    with their cha-

racteristic judgment, abstained from admitting it.
Some points again, which enter into such a system ,
may often be useful supplements of what was not
at the period of the original declarations sufficiently
expanded .     Thus, essential points, which are only
implied in the Apostles' Creed, (as having rather
the negative object of opposing existing errors, than
the positive one of declaring the Christian faith,)
were afterwards developed ; and our own Articles
have usefully systematised much which was before
passed over.
  In the German Church, as has been observed,
some latitude was at first allowed by the public
formula, with regard to this supplementary scheme
                              3


43


which even then existed in it, although it was
adopted by a considerable portion of its members,
and by these considered a part of the system ofthe
Church, and a criterion of orthodoxy.                After many
struggles, this was incorporated into the Formula of
Concord ; but the process was still continued, as well
in subsequent expansions and deductions from that
Formula, as in other points.               One instance of this
subsidiary system has been given by Mr. Rose, in
the " assuredly untenable hypothesis of an inspira-
tionextending to every word and thing in the
New Testament¹ ;" whereas the whole theory was
at first    comparatively       neglected .     In   like    man-
ner a large portion of the system of the Romish
Church was left           comparatively free,     until     it was
unfortunately incorporated           and    authorised by the
Council of Trent.          The same process took place in
former times, and in some degree exists at present in
our own Church. Thus in order to explain or defend
the fundamental doctrine of the atonement, where
the compilers of our Articles had wisely confined
themselves to the simple unexplained statement (that
Christ died to reconcile his Father to us, and to be
a sacrifice, not for original guilt only, but also for
the actual sins of men) there were added the scholas-
tic systems, that the punishment due to us could not
have been      remitted,     unless satisfaction had been
made to the justice of God ; that nothing but an
infinite satisfaction, made accordingly by an Infinite
Being,   could suffice .        So again,      as appendages

with others , as the " depository of the faith of highly-

always exactly chosen the minimum which would

certainly by some collateral definitions go beyond the
points sketched by Mr. Rose ' , and it was neces-

ceed even the principles which he lays down ; so
far, however, from             objecting to them upon this
ground, as a human system, I attempted to guard
against all possible misapprehension by mentioning
my value for them.
  I endeavoured further, to express by the language
which I employed, what sort of " human system "
it was which I condemned . In the passage quoted
by Mr. Rose            I spoke of the undervaluing Scripture
in behalf of an over-refined (not over-reformed)
human system ; in my first description of this human
system as established by the Formula of Concord,
I spoke of it as one in which " the letter of the
Lutheran tenets was enforced , mere scholastic opi-
nions were established as Articles of faith, human
technicalities , were substituted for the free spirit of
the Gospel, a logical formalism for scriptural expres-

sion of revealed truth '."           I spoke again of " nicer

and minuter distinctions which were adopted " ."                        I
spoke also , of " unscriptural appendages ; " and so
I believe throughout the essay.
  Few speculative errors are unattended with prac-
tical ill consequences also, and it is to these again, as
they existed in the German Church, that I alluded
in an expression which seems to have been misun-
derstood, when I condemned                   " the   reliance upon
the mere letter of a received system."                 It is indeed
a strange and melancholy phenomenon, that closely
blended as are the doctrines in Scripture with their
practical effect on the human heart, many should
be found who apparently separate them ; -who regard
sound religious belief, not as a talent and as a privi-
lege, but as a merit ; -as something which will stand
them in stead, in and for itself.              I do not mean that
they state this, totidem verbis, to themselves, but that
it seems to be their practical feeling ; and the very
doctrines, which were intended for their spiritual
advancement, are made the occasion of sloth and

inactivity.  This abuse of the objects of Christian
doctrine is observable indeed , in some measure , in
every age and country ; but the tendency to it, which
is deeply seated in our nature, receives an enormous
and painful increase, when points, which are either
appendages to Christianity, or occupy a subordinate
place in it, either as shades only of other doctrines,
or as deductions from them, are elevated into primary
importance,       and       constituted     tests    of right faith.

The few primary doctrines of Christianity are so
practical, that they are comparatively but rarely
associated with such feelings. Of such a state of
things, I thought that I saw much evidence, during
the period which preceded Rationalism ; and it was to
such a frame of mind only, ( a state which converts
the heavenly food destined for our spiritual nourish-
ment into a mere            source of disease) that           I   al-

part more reclaimable than a dead and contented
orthodoxism ¹."       The observation is, I think, borne
out by experience : it was called forth by one in-
stance, which I had occasion to mention, that the
miseries of the last desolating war had had a bene-
ficial effect upon Germany ; while those of the thirty
years' war had but increased the evils of that day 2 :

will, and were confident that they were guides of the

stant prayer, to a public amendment of life, we neither concern
ourselves about the divine help , nor do we abate the least in
luxury, feasts, and the whole circle of vices ; we send up cold
prayers to heaven, and pour forth warm blasphemies ; we make
our public service short, our drunken meetings long ; and yet we
boast that God will be with us and turn aside all evils ; nay we
still hope, amid the mire of our vices, that he will not only
approve our worse than barbarian life , but also protect and pre-
serve us, and be the guardian of so many ungodly cities." (In-
vitatio Fraternitatis Christi, p . 48 , ap. Hossbach Andrea u. sein
Zeitalter, S. 74, 5. ) These chastisements both in that they
seemed to come more directly from God, and were from their
nature unattended with the demoralising accompaniments of in-
testine war, might have been expected so much the more to have
brought back the sufferers to Him; and the evidence resulting
from their failure is accordingly so much the stronger.

blind, having the form of knowledge of the truth
in the law ;" of whom I thought when I observed

  Indeed the very context in which I used the ex-
pressions, as well as the language which I employed,
shewed that I regarded the frame of mind which I
was describing, as a state of imperfection , though still
of that earnest endeavour after truth which is always
ultimately rewarded .      I was      sketching   different
classes, who, though still standing short of a perfect
Scriptural faith, did yet form a part of the cheering
prospect of better days.   I spoke not of " scepticism ,'
as Mr. Rose has quoted me, but of a " sceptical
struggle after truth."     I thought not of the sullen
despondency, or recklessness of mind, which asked
hastily, " what is truth," and waited not to hear the


                  ¹ Letter, p . 59, note.


51


answer ; nor of the pride of human intellect, which
cares not, except at intervals, whether the things
of Christianity be true or no ; but of those better
spirits whose principal probation lies in the speculative
difficulties of religion, and who, though surrounded
with obstacles and dangers, of which we, who have not
had the same conflicts to endure, can have but a faint
idea, and in the midst of struggles, probably of in-
tenser pain than those of any of the other combatants
in our common warfare, are still holding on their way,
though obliged to win every step with anxiety and
doubt. Amidst our comparative tranquillity, and with
the predominantly practical character of our nation,
which generally saves us from trials peculiar to minds
of which high speculation is a characteristic fea-
ture, it will probably be difficult for many even to
conceive the frame of mind of which I have here at-

tempted to speak. And I cannot but think involuntary
injustice is often consequently done, not to the petty
and cold disposition , which thinks that doubts on re-
ligion are a mark of superiority of intellect, but to that
class whom our own invaluable Bishop Butler ¹ has de-
scribed ; men " who are without that shallowness of
temper, which renders many almost without feel-
ing as to any thing distant, or which is not the ob-
ject of their senses, (and consequently to religion)
and who from natural constitution of body and tem-
per, and from their external condition may have
small temptation to behave ill, small difficulty in be-
having well in the common course of life ; but who
yet need moral discipline and exercise in a higher

degree than they would have by the (to them) easy

may form any judgment at all, that these persons
may, in the " sceptical struggle after truth," have
made more progress, and are coming forth purer from
their probation than ourselves from that which has
been peculiarly allotted to us.   I own that I have seen
and known in Germany Rationalists of a lighter
shade, of whose progress , and of the acceptableness
of whose progress, I could not think slightly ; and
while I hoped and anticipated that they would free
themselves from their remaining errors, I could not
but feel humbled by the thought that notwithstand-
ing the privilege of a faith embraced without the
difficulties to which they were exposed,       I   might
myself have made less progress in my trials than

they in those peculiar to them.

On the momentous subject of inspiration I have first
to clear my own opinions from a very serious and
painful misconception ; and, secondly, to shew that
I did not touch unnecessarily upon the subject.
   In speaking of the system of scriptural inter-
pretation, which existed , in Germany, in the 17th
century (occasioned         principally by the Formula
Concordiæ, ) I observed that a faulty theory of inspi-
ration also contributed to its defects. This appear-

ed to me in three ways to have produced a forced
system of interpretation of the sacred volume : 1st,
By withdrawing the attention from the peculiarities
of the several sacred writers of the New Testament,
on the supposition, that as they were mere organs of
the Holy Spirit, to whom he dictated the same mes-
sage to mankind, each must necessarily have deli-
vered all the several parts of that message in exactly
the same way.        2nd , By confounding the teaching


55


of the old and new covenants, and requiring accord-
ingly, not agreement merely, but identity in both ;
not the germs only of Christian doctrine in the old,
but the whole ripened fruit.              Hence arose much
perverted application of prophecy, much forced inter-
pretation of the language of the Old Testament, in
support of main Christian doctrines . 3rd, By the as-


    Instances of these errors will occur to any one acquainted

et Socino." (so Hunnius in his Calvinus Judaizans, ap. Calov.
Bibl. Ill. ad loc . ) and again employs this passage in proof that
   P, in Proverbs viii . 7. denotes the eternal generation of the
Son, (Bibl. Ill. T. i. p . 1150, ) where there is much of the same
sort of argument. Again, Calov not only argues the same point

from Gen. xix . 24. but insists upon the phrase "            a cum
Domino,' as if it proved both the derivation of the Son from the
Father, and the identity of his essence : " quin filius et a Patre
et cum Patre operatur. Illud ob Hypostaseos originem, hoc ob
essentiæ identitatem ." So again, because Grotius rightly asserted
that the treble repetition of the word " Holy" in Is. vi. 3. was

Grotius Sp. Sancto, non vel infidelibus Judæis-sanctis Patribus,

66 prepare for the indiscriminate rejection of the doc-

   In order fairly to appreciate the character of the
question, it is necessary to ascertain to what points
it is confined . It was very natural, when the credi-

bility of the facts in Scripture had long not been
questioned, and it had not been necessary to look for
any historical confirmation of them, that the facts
should be believed on the ground of the inspiration
of the documents in which they were contained.
And so it formerly very often happened, that not
only those, who never felt the necessity of enquir-

ing into the grounds of their faith, or those who

mental facts which were recorded in it '.             In such

times any doubt about the inspiration of historical
facts would have been a startling innovation ; the
whole fabric, as then constructed , would have tottered
had a single stone , however small, been removed .
The distinct and independent basis upon which the
general credibility of the Scriptural facts rests, has
since been pointed out, during the contests with un-
believers, and it is now generally admitted that this
question must be settled before that on inspiration
can be commenced.      Still , however, since the two
characters of credible witnesses and inspired persons

are united in the Apostles, it is natural that the old
idea should imperceptibly recur ; and that there con-
sequently should be some latent apprehension, that
the admission of any error, however minute, would di-
minish the authority of the sacred writings , and there-
by open a door for the admission of more serious ob-

jections.   It is still virtually forgotten that these
facts have already been securely established on a basis
which cannot be shaken.      Since however it must

 divines, in this country also, ) inspiration was regarded
 only as a continued act of revelation , and accordingly
virtually confined to the object of revelation , " re-
 ligious truth ."     Such seems unquestionably from the
 combination of several passages written at different
 periods of his life, to have been the permanent opinion
 of the great parent of the Reformation, Luther ' .            It
