70


every thing contained in holy Scripture was to be
attributed to the especial           suggestion, inspiration,
and dictation of the Holy Spirit ; not those things
only of which the sacred writers could obtain no
knowledge by natural means, or those of which
though not in their own nature beyond their know-
ledge, they were actually ignorant, but those also
with which it was both in their power to become ac-
quainted, and which they actually did know, whether
by experience or the ministry of the senses."                This
suggestion, inspiration, and dictation was contrasted,
moreover, with " the mere divine infallible direction

and assistance."      The theory which supposed that the
Apostles in writing those things which they knew either
from experience or from the light of nature, were so
guided by the divine assistance and Spirit, “ ne quid-
quam scriberent quod non esset ex re, decoro, vero,
congruo," was rejected, as destroying the inspiration
of the Scriptures ' ; and human agency was so en-


continued by divines even of a different school, as Buddeus ,
Dogm. 1. i. c. 1. § 10. p . 92. Mosheim Dogm. c. 2. § 8. p. 120.
The distinction would indeed have been correct had the meaning
been that revelation was the original communication, inspiration
the continuance of the knowledge thus given.

since the whole seemed to depend upon this faulty
theological system.   It was a fancied idea of expe-
diency, in support of the main Protestant position
against the Romanists,      which gave rise to this
system among them.     Deeply had their descendants
to regret their short-sighted policy ; as indeed will
every one who is induced, however unconsciously, to
tamper with the truth, by regarding in the first in-
stance what is expedient.      These foreign additions
seemed to bind yet closer together the ancient fabric
round which they twined, and, perhaps, as long as
they remained undisturbed, they did so ; but when
removed, as in time they must be, it was found that
they had loosened the joints of the structure, which
they had appeared to decorate and unite.

  It may, however, tend more to exculpate me from
the charge of causeless innovation, if I can show
that the same principles have been held by eminent
theologians, in this as well as other countries. It
would lead too far now to speak of the opinions of
the Fathers ; though I am convinced from an examina-
tion of the anti-Nicene Fathers, as also from the
writings of Jerome, Chrysostom, Augustine, Gregory
of Nazianzum, and Euthymius, that they did not hold
the rigid theory of dictation often ascribed to them :
that while they stedfastly adhered to every thing es-
sential, they were far removed from the exaggerated
conceptions of the scholastic theologians . It would
also be too wide a field to speak of the general opi-
nions of our own earlier divines ; I will therefore
only extract a few passages from writers who will
not be suspected of any undue compromise with


75


ultra-liberal views ; some of whom appear to me to
have been actuated by the same motive, which in-
duced me to make the observations upon this subject,
the wish, namely, to prevent the temporary evil which
might arise from exaggerated statements of this im-
portant doctrine.

  I may be permitted ,        however, first to give at
length one quotation      from the celebrated De Do-
minis, because it meets well the popular objection
that the admission of such minute inaccuracies would

weaken the authority of Scripture in other points.
De Dominis himself shrinks apparently from avow-
ing an opinion then so unpopular, though it appears
to have been his own.    He introduces his observa-

tions, accordingly, as if " excusing ' Augustine and
other learned and catholic teachers, who suppose the
memory of the Evangelists to have sometimes failed

them, so that they inadvertently put one name for
another, as Jeremiah for Zechariah, or appear not to
agree about time, or other circumstances of the ac-
tions ; without admitting such error, however, in the
substance of the actions, or in the objects of revela-
tion.  Such errors, exterior to the substance of
the action, neither do nor can do any injury, nor
do they relate to any thing which is an object of
divine faith, but to that which admits only of a human
knowledge.    Nor does there seem to be any thing
contradictory (absurdum) in supposing human know-
ledge to be liable to such failure, even in the sacred
writers of the Holy Spirit, whenever it does not ex-


              De Rep. Eccl. L. vii. c. 1. § 22 .


76


tend to the substance of the fact, or the revelation
united with it.   For the Holy Spirit may leave his
writer, as a man, to that infirmity, which is the con-
dition of man, provided that the truth remains un-
shaken as to the substance of the facts related , and
the divine words, doctrines, and mysteries.     For in
these, Scripture ought to be unimpeached ; nor is it,
as to these points, affected by those slight inaccu-
racies in the circumstances of the relations. Certainly
the Evangelist John was not able to set down pre-
cisely the number of furlongs which the disciples
had rowed, where he says, " when they had rowed
25 or 30 furlongs :" the      Holy Spirit assuredly
knew ; and yet he left John , in this, to employ his
own human judgment, although it was uncertain .
It is, then, no valid inference, that if the Evan-
gelist could err in this name or in that circum-
stance, therefore he might in what related to mat-
ters of faith ; since we do not admit liability to
error, except where the question relates to natural
human knowledge, which does not necessarily involve
any knowledge of matters of faith.      It is of slight
moment what De Dominis's own opinion was : yet
most, probably, who know his character , will be in-
clined to think that he adopted , what he calls in the
following sentence, the " pia interpretatio," rather
to avoid giving offence than as his own opinion .
" I know," he adds , " that there are many passages

in Scripture, which seem to imply a mistake in the
writer, and yet that solutions of them are brought
by holy doctors, so as to clear the sacred writers from
all inaccuracy ; but I know also that violent, harsh,


77


and forced modes are employed for clearing them ,
which ought indeed to suffice for a pious mind, but
which do not satisfy the more rigorous demands of
the human understanding.     I indeed , for my part,
lay aside my rigor, and would rather embrace the
pious interpretation amidst difficulties, than admit
even those slight errors, relating to the circumstances
alone.      Yet I would say thus much in excuse of the
very holy and learned Augustine, and of other Ca-
tholic writers, who admit these errors, by which
no injury is done to the faith, rather than urge
those too harsh and violent interpretations , which
excite the ridicule and rouse the spleen of unbe-
lievers and worldly men, and consequently diminish
from the faith in holy Scripture, even in essentials
and in things revealed , in which there can be no
error ." The observation in this last sentence is

of importance, I think, for most ages, not only for
the sake of those who are perplexed by the difficulties
raised against revelation , but on account of the pain
often unnecessarily caused to believers themselves.

   1 The same opinions are well and concisely expressed by Hol-
den (Anal. Fid . 1. i . c . 5. ) though in other respects he appears to be
a rigid Roman Catholic ; and it appears from the passage of De

held by many eminent writers of that church ; it is well known
also, that they were those of Erasmus, although when censured for
it he withdrew under the shelter of the excuse that he was only
giving the opinions of Jerome . (See Simon Hist. Crit . du Nouveau

   It is, however, more to the purpose of an English
theologian, to show that these views have been held

by eminent divines of our own country.         Whatever
be their own opinions on the matter of fact, some of
these eminent writers have considered the admission of

the principle not to be dangerous , some actually bene-
ficial.   Thus Bishop Warburton , in his energetic style,
argues that " had the Scriptures been written under
this organic inspiration , there must have been the
most perfect agreement amongst the four Evan-
gelists, in every circumstance of the smallest fact.
But we see that there is not this perfect agreement.

In some minute particulars, which regard neither
faith nor manners, neither the truth nor certainty
of the history in general, the several writers vary
from one another.     A variation which, though it
discredits the notion of an organic inspiration, yet
(which is of much more importance) supports the
fidelity of the historian ; as it shows that they did
not write in concert, or copy from one another ; but
that each described the proper impressions which the
same fact had made upon himself ."       Again, in sum-
ming up this subject he says , " We see then the ad-
vantages resulting from a PARTIAL INSPIRATION , as here
contended for and explained .        It answers all the
ends of a Scripture universally and organically in-
spired, by producing an UNERRING RULE of faith and
manners ; and besides, obviates all those objections
to inspiration which arise from the too high notion


  1 Doctrine of Grace, B. i. c. 6.         2 Ibid. c . 8.

                               1


79


of it : such as trifling errors in circumstances of small
importance ; for the least error is inconsistent with
organic inspiration, but may well stand with a vir-
tual and co -operating influence ; such, again, as the
various readings in the several transcripts ; and the
various styles amongst the several authors of Scrip-
ture inconsistencies which would never have been

permitted, and contrarieties which could never have
happened, under universal inspiration ; but which
are the natural and harmless consequences of the
PARTIAL ."

   " In a word, by admitting no more than this lower
kind of inspiration, so warmly contended for by men
who were in hopes that the admission of it would
end in no inspiration at all, we secure and establish
the infallible word of Scripture ; and free it from all
those embarrassing circumstances which have been
so artfully and disingenuously thrown out to its dis-
credit."

  In like manner Archbishop Secker, although he
does not think it impossible to harmonise the discre-
pancies, nor that they would necessarily be irrecon-
cileable, if no pains now could reconcile them , still
thinks that the admission would not affect the doctrine
of inspiration, while it would have the collateral ad-
vantage mentioned by Bishop Warburton.                   " Though
the difficulties were irreconcileable," he says, " the con-
sequence would only be         that the divine superinten-
dency, under which the authors wrote, extended not
to such minute circumstances, though it did and


             ¹ Sermons, Vol . vi. Serm. iii . p . 51 .


80


must to doctrines and precepts and principal facts.
Not very much,       therefore, would be lost by this :
and one advantage would be gained ; that these di-
versities would still     more evidently shew,         (what,
indeed, the whole air and manner of their writings
shews) that the Evangelists are absolutely free from
all imputation of concerting their story together, to
deceive mankind ; the assurance of which makes
good amends in respect of usefulness, for any per-
plexities we may find in adjusting their account."
  Dean Lowth, again, in his vindication of the inspi-
ration of Scripture against the views of Le Clerc,
clears his way by first classifying the cases " wherein
the Apostles may be supposed not to have acted or
spoken by inspiration ." As the first of these is named,
" When they treat of the common occurrences of
life, and such things as have no relation at all to
divine truths ," and to this class are referred " slips
of memory in matters of no consequence , some of
which many learned men suppose may be found in
the New Testament '."

  Archbishop Tillotson again limits inspiration to
" whatever is necessary to inform the world cer-
tainly of the mind and will of God .             If a person
should conceive that absolute           dictation of every
word and letter was necessary, then he hath reason
to hold this ; if on the contrary he should think
that Moses could write the actions which he himself

did or was present at ; or the Evangelists might


   1 Lowth's Vindication of the Divine Authority and Inspiration
of the Old and New Testament, c . ii . p . 40 , 41 .

write what they heard or saw, or of what they had
good assurance from others, as St. Luke tells us he
did, or St. Paul could write for the cloak and parch-
ments, then he seems to have reason on his side ."

He seems also to think it probable, that the words
of the discourses of our Saviour were not dictated

to the Evangelists ; on the ground that they must
then have agreed in words and form, as they do in
the substance.      He concludes, that it seems sufficient
to assert that the Spirit of God revealed what was
necessary to be revealed ; and as to other points did
                                                    .
superintend them in the writing of it, so as to secure
them from any material error or mistake ¹.
  The arguments of the able Archdeacon Powell , and
even his language, very much resembles that which I
have employed ; and it is for expressing an opinion,
which agrees with the last sentence, that I have been
blamed " for treating so grave a matter so lightly,"
and supposed to " consider the inspiration of the
Gospels a vulgar error ." Dr. Powell observes , that
" at the time of the descent of the Holy Spirit, the
Apostles were to receive their full instruction .
They were to receive it immediately from heaven,
and, having received, they committed it to writing,
for the benefit of Christians in all ages. And this,
I conceive, is what we are to understand when it is

said, that the Holy Scriptures are of divine autho-

He who acquires knowledge, not by the use of any
natural faculty, neither by immediate perception,
nor by reasoning, nor by instruction, but in some
inexplicable miraculous manner, is inspired.                   He,
who sets down in writing the knowledge so obtained,
composes an inspired work '.            There appears to be
no intelligible distinction between original revelation
and inspiration. And yet men seem to have enter-
tained an obscure notion of something more, other-
wise they could not have been perplexed with so
many difficulties concerning the accuracy and per-

fection of the Scriptures . They contain some few
passages which appear to have no relation to reli-

gion, and many facts, which the writers certainly
knew in the ordinary way.  Nor does there seem

any reason to expect marks of the interposition of
heaven in such matters .        The great truths impressed
upon their minds neither obliterated their former
knowledge, nor made it perfect.           When they speak,
for instance, of a Roman custom or a Jewish tradi-
tion, we are not to imagine that these things were
revealed from above, nor to require greater accuracy
in their accounts of them, than in other writers ,
who treat of the affairs of their own age, and their
own country.      When they relate the wonderful events
which they had seen and heard, it will be no objec-
tion to their credit as human witnesses, that we find
in their several histories of the same fact, such a

variety of circumstances or of method, as always occur
in others the most exact narrations.     Difficulties of

this kind could never have arisen, or must have been
easily removed , had either the impugners or defenders
of the sacred writings formed precise ideas of the na-
ture of inspiration, and attended to its use. This was

not to teach men history or philosophy, not to in-
struct them in the art of composition, or the orna-
ments of human learning ; but to make them un-
derstand and believe the religion of Jesus."
  I can scarcely be censured , I think, for having stated
in a book which, from its very nature, could not have an
extensive circulation , the same which Dr. Powell did
not think wrong to deliver as religious instruction to
an audience of young men above half a century ago ;
and which has subsequently been published by Dr.
Balguy.       I should perhaps add, that had I been
earlier acquainted with this book , I should have re-
ferred to it.      I may be allowed, as the subject is
collaterally of great importance, to adduce two more
authorities of very distinguished divines, who are
still living ornaments of our Church, and who have
both expressed principles, with which those for
which I have been censured coincide, whatever may

be their opinions as to the matter of fact in the ap-
plication of these principles . " Most of the con-
tradictions brought forward to serve the purposes
of infidelity," says Bishop Van Mildert ' , " relate
to minute chronological difficulties, and the unim-
portant circumstances of historical narration,         with

which it little concerns us to be more accurately

acquainted, and which in no respect affect the credi-
bility of the general history.      Few, however, if any
of these, are found to be incapable of some consis-
tent and satisfactory solution.       But supposing them
not to admit of this, we should not be warranted in
thence impugning the divine authority of Holy Writ ;
unless it could be shewn that the Almighty, in im-
parting the gift of inspiration for certain          special
purposes, became thereby pledged to bestow it like-
wise for purposes of an inferior kind, and wholly
unnecessary to the great end in view." And again ' ,
having mentioned the objection of the unbeliever to
inspiration, that " many instances of inaccuracy, ob-
scurity, and inconsistency may be pointed out in the
books of the Old and New Testament, whereas on

the supposition of plenary inspiration, they must be
absolutely free from all imperfection of whatever kind,”
he proceeds, " This objection seems to suppose (what
the advocates for the plenary inspiration of Scrip-
ture, are by no means obliged to admit) that the
persons thus inspired must necessarily have been ren-
dered in all respects infallible.       But we only main-
tain that they were        secured     against important
error ; not that in matters of less moment and un-
connected with the purpose of their Divine com-
mission, they were exempt from trivial errors, inci-
dental to human imperfection."
  The other passage which I would venture to quote
from a living writer, occurs in a pastoral work of
Bishop Blomfield , but from which much may be
         1
         ¹ Boyle Lectures , Sermon xxiii . p. 403.


85


learnt by the theologian .           The    question, indeed,
which led him to make the observations is different,
namely, the       disagreement between some              of the
Apostles themselves,         respecting the lawfulness of
eating with the Gentiles ; but the principles which he
there lays down are the same ; his view of inspiration
is certainly, as far as it is expressed, the same at
which I myself arrived .        " This passage ' , (he says)
is greatly relied on by the Socinians, as furnishing an
insuperable objection to the notion of apostolical inspi-
ration .    But the difficulty vanishes if we consider the
true and proper objects of spiritual illumination, which
are, the doctrinal and moral features of revelation,
and not unessential points of outward discipline and
economy : a distinction , which corresponds with the
importance attached to either branch of religion by
Him who was the author of it, and which should be
carefully kept in mind by all who are called upon to
preach it ; that they may not lay a greater stress
upon the circumstance than upon the                 essence of
Christianity.      As the Scriptures do not require, so
neither do they authorise us to believe, that the
Apostles were so inspired as to be wholly free
from error, in things not material to the effectual
execution of their mission.          But that they received,
by the immediate communications of the Spirit of
Truth, a more perfect knowledge of the plan and
doctrines of the Gospel, which they so little com-
prehended during their Master's life ; that they
were   enabled     to develope       the mysteries of re-

           1 Lectures on the Acts, Lect. v. p. 88-90 .


86


demption , with all the      certainty of divine autho-
rity ; that they were guarded from error            in all
points which could affect the purity and perfect-
ness of the faith delivered to the saints, or the
integrity and stability of the Church ; this kind and
degree of inspiration we claim for the first preachers
of the Gospel, and the proofs of it lie on the sur-
face of the sacred volume , which records their labours
in the cause of Christ ."
  I must repeat in conclusion, that I do not wish to
imply that these authors take the same view as my-
self of the particular subordinate matters of fact, in
which, after long examination, I have not been able
to adopt any of the solutions usually given ; nor is it
the actual decision upon these matters of fact, but
the principles upon which the enquiry is conducted ,
which I regard as of importance.
  I trust that this explanation of my objects and
principles,   though not so full as I should have
wished to give, had circumstances allowed, will ob-
viate further misconception .      I did indeed think it
more expedient, when writing my Enquiry, to touch
but lightly on the subject ; and a longer discussion
would   have    occupied    a    disproportionate   space.
Yet I should have entered more at large upon the
question even there, had I thought it possible that
my remarks could have pained any well-intentioned
Christian.    The few observations which I did make,
arose from the anxious conviction that it was for the

good of the Church that the subject           should be
touched upon ; and that the evils which a theory,
however limited its extent, or practical application,


87


had caused in another country, should be held up as
a warning to this.
   In this country, also, I own that I think much
depends, for individuals at least, on the turn which
the opinions on inspiration take ; the old theory ' has
here also      already shewn a tendency to produce
among laymen one precisely opposite ; one which falls
far below, as the former far exceeded, what may be
collected from Scripture.           I trust that the question
will be examined, with all the feeling of responsi-
bility   indeed , which       ought to accompany every
syllable which is spoken or written upon a subject
so important ; but that difficult as the task is, no
views of expediency will be allowed even an imper-
ceptible influence ; and I shall not then dread, either
that my views will give pain to any one, or that the
doctrine of inspiration will be exposed to temporary

disadvantages from the manner in which it is proposed .

    " The charge of contradictions ," says even an esteemed
Scotch writer on Inspiration, " relates solely to certain detached
passages, and to some matters of inferior importance, by which
the substance or essence of revelation is not affected . But con-
tradictions in smaller matters would be equally conclusive as
contradictions in greater, against the inspiration of the Scrip-
tures," &c. (Dick on Inspiration, p. 294,) and again (p. 307), " a
contradiction which could not be imputed to the blunder of a
transcriber, but was fairly chargeable to the sacred writers them-
selves, would completely disprove their inspiration . "


CHAPTER VI.


New era in Theology- what intended by it with regard to doc-
  trinal Theology, or to the Theological sciences generally, and
  by the phrase " blending ofbeliefwith science" -imputed ad-

THE heavy charge of ' liberalism ' upon theological sub-
jects, a word, which has rarely any other meaning than
a readiness to part with what we no longer value, was
founded in some degree, I suppose, upon the total
misconception of my opinions on the momentous
subject of inspiration ; in part upon a mistaken idea
of what I intended by a few phrases, such as a " new
era in theology."      The former part of this charge I
trust that I have already removed ; and if these lat-
ter phrases, as I have employed them, be indeed
calculated to raise indefinite expectations " without
any corresponding reality ," I do not regret that
I am obliged to give a public explanation of my
meaning, although I cannot but think that it was
sufficiently clear from the context .
  I have, it seems, inadvertently used the expression
of a " new era" three times in my short essay
                                            essay : of

uniting philosophic depth with pure Christian faith,
and that it promised, if continued, to form a new

and comprehensive views, and that it will form a new

  It has been unfortunate for my opinions, that Mr.

              1


91


and absurd ; or to non-essentials, in which case my
language would be inflated, misleading, and dis-
appointing. Even on this narrower field my state-
ments can, I think, have no such effect.   I ex-
pect not, any more than Mr. Rose, discoveries in the
essentials of Christianity ; I suppose not any more
than he that " we have been allowed for eighteen
centuries to deceive ourselves ' ;" but though the
essentials of Christianity have been the same from
the beginning, it does not follow that both their re-
ception, and their efficacy when received, may not
have been (in whatever degree ) impeded by other
human additions, which have gathered around them.
The Romish Church holds the essential doctrines of

Christianity , but the addition of a corrupt human
system has a grievous tendency to oppress them and
impede their influence : and even in other cases,
when a Church, as the German, had freed itself from

its heavy yoke of human traditions, I see no reason
a priori, why portions of these corruptions, which
were originally introduced by human nature, should
not again, in some other form, have crept in.     God
forbid, that any of these, or other corruptions, should
be described as such as would necessarily annihilate
the essential influence of Christianity wherever they
extend   though these clouds have indeed dimmed
                                              .
the beauty of the luminary around which they ga-

human love, such as can love what is opposite to its
own nature, ) or whether justice and mercy be pour-

flicting with each other, the one of which could not
be exerted before the other was satisfied .   I must

again protest against being supposed to judge of
those who hold these or any other doctrines : very
many undoubtedly who hold doctrines, which have
an apparent tendency to create ideas of God cal-
culated to interfere with the love of Him, may indeed
love Him yet more sincerely than we ; they may
find in what to others is       repulsive,   a food for

throughout only of the natural tendency of such doc-


94


trines, and I fear that I am borne out in saying, not
only that such sentiments have been the parents of
much Socinianism, but that to many a mind, which
has been from infancy trained up in these doctrines,
they have been a hindrance in its whole career ; —
that its efforts to grow in the love of God have been
impeded by the early predominance given to the
fear of Him.   Thus what is set forth in Scripture as
the great instance of God's love to man, has made
Him to them an object of terror ; and while they hold
the doctrine itself, instead of dwelling upon it as the
means of spiritual improvement, they think of it
only as one which it is necessary to hold.   This is in-
deed one instance only out of many ; but I may be
spared the painful task of dwelling further upon
them ; or to instance only one more, it is obvious
that the exaggerated statements which we often wit-
ness of the degree of the inherent disposition of evil,
have a tendency in general to diminish the sense of
our own personal guilt, rather than to increase our
humility.   Whatever be the degree of our natural
corruption, those who have narrowly observed them-
selves have found, that the infinitely larger portion
of our actual corruption has been owing to          our
own deficiency in employing the means of restoring
our nature offered to us . A doubt of the actual

corruption of our nature       can   only imply entire
ignorance of ourselves ; as a doubt of the natural
preponderance of our inclination to evil, does, of
human nature ; but an undue estimate of the propor-
tions of both, ends generally in virtually shifting the
guilt and shame from ourselves upon the first who fell.

  I have here dwelt upon doctrines connected with
the very key-stone of Christianity, and which, from
their very nature, were less liable to be perverted .
I have also spoken of them as they have been, and
are in part held in this country ; if any one will ac-
quaint himself with the earlier systems of doctrinal
theology in Germany, or read the accounts of them
in Pfaff, I shall no longer be supposed to have been
idly speculating on some unsubstantial vision , or
encouraging any day-dreams in those of my own
age, in what I said of an " era in the doctrinal theo-

logy of Germany."    I thought not of the discovery
of any new truths, but of that only which is the pro-
vince of doctrinal theology, the illustration and vin-
dication of all the doctrines of Scripture relating to
God and man, as well in themselves as in reference
to each other, to the general system , and to our own
nature.
   With respect to Schleiermacher's work, I had
perhaps less reason to dread being misapprehended
from the very extent of what I said : I spoke of the
whole range of theological sciences.    Very much as
has been done, very much , I think, in every branch of
theology , remains to be done : of doctrinal theology
I have already spoken : one need , I think, but name
the several theological sciences, and compare them
with the idea which any who has thought of those sub-
jects has formed to himself, to see that there is very
much to be done , in Christian evidence , both as a
whole and in its several parts ; -in the vindication of
Christian doctrine from the various aberrations from

it, (for the evils of controversy belong more to indi-


96


vidual controversy,       or a certain description of it,

to me      great faults" in the sketch of Schleier-

  It would have been well, however, if Mr. Rose,
before he censured me thus severely for the vague-

ness of the expectations which I was supposed to
hold out, had recollected the language of Bishop
Butler in his comparison of discoveries in religion
with those in natural or civil knowledge.           “ Prac-
tical Christianity (says this judicious and immortal
writer) , or that faith and behaviour which renders a
man a Christian, is a plain and obvious thing : like
the common rules of conduct with respect to our
ordinary temporal affairs.      The more distinct and
particular knowledge of those things, the study of


97


which the Apostle calls going on to perfection, and
of the prophetic parts of Revelation, like many parts
of natural and even civil knowledge, may require
very exact thought and careful consideration .   The
hindrances too, of natural and of supernatural light
and knowledge, have been of the same kind.       And

as, it is owned, the whole scheme of Scripture is not
yet understood, so if it ever comes to be understood ,
before the restitution of all things, and without mi-
raculous interposition, it must be in the same way as
natural knowledge is come at ; by the continuance
and progress of learning and of liberty, and by par-
ticular persons attending to, comparing, and pursu-
ing, intimations scattered up and down it, which are
overlooked and disregarded by the generality of the
world.     For this is the way in which all improve-
ments are made ; by thoughtful men's tracing on ob-
scure hints, as it were, dropped in by nature acci-
dentally, or which seem to come into our minds by
chance .   Nor is it at all incredible, that a book
which has been so long in the possession of mankind ,
should contain many truths as yet undiscovered. For
all the same phenomena, and the same faculty of in-
vestigation, from which such great discoveries in na-
tural knowledge have been made in the present and
last age, were equally in the possession of mankind
several thousand years before '."

what I had said of the especial untenableness of

forces us to consider much which we had before

assumed as undoubted ; it compels us often to give

many a further view often opens itself, to which,
without this stimulus , we might never have arrived.
The same which happens to individuals, takes place
also, occasionally, even during the conflict of parties ;
and this the more frequently in proportion to the
importance of the subjects upon which they are en-
gaged.      There had been then nothing suprising had

THERE now remains but one misconception more to
remove, as to the opinions upon theological subjects,
incidentally expressed in my essay.         It has arisen in
what I said, in a note, of Bretschneider's collection
of the difficulties relating to the genuineness of St.
John's Gospel ' .    Bretschneider stated in his first
work,       and has again repeated,       when    expressing
publicly his present conviction of the genuineness of
St. John's Gospel , that his object in publishing those
doubts, was to bring the question to a satisfactory
result. " Summæ theologo Evangelico debet esse

curæ, ut quinam sint libri vere Apostolici, qui vero
non, accurate quærat, et dubitationes ipsi obortas
vel removeat ipse vel, sin minus, easdem viris ipso
                                     2
doctioribus ad dijudicandum proponat "."

  The sincerity of this statement            I did not feel

        1
            Enquiry, p . 155, note.       2 Præf. p. iv.


103


 myself justified in questioning, especially since he
 did not subsequently hesitate to avow his satisfaction
 that others had been able to arrive , on solid grounds,
 at that result which he had been unable to attain.
   The work of Bretschneider, as is well known, was
addressed to scholars, and was written in a language
which rendered it accessible only to them : at the
time that he wrote it, doubts upon the genuineness
of St. John's Gospel were, as I understood in Ger-
many, generally prevalent : four treatises, which had
excited attention, though apparently of little ability,
had been already published against the genuineness
of that Gospel.       No one then probably, who became
acquainted with the work of Bretschneider, would
have either heard or felt doubts upon this subject
for the first time.     The case then, and the only case

of which I spoke, is where doubts have acquired a
general and public prevalence ; the only object pro-
posed was the producing a legitimate conviction which
could not again be shaken. Consequently the very hy-
pothesis excluded the circulation of doubts among
those who could not judge of the merits of the case,
or who would be likely to suffer by it. Where the ob-
ject is to set the question at rest, by establishing the
truth on a solid basis, I cannot but think that next to
a decisive answer to prevailing doubts , a strong con-
centration of them is likely to be most conducive to
this object. The instance of Bretschneider, I thought,
illustrated this point : before his work appeared,
there had been both attacks on the genuineness of
St. John's Gospel, and defences of it ; and the minds
of the learned still remained unsatisfied ; his book


104


brought the question to an issue, and it is now at
rest probably for ever.  No terms of condemnation

are too strong for that man who would wantonly
shake the faith of such as have not the means of ap-

preciating the strength of the objections proposed to
them but of such an one there was here no ques-

tion.   The only case supposed, I must repeat, was
when the evil was already within the system ; the
only question, whether it might not be the part of
an honest physician, if he could not remove it by
gentle methods, instead of tampering with the dis-
ease, to produce such a crisis, as would by its decisive
character cast it out. It is too often the case in
every part of theology, that until the mind has been

compelled by opposition to weigh accurately the ar-
guments which it uses, it contents itself with, or at
least enlists in its cause, such as are insufficient ; or
disguises the force of such as are good, by proposing
them inaccurately, or without sufficient limitation.   It
is not the fault of these theologians, ( as neither is
this habit confined at all to theology,) but of hu-
man nature.   Whenever we are persuaded of a fact,

be the grounds of our conviction what they may, we
can with difficulty transport ourselves into the frame
of mind of one who doubts of that fact, and view
our own arguments in the light in which they would
appear to an opponent.     Hence the weight often laid
upon arguments which are arguments only to one
already convinced, but which would have been no
arguments at all, unless the mind had been already
actually persuaded of the result .   Such arguments, I
cannot but think, involve much more than them-


105


selves : the mounds raised around the building are
thought by both parties mainly to contribute to
its support ; and when they are swept away, some
time elapses before the cloud of dust, which ac-
companies their fall, disappears, and it is disco-
vered that nothing was lost but themselves.           It is
what one should expect before-hand,              and expe-
rience daily confirms it, that more doubt as to the
truth of Christianity itself, and of its fundamental
doctrines, is produced by the weak arguments of
the supporters of each, than by the strongest em-
ployed by their opponents. To express my meaning
in the words of Burke : " Difficulty is a severe in-
structor, set over us by the supreme ordinance of a
parental guardian and legislator , who knows us better
than we know ourselves, as he loves us better too.
Pater ipse colendi haud facilem esse viam voluit.
He that wrestles with us, strengthens our nerves and
sharpens our skill.  Our antagonist is our helper.
This amicable conflict with difficulty obliges us to an
intimate acquaintance with our object, and compels
us to consider it in all its relations. It will not suffer
us to be superficial . "    These views have furnished
me with comfort, amidst many appearances in the
world, which would otherwise have caused unmingled
pain : if, however, any one had been convinced that
after all there was something erroneous and dan-
gerous in these views , that danger could only be
augmented by extending them to circumstances to
which I never applied them, while those to which I


           ¹ Reflections on the French Revolution .


106


did refer, are passed by unnoticed .     Yet no one
would argue, that because, in a trying case, a phy-
sician might admit the benefits produced by a des-
perate remedy, he would recommend the same course
to be pursued when the frame was yet sound, on the
ground that it might fortify it against possible at-
tack ; or that although he might think the use even
of a poison had produced salutary effects in the one
case, he would not deem its employment in the other
an unjustifiable tempting of God's providence.
   The whole question , indeed, with respect to this
country, is altogether an abstract one : nor (blessed
be God) is there any prospect that it should ever
be otherwise.   Without depreciating the invaluable
external evidence,   which has been collected, the
weight of internal proof will probably, in a nation of
such practical solidity as our own, on the whole more
than counterbalance any external difficulties which
may be raised .  In an age of practical piety the
internal evidence of St. John's Gospel, which ap-
proves itself to the heart, would have effaced any
doubts, which might be produced to perplex the un-
derstanding.    The contest has also, happily for us,
been carried on in another land : the questions, which
have there been agitated, as far as they relate to the
canonical authority of any book of Scripture, have
uniformly terminated in producing additional evi-
dence for that authority : the body of proof, both
external and internal, has also been purified, en-
larged, and strengthened.   External evidence has
indeed its limits,   although undoubtedly not yet
reached   the mine of internal proof is probably in-
                                1


107


exhaustible , and will continue to yield greater trea-
sures in proportion to the powers of mind, and
energy,     and diligence,      employed     to   explore     its
depths.   It is then highly improbable that our
country should ever fall into a state of such disease
as would make the concentration of doubts upon
books of Scripture, other than in the highest degree
unjustifiable, wanton, and sacrilegious .
  The question being thus abstract, I should un-
doubtedly have abstained from any observations upon
the subject, had I thought that my language would
have been misunderstood , or that what was limited
to a very peculiar state of things, would have been
applied generally.         I have been obliged to recur to
the subject, because I have been represented to a
Christian public, as not merely thinking that such
conduct might in a specific case have been honest,
but as maintaining the monstrous supposition ' , that
" the scattering doubts as to the truth of religion,
or the genuineness of Scripture is not an evil , merely
because it may call forth a reply."
  There is one more question connected with this
subject, which I must briefly explain.            After classi-
fying the different aberrations from genuine Chris-
tianity in the German church, I mentioned, as a
ground for not noticing the doubts which had pre-
vailed in Germany on several books of Scriptures,
" that these furnish but rarely, or at most only inci-
dentally, any indication of the dispositions of the
enquirers 2 ;" and I had designated it as " a suppo-

    1                                  2
        Letter, p. 158 .                   Enquiry, p. 154.


108


sition pregnant with mischief that any doubt on an
individual portion of the sacred volume necessarily
implied a diminished value for its whole contents."
The observation was made in consequence of a

disposition, which I have seen in this country, to re-
gard with suspicion and distrust any one who felt him-
self compelled to hesitate with regard to the genuine-
ness of any book or portion of a book of Scripture,
(even though it should be one of those, about which
the ancient church was divided ) ; -to impute wrong
motives to those who do so ; and to think it a merit
rather than a blessing, to be free from doubts.     I
sincerely trust that nothing I have said or can say
can   have the   remotest    tendency to diminish the
sense of responsibility with which such a subject
ought to be approached : yet I cannot but think
that a feeling as to these enquiries, very general in
this country, (a feeling founded indeed on the reve-
rence for Scripture, and therefore which must com-
mand respect,) is still calculated to augment the
danger which it dreads.     Whatever the defenders of
the genuineness of any portion of Scripture repre-
sent to be staked upon it, will generally be supposed
to be involved in it, by those also who doubt of that
genuineness ;    and   much more will appear   to be
risked upon the issue than really is.    It has been
already stated that the investigations in Germany
have served only to establish more firmly the canoni-
cal authority and authenticity of the books which
have been questioned : that the waves, which beat
against them, have only added to the height and
strength of the barrier, which opposed their swell-


109


ing. Were we then to become acquainted at once
with all which has been written or delivered in that

country on both sides , wearisome and unprofitable
as the task would to most readers be, I am fully per-
suaded that the result would be perfectly satisfac-
tory. The increasing acquaintance, however, with
German literature, among persons of all descriptions in

this country , some examples of which we have already
had, and, much more, the formation of a school of
blind admirers of the Rationalizing German critics
in one of the universities of America, lay us open at

any moment to attacks, which would necessarily have
some circulation before the means of defence could
be prepared.    We may hope indeed that these at-
tacks will be anticipated, and their edge blunted :
still, while we array ourselves to meet or to prevent
them, it is of importance, that in case they or others
of a similar character do come upon us, we should
not exaggerate their importance, or by our own
fears, magnify their strength.   There would always be
many who, without the power of enquiring for them-
selves, would hear vague reports, that such or such a
book of Scripture has been impugned, and accord-
ingly as their wishes lead them, they would either be
unnecessarily disquieted , or would unduly triumph.
Others, who are unstably " halting between the two
opinions," would have their difficulties increased . The
practical persuasion then which I wished to see es-
tablished was this : that whatever difficulties might
be started against individual books of Holy Scrip-
ture (and they have but rarely been started against


110


those which are most important to our faith, and have
been amply answered ,) there is yet nothing in this
to shake a Christian's faith : that he should prize and
be thankful to God for every portion of the instruc-
tion which has been given him : but should it unhap-
pily be questioned , whether indeed any portion of
that treasure did come from God , he should not be
disquieted ; but viewing the solidity of the founda-
tions, upon which the main pillars of our faith rest,
await the issue with humble and undisturbed confi-
dence.   The habit of drawing further consequences
from any view of an opponent recurs in so many
various forms, (and from being so very natural , it is
so much the more likely to be pernicious, ) that it
seems to me an important habit to separate as much
as possible each position from consequences which
are not necessary to it.   Not assuredly with the idea
of familiarizing ourselves with the loss of the point
which is questioned, but to prevent our attaching an
unreal and adscititious importance to it, and repre-

senting more as periled on the contest than actually
depends upon it.    There can of course be no com-
parison between this timid frame of mind, (although
often probably united with weak faith, ) and the
slightest diminution of the anxious carefulness and
reverence with which such subjects ( whether the re-
sults be communicated to others or no, ) ought to be
examined .   Yet while I earnestly deprecate such a
change, and myself deem the present state of things,
notwithstanding the occasional harshness of judg-
ment and the peril which attends it, preferable to


111


this diminution, I know not why such an alternative
should be proposed .     There     are     surely sufficient
motives in the very idea of the distress, which cause-
less innovation may produce in the minds of well-
intentioned Christians, there is enough in the hal-
lowed theme itself, to induce us to hope that the
parting with these unreal motives for caution will
produce no ill effect.   But at all events, as soon as
we are convinced that they are unreal , such separa-
tion becomes a duty. No one will accuse the vene-
rable Reinhard of indifference or want of considera-
tion for others ; yet he says , " Of the importance
of this whole subject one must thus judge .        It is be-
yond all question that religion itself is not over-
thrown, when the one or other book of the canon is
exposed to doubt.    For all the doctrines of religion
may be proved out of those, whose genuineness was
never contested ; and we see also from the case of the
early Church, that religion itself suffers nothing from
such difference of opinions.     Since, moreover, there
are no precise definitions laid down upon this subject
in our symbolical books, one can certainly not deny
a minister of our Church the liberty of departing from
the usual opinion on this subject. On the other hand
it is certain that every one is bound , upon so impor-
tant a question, to proceed with the greatest con-
scientiousness , not to force his private opinion upon

others, to use no contemptuous expressions with re-
gard to such writings, as he feels himself compelled

                 1
                 ¹ Dogmatik, S. 70, 71 .


112


to doubt of; such expressions being always a source
of pain to the rest of his Christian brethren who ad-
here to the usual opinion.          Since, moreover, we are
in danger of rejecting a portion of the instruction
which God has imparted to us, when we venture to
declare one of these books to be spurious, every one
will feel of his own accord how guardedly and cau-
tiously he must proceed in this matter."
   Yet, though Mr. Rose differs widely from me as to
the fact of the general object of these enquiries in
Germany, there is no question between us here in
principles.    I have happily only had to clear my
own views.      66 Abstractedly, it is true no doubt,"

he says, " that the man who rejected three gospels

out of four, and yet who firmly believed the fourth,
might be as good a Christian as they who received
them all¹."     It is in the application alone that he
censures what I said.        I had stated my opinion that
"the enquiries in Germany, though occasionally carried
on upon wrong principles, seem generally to have
had truth for their object 2."         Mr. Rose says , " this
is idle.   We do not perhaps above once in a century
deal with one of so diabolical a temper, as to have

falsehood for his object :           but the effect of idly
starting doubts, of exerting ingenuity in maintain-
ing them, and in suggesting them to others to whom
they would never have occurred , is as evil as if there

certainly is as evil : nor would I wish undoubtedly

66
   having falsehood for its object." How difficult it is
to have truth solely for the object, excluding in the
formation of each judgment every bias, every sup-
posed consequence, every favorite view, every idea of
general expediency, to blink nothing, omit nothing,
be contented with no grounds, though plausible even
to ourselves, as long as there is the slightest suspi-
cion of their untenableness , to force ourselves to
dwell on all the points which oppose our views, and
to survey them in all their bearings, with the same
rigid and minute attention as those to which we have
become most inclined ,-how difficult for man this is,
they probably only know who have ever had such a
bias on a subject which they deemed important, and
narrowly examined their own minds while coming to
their conclusion upon it . Yet though a person should,
from the infirmity of human nature, or from want of
self-observation, fall short of the frame of mind ne-
cessary to conduct him safely to truth, still if it ap-
pear that truth was the real object which he had in
view, I would not hastily condemn him, because he
                       I


114


failed in attaining it. My business was with the
character displayed, not with the results. " Illi in

vos sæviant," says Augustine,            " qui nesciunt quo
cum labore verum inveniatur, et quam difficile ca-
veantur errores ."
      Truth then, I still think, was generally the object
of the German enquirers ; at least so it appeared to
myself, and such I believe is the impression in Ger-
many with regard to most of those, whose names and
investigations are of sufficient note to enter into an
account of the theology of that period . They may have
been sometimes dazzled by specious views ; they may,
as I have said ',       have occasionally carried on their

investigations upon wrong principles ;" they may at
other times have been influenced by a preconceived
theory, or the prevailing opinions of the day : but it
still appeared to me that the object, of which they
themselves were conscious, was " truth," and in some
cases there is evidence that the authors would have

gladly arrived at more satisfactory conclusions if they
could ; while others , as in the well - known case of Ro-

I MUST now enter upon a series of questions, in
which I would willingly have left my essay to the
judgment of those who may hereafter investigate the


120


subject ; since it necessarily involves me in a good
deal of wearisome detail, though I hope to be able
incidentally to throw light on a portion of the en-
quiry itself. The charges, which I have now to re-
move, relate to the character of the outline which I

gave of the theology of the seventeenth century,
and the manner in which I have employed my sup-
posed sources of information.         Had the books which
I am alleged to have misquoted, been in common use,
I would gladly have left to the judgment of others,
who could examine the question, to decide whether I
have exaggerated       or misquoted what I have pro-
fessed to cite.    My object in attempting this brief
sketch was, as I stated , " rather to induce others,
whose principal department may be within the pro-
vince of ecclesiastical history, to institute a fuller
investigation , than that my own opinions should be
adopted '," and I wished to leave it for the future in
other hands.      In the present stage, however, of our
knowledge of Germany, these allegations must ne-
cessarily produce     a serious prejudice against the
views, which have been stated to have been sup-
ported by such means ; -views which I cannot but
think of some practical importance.          I have also ,
on personal grounds, no longer any choice : since I
have thought it necessary to vindicate my other
views, any omission of this part would look like a
confession that it could not be defended . I must
therefore endeavour to set myself right in this point
also as briefly as I can.       The charge, then , of Mr.


                      1 Preface, p. xiii.


121


Rose is indeed a " very serious" one ; that I " bring
forward only the unfavourable remarks which the
writers whom I quote make on any author (among
the early divines of Germany) of whom they are
speaking, and omit every thing which they allege in
his favour¹."   A charge          of such a heavy nature
ought to have been supported with the utmost cir-
cumspection ; I regret, for the sake of both, that
occupied as Mr. Rose was with other subjects, he
has given but a hasty consideration to this, and con-
sequently has, in some cases, himself misquoted those
very authorities : in other instances, assuming that
what I knew of the earlier German writers was derived

solely from the books which I quote, he has hastily
condemned me of misrepresentation, if any statement
of mine, while it approaches to one in these authors,
vary from it.   Even the omission of any thing stated
by them has been regarded as culpable ; so that the
work should have become a mere hearing of wit-
nesses,   instead of       occasionally bringing   forward
those whose depositions I thought most illustrative.
  Before I enter, however, upon the immediate sub-
ject, I must state, in justice to myself, and to many
good men, whom every age, and therefore that in
question must have produced, that I no where in-
tended, as Mr. Rose has understood me, to speak of
the universal character of any age .         No age, whe-
ther good or bad, can have an exclusive character ;
no one can speak but of that which is predominant .
In the words of the great ecclesiastical historian of

                       1
                           Letter, p. 116.


122


Germany¹ , " There are in the life of mankind , as
yet not wholly regenerate, as in that of individuals,
periods, in which that portion of human nature,
which is allied to God, others in which that por-
tion which is alien from him, manifests itself on the
largest scale .   Yet may the observer, who penetrates
more deeply, and does not allow himself to be de-
ceived by what appears most on the surface, observe
both in every age ; human nature will never deny
its essential character. In the most corrupted age,

the contrast of good to the predominant evil, is
never wanting ; as neither, on the other hand, is that
of evil to good in an age distinguished by the most
splendid phænomena ; but it shows itself in what,
to the superficial glance, appears to be a manifesta-
tion of good, as well          as in   what outwardly also
stands in opposition to it."           It was the feeling of
this truth, and the pain of having so much evil to
                             2
record, which made me first    deprecate any judg
ments upon the individuals generally, while we con-
demned and regretted the results , and which made me
enumerate some of the grounds " which should pre-
vent our judging harshly of those whose difficulties we
have not experienced ." The same sickening at the

details which I had felt obliged to give, both of the

wish to prevent the wrong conclusions often arising
from the outward history of religion , when not cor-
rected by the results, which the interior of private
life can alone supply, made me dwell on the very

most of individuals, which I had in view.         All man-
kind, I hope, except the wilful hypocrite, are better
than they seem.    The plan of my book lay in one
respect within yet narrower limits.         My intention
was not (although that would perhaps have been the

   The censures in question relate to a brief sketch

which form the theological preparation for the learned
or practical theologian in Germany ; each department
being studied at greater or less extent, according to

127


were confined to the multiplication of methods for

the subject, or with positive reference to controversy,
it was probable that these points should be thought
the most essential to the discharge of the duties of
a preacher.      Or again, if Christian morals were not
studied, they would naturally afterwards occupy at
most a very subordinate place.      An education posi-
tively bad is obviously more injurious than the entire
absence of any. The mere negative evil of the neglect
of certain studies had been comparatively slight ;
the positive evil that a faulty bias was given to the
mind by a wrong and exclusive pursuit of others, so
that the whole of theology appeared to be comprised
in the portions thus selected , was much more prejudi-
cial. In our own country, where little superintendence
is ordinarily exerted over study purely theological,
but general directions are alone given , it may still be
hoped, that, notwithstanding this deficiency, the line
taken will not be materially wrong, especially since
the mixed theological and religious instruction, given
at our Universities to all, is, as far as it goes, rightly
directed   in Germany, in consequence of the partial
nature of the impulse given, the course was posi-
tively wrong .
   It is then, on these grounds, important to observe
that my statements, with regard to two of these
branches (to which, however, I attached considerable
importance, ) remain untouched.      The first of these
is the neglect of Ecclesiastical History, which I
thought, on grounds which I need not now repeat,
contributed largely, both by its negative and positive


128


effects, to many of the most grievous subsequent errors
of Rationalism ' .   I may add to what I then observed,
that to the German mind, which is in itself naturally
more speculative than our own, and in which the
power of judging upon evidence is , further, often di-
minished by the absence of the practical life and prac-
tical habits, which our institutions foster among our-

selves, early and extensive acquaintance with history
becomes a yet more indispensable qualification. Yet
it is an undoubted fact, that in the greater part of the
17th century, the study of Ecclesiastical History was,
as Spener's practical views led him to complain, almost
extinct.   Germany seems to have sunk, as it were,
exhausted from the great effort called forth from the
Centuriators of Magdeburg, in the great struggle with
the Romish church ; an effort which, combined with
the opposed endeavours of the Romanist historians,
and other interests, made the seventeenth century

a period of extraordinary activity in this department
in Italy, France, England , and Holland . It is , indeed ,
not unfrequently the case, that, until general interest
in a subject is excited, a great advance made by a
single individual, or by a few only, subsequently
rather retards than advances the progress in it ; that
others content themselves with admiring from afar,
what has been already done, and pride themselves
on the beauty of the structure, without entering into
it at all, at least without examining for themselves
in what its excellencies consist, or whether it admit
of further perfection .       Thus while       the edifice of

Ecclesiastical History, the foundations of which were

did so ; in better times he had been himself milder :

danger also : and the severe censures, which Arnold

tributed to weaken the general belief in the truth ,
or, (which was likely to be yet more fatal, ) in the
very importance of their system.  His work gave
rise, indeed, to two valuable books in the next
century, those of Weismann and Mosheim.         Weis-
mann's Memorabilia, however, notwithstanding his
piety and his sound and tranquil judgment, were ill-
adapted by their want of order and of complete-
ness, and by the suspicious quarter whence they
proceeded, to give a new impulse : they were opposed
by Edzardi and the ultra-Lutherans.        The history
of Mosheim was too naked an outline to supply the
practical knowledge of Christian antiquity, which
was especially wanted for those times.   A work,
moreover, which imputed to the vices of an earlier
clergy the degradation of Christianity, deepening
through descending centuries, would tend yet fur-
ther to alienate men from history and historians ,
chiefly clerical .   The storm ,   however, had   then
already gathered ;     and thus, although insulated


131


points had been illustrated ', the period of the great
struggle arrived without that preparation, which a
previous enlarged acquaintance with the history of
the Church might have furnished : and thus, as I
stated, the dangers of that period, when it did come,
were increased, the means of defence were, to a

and character of Christian antiquity caused its lan-
guage also to be misunderstood ; and these miscon-
ceptions of its statements again brought with them
the shallow criticisms upon the great doctrines of
our religion ; it was, further, by this ignorance that
the pride, natural to a •state of high excitement, was
mainly fostered in the early rationalizing, or in the
subsequent rationalist schools :-a pride which, sur-
veying its own petty trophies, deemed itself superior
to all former ages, and hastily condemned as spu-
rious and valueless, whatever bore not the stamp of
its own image : hence also the wild dreams of the
perfectibility of the Christian revelation, while the
mode was unknown , in which that revelation had
proved itself in all the ages of its existence to be a
power of God.

Meantime this employment is by many considered so
contemptible, that there are not wanting preachers

 God, but their pay ; that they are also mostly not even
fitted to impart a true knowledge of God, since they
have it not themselves .  And indeed there are very

 many who have not a knowledge even of the letter
 of that which is or is not to be believed, so far
from  understanding thoroughly and spiritually,
what is the will of God in faith and in its fruits ."
Of the little effect of other instruction, where this
                                        66
was neglected, Spener thus speaks.         Catechising
is   as necessary to the Church as any other                      em-
ployment can be.           And this is the misfortune of our
country, that most who hear our sermons are igno-
rant of the first principles of the faith, so that they
can scarcely, and not so much as scarcely, understand
artificial discourses ; but these sound around their
ears, so that they gain little, besides the empty echo :
the mind is not even touched by the words which,
because they have often heard them, they fancy that
they comprehend, while they do understand nothing
more of their force, and of the truths expressed by
them, than the most ignorant. I speak not only
from my own experience, but from that of others, who
have observed with concern why our sermons are
of so little avail '."

" It cannot be doubted," says Spener again ', " that
the study of the Bible should be the chiefstudy : nor
can this be questioned by any one who reflects , that the
first principle of our theology is not reason, but reve-
lation, which we have only through Scripture ; hence
all studies in theology itself or introductory to it,
should rank as they are more or less directly con-
nected with Scripture.          But it is on the other side
undeniable, that this chief portion of theology, Scrip-
tural Interpretation , has not hitherto been pursued
with the befitting diligence or ability : and hence it
is, that a great portion of students are much more
strangers to this blessed book than they should be ;
nay it is to be feared that it remains but too strange
to them during their whole lives, even in their very
ministry, if God does not awaken them in his service,
so that they subsequently fetch up, by their own ex-
ertions, what others neglected towards them.   How
great is the injury resulting from this neglect, is evi-
dent to all, especially to those who do not judge
from appearances but from the real state of things."

  IV. For the above three divisions of theology,

namely, Ecclesiastical History, Scriptural Interpre-
tation, and Catechetical Instruction, little more has
been necessary than to illustrate further my former
positions  with regard to the remaining portions ,
Doctrinal and Moral Theology, practical books and
sermons, there are also positive censures to be ob-
viated.     Of those charges, however, which affect the
first class, Doctrinal Theology, five or six not only
refer to individuals, whose names are little known in
this country, but turn upon points so minute, and
would require a detail so dry, that no one probably
would follow me through it.           Attack has much in
it which is inviting, defence nothing.          Many will
trace with eager interest the rapid impetuosity of

                  1 Francke'ns Stift, i. 90.


153


an assault ; few, except those immediately concerned ,
will watch the slow and tedious process of repairing
the breaches which have been made, or have any
pleasure in observing how stone after stone of the
dismantled battlements     are gradually restored.   I
will, therefore, only dwell here upon those points,
which afford me an opportunity of illustrating the
state of German theology, referring those who wish
for this minute satisfaction to the end of the volume.

If Mr. Rose ' looks more carefully to the authors
whom he accuses me of having misquoted or garbled,
he will himself find that, 1. Twesten does not, as he

supposed, modify the " severe judgment" which he
passed upon Baier and Quenstedt.       2. Twesten's
66
   very favourable opinion" of Hutter has no refer-
ence to the qualities which I condemned in his work,
and in which alone I was concerned ; nor is his opi-
nion in fact thus favourable .   3. That Hollaz, whom
he blames me for not having mentioned, is very little,
if at all, distinguished from his contemporaries ; that
Schröckh's " great praise" of him and Höpfner is in
fact very moderate ; that if there was any offence
in the omission of Hollaz, Buddeus must also share
it, although he was writing expressly upon Doctrinal
Theology,    and that the only work mentioned of
Höpfner relates to one doctrine only, not to doc-
trinal theology generally.  4. That I did not over-
state either the dryness, the meagreness, or the po-
pularity of the work of König.
   On these and similar minute points, the judg-
ment of the excellent and pious Pfaff, who wrote
             1
               See Letter, p. 119–121 .


154


 posterior to all these authors, and was influenced by
no party feelings, will for the present afford sufficient
vindication.   He thus describes the previous state
 of doctrinal Theology .   " All the compendia of holy
 doctrines, which have hitherto appeared, are of such
a character, that though their excellence has been
hitherto extolled by the common praise of our coun-
trymen, and they still enjoy considerable reputation
(sua utique luce niteat) they can even on this ground
not be satisfactory to our age, that since one system
was extracted and worked out of the other, with a
very few variations, they dwell uniformly on the
same string ; and that metaphysical clang of causes,'
which sounds somewhat harshly and unpleasantly to
well-cultivated ears, constantly reverberates in them,
the same terms uniformly recurring in all.  To this
is added, that a certain coldness appears to prevail
in the common mode of treating these subjects, es-
pecially in the practical topics of theology; these
being set forth as theoretical propositions, so that
scarcely any life or any religious influence finds its
way into the minds of readers ; and the edification

of mind, (though it should be the principal object in
sacred theology,) derived from them is very slight.
Nor does it appear less a subject of blame, that various
theological root, and those the very chief, are here
altogether omitted ; that every thing is choked with
the thorns of scholasticism ; and that divine truths
are often made secondary to the zeal for autho-
rity : nor is there sufficient reference to the language
of the symbolical books, to the promotion of the
peace of the church , to the exhibition of what is of


155


real importance in controverted points, and of the
unreality of the mere logomachies, with which all
Theology abounds ; nor again, to destroy theological
pedantry and a sectarian spirit, or to treat the sub-
jects themselves in a style becoming to them : but
most of all, sufficient pains are not bestowed upon
that which is of chief importance, the building
up the kingdom of God in the hearts of men, and
the influencing their hearts more thoroughly with
vivid conceptions of true Christianity '."         Pfaff adds
indeed , in a charitable spirit,      that he " does not
mean to say this to the contempt of the elder writers,
that he received whatever had been said well by them
with affection and gratitude, that he could easily
understand that much darkness and much that was

trifling and valueless had come in from the Scholas-
tic Theology, but that still candour did not permit
these things to be passed over. " If Pfaff might speak
thus indiscriminately of all the writers on Doctrinal

Theology of the 17th century, I need surely no
further excuse for not entering into the minuter
shades of some of the least distinguished.
   The alleged unfairness with regard to Calov on the
other hand turns, first, upon the scholasticism and
the neglect of Scripture which I imputed to the 17th
century in Germany ; secondly, upon its polemical
character ;   and an explanation          may    accordingly
throw some additional light on these several points.
My assertion of the abuse of scriptural proof by these
theologians shall subsequently be vindicated and fur-
ther illustrated.
   1 Prologus. Galeatus, ad Institt . Theol. Dogm. p . 5-7.

   1. On the first point Mr. Rose observes ' , " When
Schröckh, from whom Mr. Pusey has taken his mat-
ter, refers him to Buddeus (an author of whom Mr.
Pusey makes constant use) why does he omit the very
strong testimony which Buddeus gives as to the work
in question ? In direct contradiction to Mr. P.'s state-
ment of the unscriptural character of the age, Bud-
deus says of that work and its author, Laudandum

in eo maximopere, quod scripturæ cum primis habuerit
rationem, et in ejus testimoniis, quæ magno numero
adducit, omne causæ præsidium collocaverit ;' and
again,    In ipsa dogmatum tractatione Calovius equi-
dem sequitur methodum caussalem, seu Aristotelico-
Scholasticam, jam tum receptam ; ad reliqua tamen
Theologia Scholasticæ vitia non adeo propensus
est, quin potius Scripturæ ubique, quod jam ante
in eo laudavimus, urget auctoritatem ."" (Isag. p. 357.)
It might again suffice, in self-justification , to say,
that as I did not found my views on Buddeus' state-
ments, (and indeed had not his work with me until
my essay was nearly finished ,) I had a perfect right
to adduce him in illustration, when my own views
agreed with his, and on other occasions to set him
aside.    In fact, however, the supposition that he
does here contradict me, has arisen from a miscon-
ception of his meaning as well as of mine.    That, for
which Buddeus does praise Calov, has no relation to
the faults which I found in him in common with his

age .    Buddeus praises him that, notwithstanding
his following the scholastic form, he does not fall


                    ¹ Letter, p. 117.


157


into the other most grievous error of these writers,
namely, that of resting their    doctrines first and

principally upon human reason, and admitting Scrip-
ture into their systems only as a subordinate autho-
rity.   In opposition to this, Buddeus praises him
for resting his system on Scripture.   Into this error
it was not probable that he or any other writers of
his age should fall ; nor did I any where charge them
with it.   The paramount authority of Scripture, as
it had been restored by Protestantism, so was it also
its distinctive characteristic ; and the abandoning it
would have involved the abandonment of the very
principle upon which Protestantism was founded.
It was not, therefore, probable that this error of the
scholastic age would re-appear in the same form
among Protestants ; nor did it, until the rise of a
new philosophy, ( that of Wolf,) tended to soften the
contradiction .
              The escape, however, from intellec-
tual or moral error would be comparatively very
easy, perhaps too easy for the purposes of our con-
dition here, did the same fault appear only in the
same form, so that we should be safe from its in-

fluence as soon as we had rejected it in that one
shape . Yet as every error has its seat in human
nature, there are none probably from which we are
wholly secure.    Since they can, accordingly, only be
avoided by unremitting vigilance, we are probably
even most exposed to a modified influence from them,
when in another form we have entered our protest
against them ; or when they appear in their full ex-
tent to be at variance with principles of which we
make our boast. In the language of Burke, the


158


error 66 stalks abroad while we are gibbeting its
carcase or demolishing its tomb."                The form in
which I thought that scholastic theology did re-ap-
pear, besides the phraseology, &c. was, that in the
course of the controversy the system was first form-

ed, and then passages of Scripture found to support
it ; the stress of evidence indeed, in conformity to
the fundamental principles of Protestantism, was laid
upon these passages ; and the authors in question un-
doubtedly persuaded themselves that such evidence

was adequate        still the system itself, or the parts of
that system, were formed independently of it, and
consequently portions of doctrine were often rather
introduced into Scripture than drawn out of it.
The same process was frequently repeated in the
further expansion of the system : when any new con-
troversy arose, instead of recurring to Scripture to
modify their doctrine according to it, the parties,
being persuaded that their system was correct, pro-
ceeded to prop it up by other theories or deduc-
tions, and found these also in Scripture.

  1 Since writing the above, I have found a similar statement in
Pfaff(de Præjudic. Theol . § 10. p. 561. cp. p. 596.) “ This
fault (a vicious attachment to system) has extended itself over all
parts of theology. In doctrinal theology, the system of faith is
first formed in accordance to the Confession of the Church to
which each belongs ; and not until this has been done, are pas-
sages of Scripture looked for to prove the tenets , which have been
already given out as true . Moreover, (he adds, on another de-
fect, the causal method ,) it is thought necessary that the series

without any great advantage, is productive of great wearisome-
ness, especially since the same causes every where recur, or if
they do not readily present themselves, are to be invented . "

  By another abuse of scriptural evidence, theolo-
gians of this day heaped together, in support of a
given doctrine, passages that possessed no real force
of proof, thus encumbering and disguising the real
proof, and employing armour which had not been
essayed, which accordingly made a show till the
day of battle, but left the wearer unguarded and de-
fenceless at the time when he most needed it.       The

more a writer quoted Scripture in this manner, the
less scriptural would that writer probably be.      The
influence   of system in fettering the meaning        of
Scripture has been already touched upon.         It was
perfectly consistent then with my own opinions, that
Buddeus did, in one point, contrast Calov's conduct
with an error of the scholastic age, which I did not
impute to his work, and praised him for having rested
his cause on the testimonies of Scripture, " quæ mag-
no numero adducit :" my complaint of the Doctrinal
Theology of that period was, that it made not too
little, but a misapplied , and therefore often too much,
use of Scripture ; to purposes, namely, for which it
was not intended .   The neglect of Scripture ' , which
I did regret in this age, had no reference to this.
It was the neglect of teaching it for other purposes
than those of controversy, and of doctrinal systems,
which were frequently principally controversial : it
was the comparative neglect of the study of Scrip-
ture for itself ; that theologians were instructed in it,
when they were so instructed , with reference to
doctrinal theology, to polemics, to controverted pas-

sages, but not in connection , in which , however, alone

   2. The other statement respecting Calov, which

was probably little known in this country, I referred,

seemed at first to make this sacrifice, they have
