  The larger illustration of the perversion of Scrip-
ture, introduced by this system, might, by tracing it
to its various sources, become an useful work for all

times .  At present, however, instead of entering
into this more extensive field, I will only produce
two additional testimonies from writers of different

schools, but both well acquainted with the doctrinal
theology of their country, and possessed of great
learning and discriminating judgment . The first is
C. M. Pfaff, who was connected with the school of
the Pietists, but had preserved himself by enlarged
intercourse with theologians of different countries
from the confined views, by which the utility of the
later members of that school was circumscribed . He
says, then, in treating of the theological defects of
his day¹, " No one will deny that doctrinal theology
is very superficially learnt, who has observed, that
what is for the most part exclusively imbibed, are

common-places concerning the faith, and theological
refinements, involved in the causal method ; that the
mere theory of things is dwelt upon, to the neglect of
ethics, (although these also belong to doctrinal theo-
logy, ) as well as of the practical tendency of the doc-
trines, which, however, ought here to be the principal
object : that those points also which are most practical,
the doctrines of repentance, faith, turning to God,
sanctification, good works,         in one word, those of
true Christianity, are but coldly interspersed : that
the scriptural proofs are brought without discri-
mination, and are numbered not weighed : that
the doctrines are from time to time involved in
scholastic subtleties, which contribute absolutely no-
thing to instruction in the truth or in piety, while
the simplicity of the faith is neglected : that scarcely
any care is taken to produce a religious conviction,
and that the principal objects are the pre-conceived
and partial opinions (præjudicia) of divines ."     Pfaff
accordingly proposed to himself in his own doctrinal

work ', among these more important objects, to dis-
tinguish between the several degrees of proof ; pro-
posing, first, those passages which treated directly
and principally of the point in question ; secondly,
those which proved it only to a certain degree and less

found in each passage, which this deemed useful, a

further swelled through the error , to which the ease
of bringing such proofs together, soon led them,
namely, the laying an especial value on the number

it would be great injustice, to deny that there were

markably enough from each other ; that some em-
ploy more selection in the     dictis probantibus,'
more wisdom in their collocation, more restriction

in their employment, and more art in displaying
their valid sides ; while others prove every thing
out of every thing, and only       appear anxious to
force each of their positions out of a large mass of
passages in the Bible : although this weak side is
remarkable enough in all, only in some, more, in
others less ."

   The state of doctrinal theology at the beginning
of this century is again very forcibly characterised in
one of the early dialogues of Andrea '.            " A. I have
most happily escaped to-day a great danger.                    B.
Indeed ! I wish you all joy.            A. Thanks, but you
must include him also, by whose aid I now believe
more circumspectly.         B. What is this ?          Can you
then believe uncircumspectly ? A. Very easily, for
when I believe a doctrinal statement, which seems

to me very sound         and clear, there may lie one
little word in it, which involves the most dangerous
consequences.      So it happens that for want of cir-
cumspection, my belief becomes impious.                 B. And
you then become guilty of impiety ?               A. I do , and
am therefore now devising formulæ, that I may
for the future believe as            circumspectly as possi-
ble.    B. For my part,        highly as I esteem your

acuteness in disputing       and distinguishing, I can-
not yet raise myself to that height of genius ; but
seize with simple and almost peasant faith what I
see to agree with Scripture, and am not so anxious.
about words, as to fear from them some heresy I
know not of.  A. Look now how careless thou art.
Has not Christ suffered ?        B. Truly.         Glory be to
him for it.     A. Tell me then , according to what

mode of union of the two natures ?        B. I am bold

suffering, however, must have had its origin in the
preceding or following divine will .     B. I only know

A. It is not enough to believe all this generally, but
thou must observe the rocks on which human curio-

sity splits, that the same may not befall thee in thy
way to salvation .    B. Yes, truly, as new laws pro-
duce new crimes, so new points of doctrine also new
heretics. A. Thou shouldest not mock in so serious
a matter.   B. I confess that I also did once uncau-
tiously attempt the same as you : I wished, namely,
to undo all the knots formed by human reason, and
level all these rugged rocks ; but since this pursuit of
unimportant minutiæ ever led me again and again to
new ones, my wish would have been to banish from my

mind all memory of them ; I then closed all my senses
to them as much as possible, and dedicated my whole
mind to a humble obedience to Christ .        Since that


175


time I have become more tranquil, and am only dis-
turbed by the recollection of my former curiosity.
A. But how is it possible, that among so many ob-
jections, distinctions , and even sophisms, thou alone
hast arrived at the truth without a guide ? B. Very
easily, for while others consulted innumerable expo-
sitors about divine things, and endeavoured to re-
concile the enormous discrepancies between them, I
was convinced that the truth of the words of Christ
depends upon no artifices of interpretation, but on a
simple and humble will, and thus I endeavour to
found my obedience on no evasions or excuses, but
on a constant readiness [to admit the divine truth]
and on a pious practice .         A. That is in truth a
boorish theology.     B. Be it so ; that other          is a
sophistical one, and has become so thorny by means
of unprofitable questions and distinctions, that in
these days neither Peter nor Paul, were they to
return to life, would be able to satisfy it."

have received power and opportunity from God so
to do, should earnestly devise means for bringing
back our countrymen from this scholastic theology
to that of the Bible ¹"

ciples were generally spread."          Anton also observes

   VI. Practical books .       Works of this class, with
some great exceptions, are from their own nature
of a more temporary character than any other theo-
logical production .    Generally speaking, they are,
and must be, adapted to the peculiar circumstances
of their own age ; they must be especially addressed
to correct its prevailing evil tendencies ; they must
promote pre-eminently those portions of the Chris-
tian   character which are       least cultivated .    They
must also in their external form partake in some
measure of the habits of the times.   Although
then such as are founded on a deep knowledge of
human nature, and animated with genuine piety,
must benefit other ages, since human nature remains
essentially the same, their most direct influence be-

longs to the age in which they are written : subse-
quently they may indeed often form individuals : trans-
fused into their minds they are re-produced in other
shapes, but are themselves withdrawn from circula-

                       1
                       ¹ L. c. p. 32.


181


tion.   Their body perishes, while the soul which
gives it life,   migrates   into another and another
frame, and thus continues often to diffuse an exten-
sive blessing, when the very name under which they
originally appeared is forgotten .
   Into this difficult field, accordingly, it was not
my intention to enter ; the subject which I had laid
down for myself in these pages was, as I have stated ,
the different branches of theology, some acquaintance
with which constitutes the preparation for the subse-
quent scientific or practical     exertions .   Though I
have, therefore, incidentally in different places , men-
tioned the principal of those practical books, which had
come to my knowledge, I made no distinct head on
this subject, but merely observed , as an illustration,
that Buddeus stated the same fact as Schröckh ( the
extent and vehemence of theological controversy, ) and
the same ground for the degeneracy and defects of
books of .edification .
                      I quoted from Buddeus the
following passage : " Fuit ceteroquin jam sæculo
decimo sexto ea temporum infelicitas, ut in certa-
mina et intestinas dissensiones raperentur præstan-
tissima ingenia ; indeque contingebat, ut qui vitæ
morumque præcepta inculcabant minus sapere reli-
quis viderentur."
   On this Mr. Rose observes : " Now it is a little

singular that Mr. Pusey's attack on Germany relates
to the seventeenth century, whereas the passage
from    Buddeus refers to the sixteenth, and that
writer goes on to state, that as the evils of this state
of things were felt at the beginning of the next cen-
tury (viz. the very seventeenth, of which Mr. Pusey


182


speaks so contemptuously ?) there arose in that cen-
tury first the great Arndt, then the admirable J.
Gerhard, and then a whole host of others, who felt
and endeavoured to remove these evils ¹."

   I must again regret that Mr. Rose wrote this por-
tion of his work in so much haste ; he could not
otherwise but have observed that a writer who says
" fuit ceteroquin jam decimo sexto sæculo" must be
speaking, not exclusively certainly of the 16th cen-
tury, but also, and that principally, of that which
followed it.    The phrase can mean nothing but that
the subject spoken of existed in some degree in the
16th century, but was realized in its full extent in
following times.      That Buddeus mentioned indivi-
duals who opposed themselves to this prevailing
character, who endeavoured to give fresh life to the
decayed zeal for piety and holiness, and to check the
increasing corruption of manners (cum magnus in
pietatis sanctitatisque studio tepor exortus esset, mo-
rumque corruptio valde invalesceret, ) is no more
than I myself have done.         The very first instance
quoted, that of the " great Arndt," is one on which
I have myself dwelt , and the account which I gave
of the vehement and unchristian opposition made
to him by the clergy, is a melancholy proof of the
truth of Buddeus ' remark,        ' qui vitæ morumque
præcepta inculcabant, minus sapere reliquis videban-
tur.'   Of the second also, who is mentioned, the ad-
mirable J. Gerhard, I have myself frequently spoken.

  Mr. Rose adds, however, that Buddeus " mentions
in addition a whole host of others, who felt and
endeavoured to remedy these evils." This appeared
to me from my recollections of Buddeus extraordi-
nary, yet as he frequently gives large catalogues , I
feared that I had overlooked one, and referred with
some mistrust for this       whole host,' relating as they
did to a whole century, and brought together from
the whole extent of Protestant Germany. I own I
was surprised when this whole host dwindled into
eight , two of whom I had myself mentioned .               Yet
in truth the list might have been swelled ; this would,
however, only have increased the melancholy evidence
of the predominant state of the German Church in
this century.    These names should occupy a place in-
deed in any history of these times, as those of men who
felt and who endeavoured to remedy, but in vain, the
existing evils ; they should be named as predecessors
of the great Spener, as having carried on the same
struggle as he did with the vices of their times, and as
having met with the same reward from their contem-
poraries , the imputation of heresies, and misconcep-
tion of their intentions.   Yet, however interesting
their history is as a proof that God did not forsake

   VII. Preaching.           Two censures are cast on my
statements of the state of the German pulpit in the
17th century ; 1st, that there was good mixed with the
evil, which tempered and redeemed it, and that this
I omitted ; 2nd, that the instances given of its pole-
mical character are not sufficiently large to form an
induction.
   1. The first charge is conveyed in the following
words : " Mr. Pusey concludes what he has to say
by mentioning that there are splendid exceptions,
such as Arndt, J. V. Andreá, and J. Gerhard, but
that Schröckh says these were but exceptions, and
influenced the whole but little. Why did he not
add what Schröckh says, two or three pages before,
when giving this list of preachers, viz. that it is con-
solatory to find in the midst of all these evils of the
period, men who ' , though not to be imitated in
every thing, yet preached (and set the example of)
sermons, composed with so noble an aim that they
could not fail to produce a happy impression in in-
numerable instances ? This is the point for which I
                            2
contend.  There was good "," &c.     Yet, with the
exception of what is left to be inferred from his
parenthesis, the effect of the example of the ser-
mons of these valuable men upon the clergy, I have
said every thing which Mr. Rose asks why I did not
say.   This parenthesis, however, has arisen in a mis-

   2. The second objection is , that the instances given
of Lutheran preaching are not sufficient to constitute
an induction .     Undoubtedly they are not, nor had I
any intention of furnishing one ; nor could instances,
multiplied to any extent, be sufficient for that pur-
pose . It might still be urged, however wearisome a
catalogue had been produced , that they were but ex-
ceptions.    Those given were quoted not in proof, but
in illustration.       The proof really given was de-
rived from the language of those who either lived
nearer to or at those times, as Gerhard, J. Müller ',
and Spener ; or of those, as Schröckh³, who had
access to the means of judging that the induction
was correct        or it resulted from what appeared,
either directly in the course of the history itself, in
the opposition to genuine Christian preaching, (as in
the cases of Arndt , or Gerhard , or Spener ', ) or in

nitely enlarged ; the very full notices of such preach-

Protestant controversy, shews that the subjects
were unseasonably and intemperately brought into

a wide extent,     and in an unprofitable manner '.
Two points, however , appear to me to furnish a more
compendious and yet fuller evidence of the state of
the German pulpit ; -one (of which instances have
been already given ) , the civil edicts against polemical
preaching ; the other, the charge constantly urged
against the Pietists, that their preaching was not
sufficiently polemical.      Of such edicts, many occur
in the controversy with Calixtus, as well as in the
contests which prior to his time rent the Lutheran
church.   I will instance some of those which relate
to the Pietistic controversies alone, since the late-
ness of this period increases the importance of the
evidence.    In 1691 , then, an edict was passed to
this effect, extending to the whole dukedom of Prus-
sia , which , having been found ineffectual, was repeat-
ed in 1692 , and a fine imposed on the ministers who
should persist in preaching against the Pietists . Yet
the interference of Seckendorf was again necessary

the same year ³.     Carpzoff, who was distinguished for

his skill in preaching, declaimed against the Pietists
in all his sermons as well as his lectures, and in conse-
quence of the disturbances to which this gave rise,
the employment of the names of Pietists, or Pietism,
as opprobrious titles, in the pulpit or the lecture-
room was forbidden in Electoral Saxony '.         In Gotha,
in 1692, after sermons had been repeatedly preached
against the Pietists, and complaints made against the
General- Superintendant as a Pietist, all the charges
were declared by a civil commission unjust ; those
against the latter a pasquinade         .   In 1693,     4, a
contest was occasioned in Dantzig, by Schelwig's
publication of his sermon against the Pietists, entitled
"the expulsion of the evil of fanaticism," when the
authorities again interposed .        In 1715, the Duke
of Saxony published a strict edict against the fana-
tics, in which adherence to the symbolical books and
the catechism, &c. was prescribed, not merely as to
the doctrines, but as to the very expressions to be
used (modum loquendi et informandi) and especially
as to the explanations of the regular biblical texts :
yet in this edict, where one should expect nothing
favourable to the Pietists, we have the remarkable
admonitions, that " should any one fall into errors,
and endeavour to disseminate them, he shouldfirst be
treated with as a brother, and the affair not be
forthwith brought with vehemence into the pul-
pit, but that information should be carried to the pro-
per place, where the case must be fairly examined, and

further directions expected ; well remembering that
religion was a high and holy subject, which was
to be treated not withfleshly zeal, but with reverent
caution, and not to be defended but with true love
to God and erringfellow-Christians¹ ." It is added ,

carry them too far beyond these bounds."           From
a subsequent part of the same edict, it appears
that the offence had been mutual and very extensive.
In 1726, an edict 2 was issued in Electoral Saxony,
expressly to restrain the abuses of the Elenchus, re-
ferring to former decrees and constitutions.  In this
again it was allowed that the Elenchus, or office of
reproving, in its due degree, and according to the
divine word, belonged to the Teachers : it was stated
that the only object proposed was to stop the abuse
which had hitherto existed, and especially the in-
ordinate imputation of errors to individuals, and
the employment of contemptuous or even defamatory
names in these censures ; whereas they ought rather
to warn the youth against such abuses, and to lead
them to a true and solid knowledge of those sciences
of which they treated, and not less to the courtesy,
Christian modesty, and humility, which belonged to

" there arose on the other side great commotions ;
they complained of the dangerous state of religion
in Electoral Saxony, in that Pietism was gaining the
upper hand, and the blame being imputed to the
first court preacher, Marperger, there appeared all
sorts of defamatory writings against him."    A similar
edict was published in Hesse.        There occur also
some remarkable expressions in the edict issued in
the name of the king of Poland and the elector of
Saxony ' in 1731 , in Upper Lausitz.      It is directed
to all beneficed Clergy, Deacons, or their substitutes,
to Rectors of schools and others employed in them,
and expresses great displeasure at the offensive alter-
cations in religious matters, wherein the modesty
becoming theologians had been laid aside, and all
sorts of disorder introduced ; satirical style, namely,
captious misinterpretations of the words and mean-
ing of a writer, unfounded relations and charges ;
sectarian and invidious denunciations of Pietism and

Pietists against persons who adhered to the confes-
sion of Augsburg ; tending, if not directed, to pro-
duce division .     It states, referring to an edict of
1727, that the intention was, not to prevent the refu-
tation of any errors against the symbolical books,
where they really existed, and did not proceed from
wilful distortion of words, or sophistical inferences ,

but,    " after the example of the other Kings, Elec-
                  1
                    Walch, Ib. v. 487. fgg.
                            0


194


tors, and Princes in and out of the Roman empire,
to forbid the use of those two sectarian and invidious
names of Pietism and Pietist, (which had hitherto
been extended far too wide, and employed without
any bounds, according to the will or passions of
each, ) in disputations, sermons, school instructions,
and public writings ."   It adds, " from such odious
titles too little good had resulted to the Lutheran
Church ; on the contrary, there had arisen the per-
nicious abuse, which was constantly preying more and
more, that upright and unblameable people and mi-
nisters, (though they neither acknowledged in the
least, nor were convicted of, the errors imputed to
them, ) were yet grossly libelled, and rendered ob-
jects of suspicion, both in and out of that land.
Thereby had embittering, and all sorts of mistrust,
been occasioned, excited, and continued in the
minds of teachers and hearers ; these again had burst
in several places forth in but too various and un-
restrained wrong, contention, uproar, &c., which had
been a source of trouble to others also, and had dis-
turbed the tranquillity of the commonweal : under
this pretext also had many other feelings and wrong-
ful private objects often been gratified : even what
had in its favour the appearance of a peaceful, re-

tired, pious, and inoffensive life, was forthwith
arbitrarily cried down, hereticized, and destroyed,
without end or measure ; so that it must almost
wear the appearance that there was something in
the Confession of Augsburg opposed to influential
Christianity."   Suspension, accordingly, or, upon
continued disobedience, removal from office was de-

nounced against the use of the words        Pietist or

      C
The     Elenchus nominalis' was still permitted, if

used for its right ends, and with the fitting modera-
tion, prudence, and discretion. In 1734, an edict
of the duke of Schlesswig Holstein ' against separa-

tists and fanatics, again, contains directions to the
Clergy, that " should there be one or other of pecu-
liar habits in their cure, they were not forthwith to
bring the matter into the pulpit, whereby the con-
sciences of the weak were yet more disturbed, the
bad made much bolder, and pious and excellent men
were sometimes loaded with sectarian names or re-

vilings by the ignorant, and were rendered objects
of suspicion : whereas those who were misled by
weakness or simplicity, (the intention being good,
and the object real piety, ) might more frequently
be brought back from the ill prejudices, or perni-
cious errors, which they had conceived , by charity,
mildness, and patience ."
  The especial evils of this kind of controversy are
sufficiently evident ; they are well expressed in the
edict of the king of Sweden in 1726.       This edict
is again the stronger, since it followed upon many
others against fanaticism , which had been unhappily
applied to the so-called Pietists.   In this, the con-
duct of some preachers is condemned, who took
occasion in their sermons to use the names of Pietist
or Pietism, as those of a sect or of certain errors ;

  The objection made to the Pietists for depreciating
controversy was mentioned in the Enquiry ' .     It was
not, however, all controversy, (i. e. all vindication of
the truth in opposition to those who departed from
it,) which they declined , but the narrow, the bitter,
and the exclusive personal controversy which then
reigned.  Spener himself wrote ably against both

must still in great measure depend upon the credit
attached to the relator.  Yet it may perhaps give
more vividness to our ideas of the preaching during
this long period , if we examine a few statements from
men who lived in different portions of it, and who
possessed the means, and judgment, and candour, ne-
cessary to form an accurate estimate.  Many of the
general defects in the earlier part of this age are
touched upon in a Dialogue of Andrea, entitled
" The Pulpit Orator," in which far more is conveyed
than appears upon the surface.     Much more will
occur in the passages which will be adduced from him

with regard to the general character of his days.
  " A. Tell me honestly, I pray thee, what you found
wanting in my sermon.          B. One thing        only, but
that a main point.      A. Not surely the arrange-
ment ?    B. It was, I believe, according to the rules
of the methods.    A. The pronunciation ?           B. You
must speak, as God has made you , only not imitate.
A. Or the action ?     B. About that I am indifferent,

if it be only quiet and not gesticulatory.           A. My
sermon then was too long ?          B. If a sermon is good,
it cannot be too long ; a bad one always is.         A. Or
did I not produce      quotations       enough ?    B. You
could not have meant to empty a casket of quota-
tions.   A. I spoke then too slow ?        B. Ha ! in the
pulpit one must teach, not talk volubly.        A. Or not
loud enough ? B. I like the voice of a man, not
the braying of an ass. A. Or should I have used

more subtile distinctions ?    B. Yet you were there to
instruct the ignorant, not to dispute with heretics.
A. Do then explain yourself.            B. Hear me ; you


204


said I think much, very much, which was good, but
it only flowed through you as through a pipe. A.
Indeed ? B. So that much, moreover, contracted the
taste of the pipe, and savoured accordingly. A. No
good compliment.      B. The best which I can make.
For when you only cast forth good and wholesome
doctrines, and shew nothing of them expressed in
your life and your manners, so that you seem, placed
as it were out of yourself, to speak one thing and
think another, do you not make us believe at last,
that your holy words         are only practised solemn
words, without any real feeling, just as poets make for
the occasion, at one time bridal-songs, at another
dirges ? You have, it is true, many passages of
Scripture in readiness, but they exhort, strengthen,
instruct, console not you, though others die with
joy with only the consolation of the divine word.
A. You are severe upon me.   B. Is it not however

evident, that the worst men often preach the best ?
and that many cannot but preach ?           I wish but one
thing ; that for the future you should say nothing,
but what you expressed by your example in action,
or at least realized by serious endeavours after obe-
dience to God.     A. That is harsh.    B. Incompa-

rably harsher, however, openly to contradict one-
self before God both in words and works, and convert
the divine service into an empty clatter of words .
A. True, certainly.    B. And as true, believe me,
that a simple plain sermon, exhibited and sealed by
your life, is more valuable than a thousand clever
declamations ."

   The evil of the schemes of preaching, mentioned
in the Enquiry, upon which we have just seen Andrea
touch, is frequently dwelt upon by Spener.   " Since

art has come up into the pulpit, strength has much
diminished, (conformably to St. Paul, 1 Cor. i . 17.)
and I fear that the       latter will not again increase,
until we return to more apostolic simplicity, which
                            66
God grant ." And again ,       Since a dispropor-
tionate value has been so often attached to that
ecclesiastical oratory,      and this not for very many
years (for in our grandfathers ' time little pains were
bestowed upon it) many have filled the mind of the
student with the pernicious opinion, that since they
are destined for the pulpit, and the main duties of
their office lies in preaching, the first and greatest
object of their studies should be to learn the art of
preaching.      Hence it has become the first and last
care of many, who are fascinated with this opinion, to
study Homiletics ; to learn the art of making artificial
discourses, wholly unconcerned as to the matter of
what they are hereafter to say :-not unlike one
who, anxious      alone about acquiring dexterity in
putting together and fashioning shoes, should be
entirely careless about the means of obtaining the
leather ; so that he must afterwards either beg it,
or for want thereof make out of paper, parchment,
or other unfit materials, very elegant shoes indeed,
according to the rules of art, but such as can never
be used.    For what else can we hope of men,


  1 Letzte Theol. Bed. iii . 723.
  2 Consil. Lat. i. 222, ap Hossbach Spener, i . 33.


206


since they never have duly admitted the objects of
belief or of practice into their minds, have never
placed themselves in that frame of mind out of
which (after the necessary thought) what is to be said
streams forth spontaneously, employ all their labour
on ecclesiastical oratory, concerned only about the ar-
tifices of arrangement, of expansion, and of illustration,
and sweeping together with all diligence that, and that
only, which tends to enable them to speak methodically
and in set terms, not what will benefit the hearers , but
what they think the method they have chosen offers
or requires ?     Hence, when they say any thing good,
they speak, alas ! almost always what they have, with
great want ofjudgment, borrowed or transcribed from
others ; and even this they often do not sufficiently
understand, and so do not rightly apply.                   When
these expedients fail them, they speak absurd things,
as one would expect from men, who are ignorant of
true theology, whose principal anxiety is to learn the
laws of oratory, to collect formulæ of            arrangements'
as the sole treasure of wisdom, and place their whole
confidence upon it.'   At other times Spener enters
into the details of the evils generated by this system.¹

ties and effects ; on nature, the impediments and
aids of renovation ; on the contest between the

flesh and the spirit, and what is to be       observed
with respect to it ; on the power and obligations
of baptism, &c. are not treated or impressed with
sufficient care by most preachers, so that there
reigns among the hearers a deep ignorance of these
main points of Christian doctrine.      Many do not
propose the doctrines which they announce, so as to
shew their hearers what the uses of these doctrines

are in the Christian life , what incitement to piety
they contain, what consolation.     Many treat the
points of controversy more than is necessary for the
instruction of their hearers in all their sermons,
and in truth so, that the greater portion do not even
understand most of the discourse, the rest come at
last to the conviction , that our main endeavour lies
in observing what is contested between us and our
opponents, neglecting those portions which we have
in common .    Many are more indolent in the cate-
chetical instruction of youth, both publicly and pri-
vately, than the importance of this matter, and the
injury which ensues from its neglect to the Christian
church, require .   Many think that it is no portion
of their duty to trouble themselves about the know-
ledge as well as about the life of their hearers, and
their progress in it, or to promote their edification by

private intercourse.   Many are      wanting in other
parts also of their duty to the flock intrusted to
them."    The complaints of practical Anti- antino-

mianism (which occur so often in Andrea , ) as en-
couraged by the partial preaching of Christian doc-
                       1


208


trine, are urged again and again by Spener.            " The

doctrine of justification by faith alone ' , is a holy
doctrine, and we should not think it too much to shed

our blood for it ; but when the great careless multi-
tude so shamefully abuse it, that even while continuing
in sin and in its service, they still console them-
selves that they shall attain eternal life by faith alone,
will live and die in dependence upon this—then is
such doctrine, (which many entertain , in order that
they may still indulge their fleshly mind and their
careless security, ) not a true but a false doctrine ;
for it is a shameful perversion of the truth.             Its
source in many is this, that they have so often heard
that we are to be saved by faith alone,               not by
works : but they have not heard taught with the
same zeal what saving faith is, or how they are to
try themselves, whether they have that faith ; that
there can be no faith without repentance and a hatred
of sin ; that faith is a divine operation, not a mere
human conception ; and such like.             And their igno-
rance of these things prevents their understanding
the other holy doctrine of justification ; and the mean-
ing, which they in their security attach to it, is a
false meaning and a false doctrine .            And so it is
with other points.     So that we have not only ground
to complain of evil lives, but that with all these
discourses about faith, very little faith is left, nay,
that most are wholly ignorant what faith is.             For
had we more faith, we should have a very different
life ; so that our concern is not merely with the fruits


                1 Letzte Theol. Bed. i. 78.


209


but with the very root of good, since the fruits can
never come forth, where the root is not.     Would

that we showed our zeal in defending the truth of
our doctrine against       careless and wild people, as
much as against open opposers !          This last is effected
by our disputing, orally and in writing , when neces-
sary, and is not to be rejected, but has its excellent
uses, when confined within due bounds,               and not
regarded as the one thing needful, from which
the welfare of our Church is to be looked for."
So stiff was the state of preaching, that because
Spener urged practical Christianity, he had to defend
himself against the imputation of preaching mere
morality.    " It is," he says ' , " an utterly false im-
putation on the part of opponents, that we forget
faith, and treat only the moral side of good works .
Were any indeed to do this, I should deem him ut-
terly inexperienced in the ways of God .           But these
things are given out very wrongfully of the faithful
servants of the truth, since these acknowledge that
the tree must first be made good, and then good
fruits be expected from it. "           So also in another
place , he speaks of the            ill effects of constantly
preaching the weakness of our nature, without speak-
ing of Him through whom we may become strong.
" I have often observed," he says, " in many well-

disposed persons, and some have even owned to me,
that it has been a considerable hindrance to them in

their career, that they constantly heard and thought
of this only ; -- how that we were poor weak men,


  ¹ Letzte Theol. Bed . 1. p. 79.              2 Ibid. ii. 333 .
                               P


210


who could not advance to the highest point ; they
consequently became indolent, and did not set deci-
dedly about that which they held it impossible to
attain ; and began to think that they might remain
children of God, although they did not apply them-
selves earnestly to good."        The prevalence of this
state of mind induced Spener to dedicate a year's
course of sermons to show the possibility and the
necessity of active Christianity.
   In 1719, Pfaff again ' laments that the principal
object in Homiletics (which were every where dili-
gently studied) was to explain a biblical text in eccle-
siastical language, according to an artificial form ;
that the whole space was occupied with curious de-
vices and oratorical expressions, to which doctrinal,
elenchtic,    hortatory,   consolatory      inferences   were
added.  Accordingly, if you gain the praise of elo-
quence you have done admirably ; but if you have

filled up an hour with speaking, though what you
say be taken from the postillæ, (the German ho-
milies) you suppose that you have done the duty of
a preacher.     But who sees not, that this is an empty
mode of treating these subjects ?           The text is as-
suredly not to be conformed to the method, (unless
it be a natural one) but the method to the text , and
to the circumstances of the speaker, the hearer, and
the time .     If at least the principal object is to be,
not to obtain the praise of eloquence , not merely to
discharge an ecclesiastical ceremony, but that they
whose hearts have partaken of the divine light and

holiness , should, out of the good treasure of their

   Similar     accounts are given by writers,               whom
their times removed from any influence which pass-
ing events may by some be supposed to have exerted
upon contemporaries .            Not to swell too much the
weary and painful catalogue, I will add the state-
ments of two writers only, men who had no prejudice
against the orthodox school, to the more deserving
of whom they always do full justice.                   The first is
Herder, a man who had a high conception of the
duty of a preacher ' , and who gave his countrymen
many an earnest and well-timed warning.                   " At the
                      2 " the word of God and a
Reformation," he says ",

sound feeling were restored , and our Confessors trod

popular mode of preaching was expelled by the dog-

This account Herder expands considerably in another

if one were to trace the doctrinal preaching down
the course of Christian history,             and    observe the
colour which it took from each age, and the sciences
then fashionable.        Luther e. g . spoke the          simple,
energetic,     unadorned language of a sound mind :
he spoke from the heart and the breast, not from the
head and the memory.       His sermons remained,
therefore, long the model, especially among strong-
minded preachers in our Church.                Chemnitz, Ma-
thesius, Weller and others imitated him.                 In time,
this   style   degenerated      into little     tales,   edifying
city-histories, in brief, into a      town-preacher's style,'
of which we have a multitude of specimens from
the preceding century.            Universities     and    Courts
would fain be distinct ; the former were sunk unhap-
pily in bitter controversies, the latter were infected
with the plague of an evil Spanish                 taste :   both
polemics and a petty taste came accordingly into the
pulpit.    Now were there delivered fearfully dogma-
tic , refuting, thundering sermons, or others all em-
blem and imagery, which again maintained them-
selves for a long time.       Men of finer mind and better
heart attached themselves to mysticism ; until at last

no yoke to be imposed upon him as to the double exordia , the
divisions of the thesis, the applications, and the tone in which he
says Amen. A great portion of the disgust at sermons has been
occasioned by their strange, short-sighted uniformity : an animal
with an emaciated body stretches out two heads one after the
other, displays two or three teeth, and drags after it a five-three-
or twofold tail, which feebly wags . The best form of sermon is
that which text, matter, and circumstances suggest ; it will then
never become wholly uniform, but will yet be conformed both to
itself and the hearers, i . e. be good ."


213


from their remains in our century Pietism arose,
from which again other sects were formed, each of
which would form its own language, for edification
and the pulpit, with great confusion of words, and
even with a peculiar psychology.           Since this deli-
very spoke to the heart, neither the Spanish imagery,
nor the empty tinkling of words , nor the contentious
controversial theology could withstand it.           Philoso-
phy however [the Wolfian] roused herself against it,
and   unquestionably with better fortune for the
human mind, which loves clearness ; until at last she
enveloped the whole of doctrinal and moral theology,
nay sermons and catechisms, in a new Spanish
mantle.    Now Religion belonged to the       best
                                   6
world " ; and from the idea of the   most perfect
                    "
being ' were deduced the connection of all things ,
of sin also, of vice, of the righteousness of Christ,
of repentance, of eternal punishments ; —as was to
be proved.'      Where could       this be better proved
than in the pulpit , where no one contradicted ? and
accordingly even the pulpit (especially since Wolf
had written in German) was overwhelmed with a ter-
minology, which even now has not wholly disap-
peared ; nay, which seems almost to domesticate
itself in our language, although the common man,
even after he has heard it a hundred times, under-
stands it just as little as at first.        In the contro-
versies, at that time, about the philosophical mode of
preaching,    a philosophical sermon was            translated
into good intelligible German : how often had one

occasion for such translations, and compassioned the
multitude who could not make them ! Our last
literary language, which has also found its way into
the pulpit, is, however, not from Wolf's writings,
which are now (1786) no more read , but from France,
England, and Italy, and whence not besides.     The
too numerous translations, (generally done by mere
mechanics, who know not the genius            of the lan-
guage) corrupt it ; too early, hasty, objectless read-
ing corrupts yet more.     I knew one from whose
preaching one could always tell what he had last

read ; another, who doated on Crebillon,            actually
preached à la Crebillon - how ? you will conceive.
When Klopstock became          fashionable,   all    young

                                     "
become acquainted with the arts,'      the arts' also
appeared on the pulpit : now, that young gentlemen
bring biblical expressions into their romances and
moonshine verses, it were ungrateful if the pulpit
were not in time to follow their example, and also
borrow the romance and moonshine tone. Oh Luther,
when one thinks back on thee, and on thy pure, firm,
and intelligible language !"
  This passage from Herder, as well as those which
relate to the " arrangements ,"       refer principally,
though by no means exclusively, to style : yet even
the style, as here described , is an additional evidence
of the defective state of Christian preaching.         I do

not mean to say that this style can be any criterion


215


of individuals who adopted it ; individuals may suffer

in some degree from the faults of their age : but it
is, I think, impossible to conceive that had the
Church been duly impressed with the importance of
that message which they were commissioned to teach,
there could have been such an entire dependance
upon these broken reeds of methods, and these tinsel
ornaments ; that they should have taught, not what
the fulness of their own conviction poured forth,

but what a cramping method dictated ; that they
should have thus set their sails to catch every breath
of applause, bestowed on what popular taste stamped
with the name of eloquence.
  The other writer, whom I will adduce, is Staüdlin .
He says ' of the end of the sixteenth and of the whole
of the seventeenth century : " All theological sciences
breathed a polemical spirit.          This was the case not
in doctrinal and moral theology only, but also in
scriptural interpretation, and hermeneutics, ecclesias-
tical history, the methods of theological study, pas-
toral   theology, the theory of sacred             oratory and
catechising ; nay the sermons and catechisms breathed,
a polemical spirit." " A number of so- called methods
of preaching," says the same author, " were distin-
guished ;    new ones constantly invented ;              contests
took place about them ; various exordia and practical
applications were devised : but in fact, there resulted
only too many and too stiff homiletical rules, while the
matter, the object, and the spirit of sermons , were
too much disregarded ."


            Gesch. d. Theol . Wissensch. Th. 2. S. 1 .


216


  Staüdlin again repeats, and apparently approves,
as also does Knapp ' , the judgment of Spener, that
the system of homiletics was one main hindrance of
good preaching, but says that the efforts of Spener
to alter this system, though they influenced some, who
were not of his school, were opposed by others [ of
the self-called orthodox party] and even regarded
as a heresy.      He also states the fact, that the Wol-

fian philosophy, which brought new evils into theology
and the pulpit, was introduced into the former by the
orthodox party as a stay of their system.          The same
author speaks also of the defects both of the late Spe-
nerian party, and of their opponents ; and describes
these last as " learned , pedantic, despotical, polemical,
in regard to others as well as the followers of Spener ".
It is to be remarked also, that the ultra-orthodox
party itself, as soon as the conflict with the Pietists

commenced, did not regard the charges of a pole-
mical   character, extreme attention to " methods"
of preaching, exclusive         regard to      one portion of
Christian doctrine, as groundless imputations ; but
justified them, and charged their opponents in all
these cases with causeless innovation . The facts were

admitted ;   it remains for posterity only to decide
whether they or their opponents regarded these facts
in their right light.
  I have now produced , though with much regret,
a considerable portion of additional evidence as to
the state of the Lutheran preaching during this long

Tugendspruche, in which they were quoted with

the horrible deficiency of those , from whom the truth

Proofs of these assertions were required.       Francke

motives, and contained nothing but the truth.    " I

confess," he continues, " that the sermons of these
preachers are not what those of ministers of the
Gospel should be.    They are not sufficiently adapted
to edification ; on the contrary, there are many things
in them which rather impede than promote real im-

examination , of the qualities of true saving faith, of
enlightening, sanctification , &c. are either not at all
or but coldly touched on. - They endeavour to refute
old extinct heresies, although this tends not to edifi-
cation   they bring forward even offensive controver-
sies ; as, ' whether women were of the race of men
(Menschen) ?' as did lately M. N. , and employed the
argument,    if they were not, they must be born of
dogs and sows.'     The unedifying and very pernicious
offensive way in which M. S. preaches is known to the
whole town.    What can it tend to edify, if one takes
 his Exordium'a laude Erasmi' and tells the congre-

 gation how many editions there are of his Adagia ?
 Or does it become a minister of the Gospel, on Easter
 day, to deduce at great length from fables, the

of chastity as this same M. S. has done ?"                   ( The

serted in a book intended for youth . ) So again
this preacher says ; " He would now set aside the
example of Christ's humility , and adduce a humble
Emperor."  The tale , in brief, is that an emperor
met a drunken peasant, who told him that his
nose was in his way, on which the emperor put aside
his nose with his hand (which the preacher accom-
panied by action), and bade the peasant proceed .
  Is not this," cried the preacher, " an example of
humility in an emperor ? Had he not power to make
the peasant a head shorter ? yet he did not. Such
examples let us follow."             Other sermons are re-

quantity of Greek, Latin, and Hebrew ' intermingled .
Strabo again is quoted in Latin ; all this serves more
to obtain a reputation for learning among the people,

what aids to a godly life, is not displayed to them, as
it ought : nay, they warn incessantly against the Per-

   Yet these men, although contrary to the royal
commission seven years before ( 1692) , still continued
incessantly to warn their congregations        by name
against Francke and Pietism and Perfectism (far re-

been from an advanced state of religion ) and one

226


of them told his people that the way to Francke's
church was the way to Hell.
   It has now been shewn, as far as the space admit-
ted, that the defects charged upon the predominant
character of the Lutheran theology were not exag-

gerated ; that the authorities were not misquoted ;
that the evidence, where it was regarded as evidence,
bore out what was alleged.            Additional secondary
evidence has also been produced , from persons bound
together by no common tie, of various characters,
united by no interest to exaggerate these defects,
and of characters incapable of being swayed by such
temptation.      Would that it had been otherwise ;
there is nothing which a Christian would not sacri-
fice for the conviction that the Christian doctrines had

at all times been uncorrupted, in their scientific cul-
tivation, as in their practical employment ; that they
had never, even apparently, lost their savour.   Pain-
ful, however, as the consideration of any period must
be, in which they do appear to have been misapplied,
or neglected, or corrupted, our ignorance, which is
the cause probably of much of our pain, may be also
our   comfort.      Although Christianity may appear
not to have produced in very many the effects,
which one would have anticipated from it, it may
have (and God forbid that we should not think it
had) produced effects proportionate to the form in
which it existed ' ; it may at all times have prepared

    " Even in the darkest times, " says Herder of the times of
Romanism , " there were pious Christians, who perhaps with less
knowledge, as the honest straight-forward Barbarians which they
were, did more good than the highly enlightened Christians of


227


those very men, in whom it appears in its coldest
form , for that state, in which they may continually ad-
vance unimpeded by those hindrances with which they
were here beset.        God only knows, whether amid
all which appears unlovely, all which looks like cold-
ness, they may not have loved Him more sincerely,
more warmly than we.
   I must again observe that I have been forced,
even more than before, to dwell upon the gloomy
side of the picture ; yet it must be recollected to our
comfort, that it was in this period that Andrea and
the other valuable men who were united with or
followed him , lived and acted ; that it was in this
century that the indefatigable Spener, and some of
the best of his adherents, were raised up for the
reformation of the Church ; and that although they
could not change the character which that Church had
assumed, still they and others, whom history cannot
record, were in their more limited sphere the means
of bringing the doctrines of Christianity, in their
most influential form , to the hearts of their hearers.

other times. They stand not in the catalogues of the " Witnesses
of the Truth ; " among these were admitted those only who excited
attention ; and the greatest portion of upright Christians at all
times live tranquilly active and concealed . Even the grossest
Popery might effect much good." Br. üb. d. Theol. B. 2. S. 201.

THE brief outline of Lutheran theology in my En-

quiry, was confined almost         entirely to the public
exertions of the clergy. These I deemed in general
mis-directed, in consequence of the unfortunate at-
tention which that clergy was induced to devote to
minor points of controversy and to modes of expres-
sion, joined to the mis-direction of general, as well
as of theological education, and the purely intellec-
tual conception of Christianity, which in consequence
prevailed.    On the supposition that the whole evil


229


of each portion of this period was attributed to the
several controversies, which in succession distracted
the Church, it has been urged, that it " is unreason-
able to suppose the same spirit existed to any inju-
rious degree, in the numberless ministers who were
withdrawn from all the excitements, which cannot .
but exist, when large bodies of men are congregated,
and which tend to keep up all the peculiarities and
distinctive features of parties and systems ' :" and
the account has been represented not only as untrue,
but in the highest degree uncharitable.      I am com-
pelled, therefore, to enter upon a subject, upon which

I should otherwise have willingly avoided detail , the
ill state of the German church in the 17th century.

It is a painful thought, that the effects produced by
Christianity should at any time have apparently
borne so little proportion to the stupendous means em-
ployed in it for the renovation of our nature ; that any
portion of the spiritual vineyard should appear to
have yielded so small a return for the bounty of its
Lord    it is yet more painful that so many of His
labourers should appear to have been so little bent
upon executing their office, or so much to have mis-
taken its nature.    Yet on this, as on so many sub-

jects connected with the state of the Christian world ,
the precept " judge not" provides             a consolation,
while it prescribes a duty. We know little or nothing
of any heart ; next to, or probably less than nothing
of the real life of any individual but ourselves.       The
description of our Saviour has held true of every


                    1 Letter, p . 114, &c .


230


time, that the broad and evil road is that which is

trod by the multitude ; enough for us if we are enabled
not to shape our course by theirs , if we follow them not
to do evil : how many of these may inwardly be long-
ing for a return to their Father's house ; how many
may be gradually turning back to the strait and diffi-
cult path ; how many of them may, after all their
wanderings, strike back to it, can be known to the
Searcher of    hearts   alone.   While   the   Christian
teacher is bound , as he values his own salvation , not
to call evil good, nor to widen that path which his
Master has taught him is narrow and laborious, he
may still, and he ought to hope that myriads have
indeed found it, of whom men, who can judge by
the outward show only, would think unfavourably.
   There is then no want of charity in         recording
these appearances ;     there need be no source of
deeper pain beyond what must result from the con-
templation of these calamities in any portion of our
common body ; and although I would rather draw
a veil around these objects than bring them forward
to light, the instruction which they give is too im-
portant, the knowledge of these times is too much
connected with the events which followed, to allow
me to follow my own wishes.      The account will also
make us acquainted with some admirable men, who,
although apparently unable to produce any general
change in the evil times in which they lived, yet
shone like lights in the world, who must in a nar-
rower sphere have been the instruments of extensive
blessings, and who out of their graves yet speak .
  One of the earliest of these was the immortal J.
                             8


231


V. Andrea ' , a man who, to use Herder's language,
"bloomed like a rose among thorns ;" who was ill
appreciated by most of his own age, though valued by
the better and congenial spirits ; who was forgotten
amid the coldness of subsequent times, until after a cen-
tury of general oblivion, he was in some measure re-
called to life by Herder ; in some measure only, for
Herder's age was not yet ripe nor earnest enough to
receive him :-a man, of whom Spener says, " I for my
part esteem his writings so highly, that if I could and
might recall from the grave one man for the reforma-
tion of our church, it would require much thought
to choose one in preference to him "."           Yet his exer-
tions as a writer were but a small portion of his
stupendous activity : these fell principally in a period
before the 30 years' war ( 1614-1620) , during
which the vain attempt to correct the prevailing cor-
ruption of manners, the divisions amongthe citizens of
his cure, and the calumnies with which he was assailed

both near and far, " made him," he says, " old and his

head grey before his time ¹."     He was afterwards re-
moved to a place (Calw) where his labours could find
more room.      Here, during the horrors of the 30
years' war, (of which this place had its full dreadful
share,) he devoted, notwithstanding the weakness of
his health and bodily frame, an almost incredible acti-
vity to the mitigation of these heavy inflictions ; he
rescued by his provident institutions that city from
famine ; relieved the flocks of destitute beings who
were driven homeless through it ; was the animating
soul of all exertions amid the general despondency ;
opposed with some effect the tide of increasing cor-
ruption ; restored discipline in some degree ; con-
soled alone thousands of the sick and dying ; pro-

vided for the religious education of the rising and
orphan children.    His strength for all this, and more,
of which no general description can give the faintest
conception, he traced to that source from which
alone such strength could have been derived, a firm
faith in his God and Saviour, to which human hopes
or human fears are as nothing ; -that pure practical
faith, which it was the one great end of his existence
to promote .
  From this fair picture we must turn, however, to
the account which this able and conscientious mi-

nister gives of the state of the Lutheran Church.
He was well qualified to judge of every thing which
related to it ; for his intellectual endowments were
of a high order, his mind comprehensive and pene-
trating, and he had cultivated it with no ordinary

               1 Hossbach's Andrea, S. 20.


233


care .   He had " studied and mastered almost all
the sciences, which could be studied, and which con-
tribute to form the mind ' .          He was devotedly at-
tached to the church in which he was educated, and
especially to its great founder, Luther, whose heroic
faith and courage he almost regarded as a miracle ' ;
he belonged to the stricter part of the Church, in-
heriting from his grandfather his attachment to the
Formula Concordiæ ; he had travelled much, had
seen other churches also, and much as he admired
the discipline of Geneva, he thought the line of separ-
ation between the two portions of the Protestant
Church, far too decided to be overstepped .             Though
he avoided the controversies of his times, his lan-
guage with regard to the Reformed is even severe 3.
In the combination then of his high natural endow-
ments with his ardent piety and great practical judg-
ment, he possessed every qualification which could
enable him to appreciate soundly the state of his
Church ; his love for her would prevent him from
judging uncharitably.  Yet, although he throughout
distinguishes between the doctrines of the Lutheran
Church and the mode in which her ministers taught
them, there is not a single point in her practice over
which Andrea does not mourn ; -most frequently in a
tone of deep earnestness, at other times, since this found

controversies of the preachers, and the general cor-

   The materials are so copious that it is difficult to

as is natural, frequently occur intermingled with
each other ; the style (much being in dialogue) is
often so peculiar, that the effect must be considerably
weakened by an extract of those parts only which
relate most directly to our subject, deprived of the
introduction by which they appear in keeping.

5. " Apostasia." " B. Are not these spots, that whole lands, and
so many of the Reformed , deny Christianity ? A. That has the
whole world long done, and will continue to do,-unless you
think that Christ is less offended by impiety than by error. B.
Amongst us also ? A. Certainly ; for to pretend that we are the
purest of all in the Christian confession, and are precisely con-

any, what is it but to mock Christ ? B. Man will never be without
defects. A. Never in truth : yet do but think with yourself,
what blasphemies, luxury, impurity, vanities, thefts, injustice,
voluptuousness, impostures, are publicly tolerated , or almost ap-
proved among us. Since all these things have nothing in com-
mon with Christ, what matters it what religion we boast of?"

nisters are still introduced or cast out at will ; simony

formed upon it, and the beauty of literature was restor-

foes, superstition, dissoluteness, barbarism ; but the
secret snares of the devil lay us low, so that our joy
is little founded, and for the most part the name is
left without the substance. Whereas our whole
life should be formed after the example of Christ,
whose name we bear and whom we confess , it has
come to pass through our horrible negligence, that
Christians are in nothing distinguished from the
children of the world : whence we may easily

infer both the delight of Satan (who, when he has
most ingeniously spoiled us of our goods, allows us
very readily to boast in the husks and the shell,)

life so utterly corrupt ?        No other gives more up-
right instruction, and is more crooked in its practice ;
none is more unblameable in its institutions, or more
criminal by its transgressions of them ; none fairer
in form, or more wrinkled through so many contor-
tions ; none more adorned by laws, or more pol-
luted by evil practices ; none more confined by rules,
or more unconfined through neglect ; on one side
ingeniously constructed and praiseworthy, on the
other wholly dissolved and of ill report ; intelligent

in its confessions, in its teaching senseless. To
conform to her in belief is thought pleasing and
wholesome ;      to conform to her in life is deemed
dangerous, gloomy, and oppressive to the conscience.


has indeed very many adherents of his heavenly kingdom , but few
imitators of his life ; many who aim at his honour, few who bear
his cross ; many desirous of his consolation, few who share his
troubles ; many who share his table, few his abstinence. All wish
to rejoice with Christ, only few endure to suffer with Him ; many
follow Him to the breaking of bread, but few only to the drink-
ing of his cup." Add Menipp. 68. Utopia. 35. Waldenses.
On the false Gospel which prevailed , see Mythol. Christian . v.
14. Evangelium. 24. Lutherus. on the abuse of the sacraments,
ib. v. 16. Eucharistia. on the neglect of the imitation of Christ's
example, ib. iii. 21. Icones. Menipp . 100. Jesus ; and many
others. It was from this inconsistency of life and doctrine, this
negligence about the practical application of Christian truth,
however rigidly the system was for the present abstractedly held,
that Andrea anticipates the convulsion which at last seizes
the German Church. " Happy concord" (of life and doctrine)
he says, in contrast to the only dread of his contemporaries,
namely, that other Christian parties might prevail: " Most happy,
and unless this be, I fear no other religion, but the absence of
all." Menipp. " Apostasia. "


250


The principal blame of all this appears to me to lie
in the unreal imaginary faith which no where pro-
duces fruit,-not that faith which gives life , which
supported upon Christ's merits, justifies and saves
without works ;         and in the profaned use of the
sacraments."

      It may be permitted to insert, in illustration, some
apologues, which, although the style is very different
from the preceding, breathe the same earnestness.
The first is a contrast of the controversialists of his
day with the genuine heroes of the Reformation and
of former times.          The Christian Religion, he re-
lates ' , heard day by day of the threatening and
formidable incursions of its enemies ; she deter-

mined accordingly to muster her citizens, and re-
view their weapons .           She found, beyond all            con-
ception, every thing prepared.              There was namely
a vast host of armed men, strong threatening forms,
weapons, which they exercised admirably, bright and
flashing from afar. But as she came nearer, she
sunk almost in a swoon.  What she had thought

iron and steel were toys ; the swords were made of
the dull lead of words ; the breast-plate of the soft
linen of pleasure ; the helmet, of the wax of plumed
vanity ; the shield, of papyrus scrolled over with opi-
nions ; the spears, thin reeds of weak conjecture ; the
colors, spiders '-web of philosophical             systems ;     the
cannon, Indian reed ; the powder, poppy-seed ; the
balls, of glass.       Through the indolent neglect of

  1
    Mythol. Christian. I. 13. Christiana religio.      Also in Herder

their leaders, they had sold her true weapons and
had introduced these ;        nay, they even made her

strength were proved, contemptible.    Bitterly did
Religion weep, but the whole assembly bid her " be
of good cheer, they would shew their faith to the

faith,   since your actions are worthless ?            I swear
to you, when I aforetime led naked unarmed com-
batants into the field , one martyr, one warrior faith-
ful to death, was worth more to me than a hundred
 ofyou in your gilded or silvered panoplies."

   The same great object of substituting practical
piety, Christian charity and simplicity for the mere
intellectual contest for superiority, appears in two
other writings on the same subject.                The one is
entitled The Disputation, the other Polemics .                 In
the first   " Belial, the prince of sophists, having been
defeated in his contest with Christ, is set upon de-
vising modes of making a compact or a truce.     He

betakes himself to an University.          There he puts up
certain theses on this matter, upon which he proposed
to dispute.      On the day appointed he stood forward
to defend them. There appeared , Metaphysic, Logic,
Rhetoric, and all the rest of the lovers of erudition.
Vehement was their conflict with Belial .               Sharply
did they strive ; but he was too slippery : armed at
all points with logical distinctions, and voluble of
speech, he was never less silent than when they

dumb.        He seized his papers indignantly, and was

  I thought to have only to do with fictitious per-

The second ' is in the form of a dialogue, and relates
to the practical clergy.         " A. What a host of books !
B. Would there were also of purchasers !              A. The
fault is yours, who inundate us with such a mass of
trifles.   B. Tastes are different : one must serve
all .   A.    But I can find nothing for my object.
B. It must be a profane one then.            See here ! Medi-
tations, prayers, Self-communion.              A. Let the su-
perstitious, the enthusiast, and women take delight in
them.        B. Here is a summary of Christianity . A. Of
that toilsome one, under whose rules no one will live?
away with it.         B. Will you then have a Heraclitus
or a Democritus ?          A. To weep over the latter, or
laugh at the former ?          B. What will you then ?    A.
First some suit of theological armour, by which I
may fall upon and overcome all opponents at once.
B. Would you have a paper armour, look through
my polemical books : would you have a real one, you
can become invincible by piety alone . A. You mean
to startle me. I seek a wordy victory.   B. Therein
will Satan never yield .        But do you wish for aught

                      Menipp. Dial. 3. Polemica.

besides ?   A. A quite perfect " Guide to Preaching."
B. That the Holy Spirit will give, if you ask him
devoutly. A. Am I to wait for an inspiration ?
B. Not indeed a fanatical one, but yet an indwelling
of the Holy Spirit ; but memory with you stands in

place of the Spirit.      A. How so ?       B. Because you
maintain that you have only so much divine commis-
sion to men as you have committed to memory.
A. Quite right ; for the writers whom I follow were
ruled by the Spirit ' .   B. Why then do you not your-
self obtain by prayer something of the same Spirit ?
A. I am not yet arrived at this degree of their gifts.
B. You are then only a relator ?              A. Say rather,
an imitator.     B. I would rather you were a reader,
that would be safer and honester.              A. That could
any one do, and for that, no sciences were necessary
and no expence.        B. It had been well that with so
much expenditure you had become fitter for the care
of souls, or at least that you confessed that in holy
things you were just as ignorant as we.      A. You
would not surely call the solution of sophistical argu-
ments, and the praiseworthy imitation of our teachers
an expensive ignorance ?   B. That I would scarcely
pronounce ; but this is very strange, that you refute

all by your words, and invite none by your actions,
that you read forth all that is holy according to a
prescribed rule, but are wholly without union with
God, in whose name you proclaim it .”

                                                     66
  ' Theologiæ Encomium, ap. Hossb. S. 271. fg.          Happy,
happy Church," he says in a tone of the deepest feeling, in his

" Happy, happy Church, whose ministers are not destined to it

demned to it for their dulness of understanding, nor admitted to
it for their practice in petty learning, nor obtruded on it by the

may make a trial what they can do either upon or against
men's souls ! O happy, happy Church, whose ministers seek
their honour in the word of God, wealth in the increase of the
Church, learning in the avoiding of Satan, pleasure in subduing
the flesh, reputation in the testimony of the poor, their end in the
wreath of faith ! O happy, happy Church, where God calls, man
obeys, angels assist, magistrates agree, the people hear, and a
kindred youth grows up ! But woe to those who have deformed
and transformed into a certain facility and negligence of calling,
the anxious care and resolute courage of their ancestors, who,
being called by God, and fearing for their own souls, withdrew

I am to persuade the people that there is no danger

but appears in a different garb, holds a book in his
hands, though he be still a blasphemer, perjured ,

administer the sacraments, to explain the word of
God ? Miserable beings ! think, that what you do, you
are doing to yourselves ; whether ye have done well
will soon appear : God is the righteous, who allows
of nothing spurious ; the Omniscient, whom nothing
can deceive ; the Almighty, whom you cannot escape."

accurate acquaintance with the holy Scriptures, and
especially with the New Testament, not so much to
convince us ofthe unspeakable self-agreeing truth,
as to bear along the heart to accordance with it,
and in obedience to it, to subdue the reason, soften
the passions, and draw us wholly from the present
to the future.     If the holy volume does not effect
this, it may fill our memory, practise our language,

swell pompously around our ears, but never will it
raise us from the defilements of the earth ; and the
error will be the more pernicious, since the show
of piety shamefully deceives the teacher as well as
the learner.  What can be more unworthy, what
a more open deceit, than when nothing of what
we would convince others touches or convinces
ourselves, and when what we enjoin them is as
far removed from ourselves as one end of hea-
ven from the other ? -Any one can talk about
religion, but to subject ourselves to it, to enter
into it, be zealous for it, wholly to belong to it, be
satisfied with it, that,     believe   me, scarce one in
thousands understands.         Can we wonder then that

in this spurious      intermixture, in which we live,
amid this discordance of faith and practice, we
persuade fewer than we wish, since it is evident
that we are not ourselves convinced ? that we

accomplish less than we desire, since every one

     The frequent recurrence of this subject in Andrea's writings
shews both the extent of the evil and Andrea's practical sense of
the truth, that a Church , in which the word of God is taught,
(however imperfect its organs, or positively corrupt ,) and the
sacraments duly ministered, must not be deserted; comp. the De
curiositatis pernicie, especially on Weigelianism (ap. Hossb. 56-
62. ) Menip. Dial . 12. Fraternitas. Theophilus (ap. Hossb. 118. )
The subject often recurs in the Mythol. Christian, see e. g . 1 , 42.

cism, formed of a mixture of new-Platonism and
Cabbalism, in which those of deeper feeling in Italy
had plunged, to supply the void left by the unsatis-
fying pageantry of Romanism, had been recast by
Agrippa and Paracelsus : in this form it took ex-
tensive possession of the German mind , whose na-
turally deep tone had been neglected or jarred amid
the controversies of theologians and the reign of
scholastic theology ' .       With this were mingled in
Andrea's time, the wild dreams, and not unfrequently
the hypocrisy of the Rosecrucians.            Every deviation
which led from Christ, as the centre of religion , was
opposed by this faithful and indefatigable servant of
God ; and his efforts were ultimately successful in
repressing this form at least of the evil.             In every
corrupted state of religion , however, with the classes
of Pharisees and Sadducees, that of the Essenes also
will probably be found ; and it is by genuine Chris-
tianity alone that these aberrations are to be checked .

   It is not to be doubted that this extraordinary,
highly gifted man benefited greatly those with whose
care he was immediately charged ; he probably also
exerted, by means of his writings, an imperceptible
influence over many portions of the German Church ;
it is even said that the superior state of the clergy of
Würtemburg at present, as a body, may be traced
to him ; but it appears, I think, from the above, and
still more from the subsequent state of the Church,
as described by Spener, that he was not able to pro-
duce any material alteration in the German Church.
He aided perhaps to prepare the ground for the cul-
tivation by Spener, but it was then ungrateful to the
labourer, and he witnessed           not the growth of the
tree which he watered,          and under whose shadow
those of a later age in part took refuge.

In the year 1675 the Pia Desideria of Spener

   One of the earliest in the century was Tarnov,
whom I mentioned ' as an exception to the commen-
tators of his age, and as having been depreciated by
his contemporaries. He was Professor at Rostock,
which seems to have been at this time the centre of
the attempts to reform the Lutheran Church.                        In
his admonition on the ministry2 ( 1616) he gives the
following description of the general condition of the
clergy.     " Were any one to say now with Paul,
' All seek their own, and not the things of Jesus
Christ ;' this indeed is more than too true ; and yet
he would be forthwith censured as a falsifier and
calumniator .      Let us see, however, whether ordinary
life confirm or refute it.        Whom then does one now
find, who, if any office in the church be offered him, is
not chiefly concerned about its income, and directs his
diligence accordingly ?              Were they deprived of

true and persevering believers belong to them also,
although they have no inward but only an outward

"many ' zealous theologians had vehemently called for

indeed through God's mercy, the doctrine of the Gos-

   The perverted character of the learning of this
day, and its ill effects in chilling practical Chris-
tianity, which Andrea noticed, is mentioned also at
the beginning of this century by Rittershuis ”. “ When
one thinks upon the present prevailing wickedness
and frivolity (which has reached the highest pitch
and cannot rise higher) , it is a natural source of
wonder why, notwithstanding the multitude of ser-
mons, no improvement takes place.                  Many how-
ever candidly confess, that the learned in our times
have more science than those of old, but the less con-
science.    This then is the single andgreat cause ;
that those     formerly taught         more through their
lives than through their words, and so found more
credence with their hearers ; hence it is then that

earnestness of mind is banished among all, that faith
is sunk, piety is gone, so that we have scarcely a
shadow of it left , if indeed we shall retain even this."

  Both the works of J. Saubert (the one in 1633 ,
the other in 1636,) bear witness to the same state
of the Lutheran church ; the one was the " book of
discipline of the evangelical church," in which, says

           6
the other,   Medicine of the Soul for Lutherans and
Papists, i. e. a solid and scriptural discussion of the
question, if we would bring over the Papists to our
evangelical religion, whether our disputing or rather
the improvement of our lives be most necessary ?'
Of him Andrea, who was his intimate friend, and
wrote the history of his life, says, in his energetic
style, that " he was obliged to undertake almost
more combats than Hercules,             in maintaining the
cause of Christ against the Christians, the Gospel
against the Gospellers, the Church against those
who presided over it, discipline against the judges
of discipline, piety against the pious, learning against
the learned, i . e. against those who would needs
falsely be looked upon as such in all its branches :
were one but to touch but lightly on what took
place in consequence, it could not be without excit-

   To the same Saubert, Evenius, the Rector of dif-
ferent schools, whom Andrea praises as " ingenuum
larvæ scholasticæ detractorem," dedicated in 1640

treated in distinct chapters, 1st, of the recklessness
and ungodliness of the old ; 2nd , of the young ; 3rd, the
negligent superintendence of domestic life ; 4th, the
neglect of teachers of schools ; 5th, the negligence of
ministers ; and 6th, of the evil magistracy.            " He.
died," says Andrea, " of a broken heart at the use-
lessness of his exertions '."

   Comenius, who derived             many of his best views
on the improvement of education from Andrea, held,
what was afterwards considered a Pietistic error,
that Anti-christ was to be found among the Protes-

applies the descriptions of him to the titles and actions
of many, one finds that there is no absence, not only of
lesser but of greater Antichrists .       Such, namely, as
exalt themselves as demigods in the temple of God, -
allow themselves and their decreta and their writings
to be worshipped, and seek an absolute dominion.
If, moreover, it is denied them, they turn things
upside down.       So that an able prince said well,
' we have no want of Popes except at Rome.'           And
is this bidding farewell to Antichrist, when we drive
from us the spirit of Christ, the spirit of mildness,
of modesty, humility, brotherly love, and such like,
through which one is not puffed up, nor greedy

   Grosgebauer again, a theologian of Rostock, is

are to exhort the unrepentant, and yet know not the
state of mind of a poor repentant sinner ; -are to

hitherto reckless academici, and never have them-

   The pernicious effects of the perversion of the
doctrine, that works were not necessary to salvation,
(mentioned by Andrea) a perversion which originated
in the earlier controversies, is much insisted upon by
             6
Stegmann on true Christianity 2.        " Luther cast
aside the merits of works, and brought again to light
justification by faith ; and forthwith people fell into
the opposite error of a fleshly security.             Luther,
casting aside human inventions , pointed out the way
to genuine holiness ; and forthwith people turned
to the other side of an indolent negligence .          They ³,
however, who boast loudly of the Gospel should
learn, that we are not freed from honouring God,
from aiding the poor, from fasting, from devotion of
the body, from purity in married life, from the prac-
tice of virtue ; but only from the tyranny of Popery ;
those who do otherwise should know that they have
escaped from the thongs of Antichrist, but are fallen
instead into the fetters of the devil.         Thus has the
