  The celebrated censure of H. Müller of Rostock,
that " the Christians of these days have four dumb
church-idols, which they follow, the font, the pulpit,
the chair of absolution, and the altar,"has been already
mentioned 2.    The dumbness must relate to the want

66
   They comfort themselves," he says, " in their out-
ward Christianity, that they are baptized, hear the
word of God, go to confess, receive the Lord's Sup-
per ; but the inward power of Christianity they deny.

 ' Seelen-Schatz, P. 4. p. 1187. ap. Arnold , S. 461 .
 2 Enquiry, p. 49. It became the subject of controversy.


285


They deny the power of baptism, since they walk
not in the new but in the old man, whereas baptism
is a washing of regeneration              and of renovation .
They deny the power of the divine word , since they
walk not according to that word, but refute the word
of God with their ungodly life, and make it a lie.
They deny the power of absolution, since they re-
main unaltered after as before ; whereas the heart,
when it is refreshed with the consolation of the divine

absolution, cannot any more love the evil and hate
the good.     They deny the power of the holy com-
munion, since they do not live in Christ, with whom
they are united, but walk according to the lusts of
their flesh, and abandon themselves to all sin. What
concord has Christ with Belial ?           All this is idolatry.
For God is a spirit, and wills that we worship him in
spirit and in truth ."

  " Müller," says Spener, " whose practical writings
have for some time been the best in our Church, who
was himself a professor of theology, and spoke from
experience, lamented the faults of the Universities
more than I may, and ascribes the greatest portion of
the blame not to the domestic education , but to the
Professors themselves.         How many pious persons, who
have spent some time at Universities, could not suffi-
ciently lament the life which they must see among the

students of theology, and think the Professors greatly
to blame.      Not Müller alone, but other professors of
theology have acknowledged the same to me in writ-
ing ; though indeed it is manifest              as the day '."

   The exceptions, which undoubtedly existed      at all
times, were without doubt augmented very materially
through the success with which the exertions of
Spener were blessed .    With the increase of good,
however, evil also always probably increases, and
there was much in the state of things at that time,
which would give occasion to such increase.           The
principal source   of these additional    evils lay     in
the nature of the controversies with Spener and
his school. The fanatical and separatistic spirit

which Andrea had successfully opposed under the
forms of Rosæ- crucianism and Weigelianism, still
continued (the original causes not being       abated)
under different shapes ; the disposition to it had been
augmented by the miseries of the thirty years' war.
This indulgence of the feeling, or rather, as was ge-
nerally the case, of the mere sensations, to the exclu-


289


sion of the intellect, prevailed in various degrees.
In most it manifested itself in the usual forms of a
neglect of the external means of grace, and of a
proud separatism : by others the results of an ecstatic
and ungoverned imagination were regarded as espe-
cial revelations   in some few, as in earlier fanatics,
the fancied abstraction from the body ended in (per-
haps was sometimes the cloke for) grovelling impu-
rities . In these sects there abounded, as was natural,

rash prophecies with regard both to the church and
state, sometimes the result of supposed revelations ,
more frequently of perversions of prophetic Scrip-
ture -an indiscriminate denunciation , not of the
abuses only of the Lutheran church, but of the in-
stitution itself ; -and fanatical expectations of a visible
millenniary kingdom of Christ, which was soon to
appear.   Among the milder of these fanatics , were
unhappily some even of the ministers of the Lutheran
Church.    It need scarcely be stated that with all these,
Spener and his genuine school had no connection,
except that they also mourned over the actual con-
dition of the Church, that they saw the necessity of
a reformation within the Church, which should give
it renewed life and strength, while they felt and
shewed the mischiefs of one, which, as in the fables
of old,   was first to separate limb from limb the
tenement of life , and then by some magic pro-
cess form the dissevered members into a frame of

more youthful vigor.     Yet although one must fear
that nothing but a wilful confusion , or the most de-
termined blindness, or at best a very culpable igno-
rance, could have caused any to blend Spener's mild
                          U


290


and practical remonstrances or proposals for reform-
ation with those of the separatistic fanatics,―his legal
and moderate plans for making the Lutheran ministry
more effective, with those of men who despised the
ministry of the Church, and slighted the ordinances
of God, -or his fears, founded upon the present aspect

of things, that some severe chastisements were com-
ing upon the Church (as they did come) and his
hopes (in which every Christian in troubled times
                                                .
must find his support) that they might produce some
further developement of Christ's kingdom among
them, with the dreams of the fleshly expounders of
Scripture -all this was done.            The blending of all
persons together, who censured the existing state of
things , was part of the regular system of warfare
against the Pietists ; and Spener and the genuine
promoters of piety were represented to the public as
having the same opinions and objects with Böhme or
Dippel or Petersen or Rosenbach ' .            The classifica-

although Schelwig had been previously mildly cautioned by

author of the Unfug d. Pietisten (whether Carpzoff or some

Halle, but Sagittarius and others were attacked by name (Letzte
Bed . iii. 550. ) and they were mixed up with all the excesses of
fanatics, and especially with Achilles and the well-known Janin.
This work , which Spener rightly termed a detestable libel, was
pronounced by Schelwig to give a faithful image of Pietism as a
real sect : the author he calls " Theologum prudentissimum et de
orthodoxia meritissimum : " its facts were approved by Löscher,
although he disapproved of the contemptuous mockery with which
Spener was spoken of (Walch i. 696. ) . Again, in the Pietistic
Catechism, 1708 , which was reprinted and enlarged 1709 , and
again 1720 , Spener stands in the same class, not with Arnold
only, but with Böhme, Dippel, Rosenbach, and Petersen (Walch
v. 756.). A specimen of its style is given by Walch. " What
is the 1st Pietistic commandment ? A. Thou shalt have other
gods besides the true God . Q. What are these gods which one
is to have besides God ? A. The devils, which the Pietists will
have to be worshipped. " And yet this was reprinted , at intervals,
more than once. Again, the author of the Idea Pietismi, 1712 ,
a learned writer (according to Walch i. 939. ) quotes indiscrimi-
nately among the books of the genuine Pietists those of Weigel,
Böhme, Stiefel, and Dippel. It is unnecessary further to swell
this hateful catalogue, as I could too easily do ; nor is it necessary
to observe either on the state of Christian feeling which it implies,

292


original deception was still retained, of including all
under one generic name, was rather a stroke of policy,
than the effect of candour or of conscience.          It pal-
liated, in some degree , the grossness of the impu-
tation, but thereby only realised more effectually the
original object of depreciating the genuine Pietists.
Few would retain the distinctions of Pietists or Hyper-

pietists (of which the latter were " freer and more
shameless, the other more concealed ," but as is im-
plied , in good understanding with them ) ; or again,
of " the grosser, the more refined, and the Crypto-
Pietists ; " or of the " gross boorish Pietists, the more,
and the very refined " (which last were described as
wolves in sheep's clothing) ;        or again, when the
Pietists were divided with reference to their supposed
motives, (as in Schelwig's " lepida divisio,") and it was
allowed that " among those who were actuated by
pride, love of distinction , (all other means of such dis-
tinction failing) , temporal interest, or necessity, some
might have become Pietists out of ignorant simplicity
or melancholy" -few would retain such distinctions,
or would probably be less misled , if they did.
   The confusion introduced by the theologians , was

propagated in the public edicts ; the title of Pietists,
under which the party enlisted against Spener had
succeeded in comprehending all who were opposed
to them , was     unhappily     employed in regulations
intended    only to check a fanatic         or separatistic
spirit, contrary, wherever it existed, to the being of

or upon the mischiefs which this confusion must have caused to
the ordinary mind.

known to most in other parts of Germany, whereas the edict was
widely circulated . It was employed to prejudice the Crown Prince
of Prussia (afterwards King William I. ) against the University of
Halle ; some exceptions are mentioned by Francke in his defence
(Schreiben, &c. in Francke'ns Stiftungen, B. 2. S. 109.). The
Hanoverian edict of 1733 , which repeats in strong terms the rest
of the preceding, only prescribes that no one who had studied at
Halle should be admitted into office without its being ascertained
that he was wholly free from Pietistic errors . (Walch. v. 541.) .
The edict of Saxe Eisenach unites the " so-called sectarian Pietism
which departs from the doctrine of the evangelical church," with


294


avoid the name altogether ; others, when they de-
scribed the evil under the title of the " so-called


" the pernicious Separatism. " (Walch i. 958. ) In Walden ( 1716 )
it was forbidden that " so- called Pietistic persons should be har-
boured, or their books brought into the country," (ib. S. 974. ) and
in that of Hanau in the same year, " the so - called Pietists of the
present day" are expressly classed with the Fanatics, from whom
all subjects were directed to detach themselves. (ib . ) In that of
Deux-Ponts ( 1734) in which former edicts are referred to, the so-
called Pietism and Separatism are classed together. (id. v. 552.)
The most unfortunate in its effects, however, appears to have been
that of Hanover, which was enacted in 1703, and renewed in
1710 , 11 , 13 , 33, & 34. The edict itself spoke of certain per-
sons who were found here and there in the Electorate, who held
the so-called Pietistic and other modern errors. The principal
evil arose, however, from additions made by the Ecclesiastical
Council of the Electorate, which appended to this edict " the
true criteria of the Pietists of these days. " The tenets here enu-
merated belonged very probably to many who were called Pietists ;
but it was in the worst feeling of party spirit that the whole was
repeatedly applied by the pseudo-orthodox to Spener and his
school. It may suffice to name them . 1. That they rejected
magisterial regulations in ecclesiastical matters . 2. Reviled
the ministry. 3. Regarded public worship as useless. 4. Would
not receive the Communion with others. 5. Slighted baptism.
6. Censured the Confession and Absolution.      7. Boasted of
having attained perfection.  8. Claimed internal revelations.
9. Regarded all who opposed them as unregenerate. 10. Held
secret meetings and misled women . 11. Defended Chiliasm.
12. Regarded laughing and dancing as sins. 13. Insisted upon
good works instead of faith. 14. Derived the power of the word
and sacraments from the holiness of the preacher. 15. Con-
sidered labor as unbefitting the regenerate. 16. Denied the
eternity of future punishments. Some of these, as e. g. the 13th,
are evidently perversions of what Spener and the school of Halle
did in a right sense dwell upon ; others are such as were held by
individuals of the fanatics ; the combination contributed to give
                                3


295


Pietism," wished apparently still to distinguish it
decisively from genuine piety : yet, though Spener
was never named, nor probably even directly alluded
to, no sufficient line was drawn between those who
were wrongly branded with this name, and those who
would have been better described as hypocrites or
fanatics.  Perplexity would necessarily arise from
the recital of these edicts from the pulpit ; they
were reprinted and collected by the self-styled ortho-
dox, and even those, in which no mention was made
of the Pietists, and which were directed against
the most declared fanatics, were employed as wea-
pons in the warfare against the school of Spener, or
even against himself ' .      No small portion, probably,


some color to the attacks upon the restorers of practical Chris-
tianity. Besides these , there were numerous decrees more ex-
pressly directed against Fanatics, as Brandenburg 1700 , Qued-
linburg, Hesse Cassel 1702, Bremen 1705, Würtemburg 1706,
Eslingen, Anhalt- Zerbst 1719, Brunswick- Luneburg, Holstein,
Waldeck, Halberstadt 1711.       The degree of the distraction of
the German church cannot, however, be fairly estimated by the
mere number of edicts, without more fully ascertaining, than
there are yet the means of doing, the nature of the circum-
stances which occasioned them . The edict of Walden above
mentioned was, upon examination of the case, recalled ; and
" furnishes," observes the historian , " a clear instance how such
edicts may often have been produced by unfounded statements,"
(Walch v. 268-70 . ) not now to mention cases in which previous
examination produced acquittal.
     The fullest collection of this sort was that by Neumeister (a
Lutheran minister) " pietismus a magistratu politico reprobatus et
proscriptus, or Pietism and what is allied to it, rejected and ba-
nished by the civil magistrate in and out of Germany, shown both
from the edicts published on account of it, and from irrefutable

of the subsequent degradation of religion, and of
the apparent extinction of religious feeling in so

has recorded these controversies , " manifests itself

in the application of it as a name of reproach to un-

v. 749.) that the republication of these edicts was part of the

for the first time by the controversialists.

   I know not whether the instances which Spener

" When the hearers see those who inculcate any

duty, themselves act wholly differently, they think
that the preacher mocks them, inasmuch as he re-
quires of them what he does not impose upon him-
self.   Hence those preachers, who lead a life out-
wardly decent, but not conformable to the true rules
of Christ (which are higher than all mere morality,
and require a person wholly different from what the
best of the unregenerate either are or can be,) cause
grievous offence ; nay, I might almost say, they cause
by such offence more injury than others whose lives
are openly vicious : for those others are condemned
by all, and no one is so senseless as to take their
example for a rule ; while all believe that they per-


             ¹ Consil. Lat. i. 340. ap. Hossb. i . 51–53 .


302


fectly fulfil their duty when they follow the example
of these.      When then they see their pastor, -not
drunken, but from time to time, when occasion offers,
oppressed with too much wine ; not unjust nor plun-
dering, but yet too intent upon his property, and
more careful about the heaping up of worldly goods
than Christian simplicity allows ; -not openly over-

but yet insisting harshly on his rights against those
by whom he has been injured , or even desirous of
revenge, though under some specious plea ; -then
will such hearers in truth praise him as a pious man,
and scarcely doubt that when they have reached the
same point, they will have arrived at the pinnacle of
perfection .    By such examples are the people per-
plexed, so that they form conceptions of Christianity
wholly different from the descriptions of our Saviour ;
and all zeal , which the reading of His word may have
awakened in them, is annihilated whenever it requires
more from them than this ;       and thus are those
who ought to be Christians, in fact amid the show
and outward confession of Christianity, mere heathen ;
venerating a certain Christ scarcely otherwise than the
heathen do their idols , but wholly destitute and inex-
perienced in every living virtue which exists through
faith . For since they allow sin the dominion over their
hearts, and the flesh, although its outward ebulli-
tions are checked , exerts an uncontrolled power, nay,
betrays its dominion by means of those outward signs,
which, amid the present corrupted ideas of Chris-
tianity, are scarcely regarded as ungodly, I cannot

persuade myself, nor could I believe even though con-

and that, deprived        as it is of every fruit of the
Spirit, it yet lives.   Such corruption is there even in
the life of those who appear to many wholly blame-
less ."

   Of the specific complaint of the evils of theological
contests, Spener thus speaks ' .   " With relation to
the controversies which are now increasing among
theologians , I have often mourned over this evil ,
when I have thought of the words of the apostle,             if
ye bite and devour one another, take heed that ye
be not consumed one of another .'                While I , how-
ever, abhor the deceit and malice of Satan, who in-
cites against each other, people filled with fleshly
desires, (of which this contentious spirit gives a clear
evidence, ) and thus draws them from more neces-
sary things, and so has little to fear for his king-
domfrom them, yet I humbly venerate the righteous
judgment of God, acknowledging that this is the
fruit of the scholastic theology, which through over-
refinement and a pompous display of learning, is
distorted into a form very different from the apostolic
simplicity.    For after this had taken place, and there
were mingled with the simple word of heavenly truth
all sorts of technical expressions, assumptions, and
distinctions, which were frequently employed by cap-
tious philosophers, and were again liable to various
questions, it could scarcely be otherwise than that
theology should be exposed to the same love of strife,
and that a certain necessity should be introduced

              Cons. Lat. iii . 218. ap. Hossb, 1 , 29 .


304


of speaking over- refinedly on all subjects.                  What,
however, is thus established, is naturally such as
another equally acute can again question, whereas
truth in its natural simplicity can easily overpower
those who are infected with the disease of contra-
diction.      Hence it has come to this pass, that amid
the defence of truth (which, considered more atten-
tively, is not a love of truth itself, but of our own
subtle conceptions, ) we have oft forgotten Christian
brotherly love ; although this was enjoined to us not
less earnestly than the truth itself, by Him who is
both truth and love.          It has often pained me, that
the endeavour after love and mildness in religious
controversies, has been regarded almost as suffi-
cient groundfor reproach and shame ; so that they
who feel themselves too weak to bear the hatred

thence resulting, sometimes withdraw from studies,
which might have benefitted the church.                  The Lord
have mercy on his Zion !"
   Similar descriptions of the beginning of the next

S. litteris, magnopere antea apud multos docentes discentesque
restinctum et languentem." The whole context, and yet more
the section referred to, on the impediments to this revival of
practical Christianity, shew that Weismann is speaking of the
revival of genuine piety in Spener's school, in contradistinc-
tion to the contemporary fanaticism or abuses or human per-

Lutheran Church,' which follows, he will perceive,

the self- styled orthodox part of the Church, Weis-

' specimens of the divine anger against the Lutheran
Church in this century ; ' namely, ' a Pseudo-Luther-

both through the natural inclination of the flesh and

Christianity brought back, barren speculation removed , &c . as
far as their influence extended ? By the Pietists.

   After these accounts, it is of little moment, whether,

while the great duties of the ministry were thus
neglected, the evils of uncharitable controversy were
added to the rest.          It may be observed, however,

I speak of its perfection , I do not mean what it might
and ought to become in conformity to the ideal of
it, but only what it became in comparison with its
earlier condition . It was gradually transformed
into a systematic science and art, was conducted
more upon general principles, became more compre-
hensive and more ordered.      It lost much of its

CHAPTER X.


Spener-Source of opposition to him— Illustrations ofhis mild-
  ness, humility, caution, moderation - Aversion to every thing
  which might produce division - Value for human learning,
  and advice on general and theological study - Light which
  the history of his controversies would throw on the state of
  his Church, and its subsequent history.


THE proverbial injustice of the world to all contem-
porary merit of a high order, has undoubtedly been
generally     diminished    by the influence        of Chris-
tianity   it would certainly not be true, broadly
stated, that we still " virtutem incolumem odimus."
Human nature, however, although its character is
modified by the leaven of the Christian doctrines,
remains essentially the same ; and although the op-
position is not so universal as in heathen times,
the experience of all ages has evinced, that elevated
goodness, if from circumstances it stand in opposi-
tion to the prevailing tendencies of the age, or cast
a reproach upon it, is not more secure from calumny
than of old .   It still remains for the faithful servants
of God, if their lot fall in evil times, to do his will
through evil as well as good report : the less painful,
because less guilty, portion of former history is still
again and again realised, that a blind and ignorant,
because unpurified, zeal considers opposition to them
a service to God .  This truth has indeed often been

misapplied.     Remains of carnal pride, even in well-


315


intentioned Christians, has too frequently induced
them to regard opposition, in fact engendered by
their own failings, as enmity to the cause of God :
an impure enthusiasm often creates the opposition,
which it longs for as a test to itself of the holiness of
its own cause.  The supposition itself has thus been
brought into disrepute ; but in substance it remains
true.   It had been exemplified towards Andrea, and
the other eminent practical Christians, who had been
raised up in this age ; its truth was yet more evinced
towards Spener, in proportion to the more important
part which he was called to act, and the more sensible
contrast of his principles and practice with those
prevailing in his   day.    Many who could endure
general censures on the age, or on the defects of the
ministry, (although even these, as is often remarked
frequently by Andrea, drew down from persons, ap-
parently not included in them,        a vehemence of
hostility, which attested the power of conscience ; ) —
many who could endure this, could not yet bear the
tangibleness of the contrast between their own con-
duct, and that of those who realized the ideal of
Spener.    And consequently, where the        animosity
did not originate ( as was too often the case) in pri-
vate motives of dislike to Spener himself, it was
generally occasioned by the contact in office with
some who acted on his principles.
   The great evils resulting from this to the Church,
in the opposition to genuine piety, have been already
touched upon . In comparison with these, it was little,

or rather it was a part only of them, that Spener's
character was aspersed, that his immediate influence


316


though still wide, was limited, his piety regarded
as fanaticism, his orthodoxy questioned, he himself
termed the ' patriarch of fanatics ; ' when even acknow-
ledged truths came to be regarded as doubtful, be-
cause they proceeded from him.  "It is perhaps not
wholly unknown to you," he says           in declining the re-
quest of a prince to write on a certain subject, " that
through the oral and written attacks of certain

hostile    theologians,      (whom God forgive)          I have,
although innocent, been so blackened                in   general
opinion, and have been made such an object of sus-
picion, that even truths in themselves the most un-
deniable, are, because held by me, regarded as sus-
picious.     How      then is it to be hoped that any
thing written by one, so great an object of aver-
sion, could find an advantageous hearing ? The
contrary rather were to be feared ." Even when

the third of a century had flowed by after his
                66
death ( 1739) ,    many were ashamed to own him,
dreaded to quote any thing out of his works, to refer
to him, or to bestow any degree of praise upon him "."
66                                 3
   Spener's writings," says Knapp    towards the end
of that century, (1792) 66 are now sunk into                  an

almost total oblivion.          Yet they deserve to be read
now also, for they contain more than an ordinary
degree of what is useful and instructive.            It is one
object of this memoir to recall them to memory, and
to recommend their employment ."
  A vivid delineation of the excellences of this ad-

   Beyond the great piety of Spener, which is on all
hands admitted, two points appeared to me, as I have
stated, eminently to qualify him to act as a reformer
of the practical character of the Lutheran Church ;
his great judgment namely, and his incomparable
humility and mildness ' .           In no instance, I believe it
may be safely said, was Spener tempted to any un-
gentle word         by the violent opposition and calum-
nies with which he was assailed ; nay, which perhaps
is one of the greatest proofs of the utter absence of
the pride of enthusiasm (at least it is one of the
rarest) , he never considered this opposition either as
a real trial or as an honour.       It was after much

painful opposition both to himself and his friends,
that he writes thus           : " I will say nothing of its

being a joy and honour before God to suffer some-
thing for the sake of truth, of which honour I deem
myself unworthy, but only mention that I have ob-
served the benefits of it ; that God namely has thereby
kept both myself and others in so                    much deeper

humility ', and made us in many points more cau-

we have formed a hearty and honest determination
to do good only, and that zealously, and see oppor-
tunity of so doing before us. Confident of the right-
ness of our intentions, we are apt to become incau-
tious, and not to weigh on each occasion all the cir-
cumstances (as were best) so carefully as the state
of our times requires ; but are apt to think, that
because the cause is good,             we cannot easily go
wrong. This, however, is then very likely to happen,
and may thus injure what is good .   Nor do I deny
that the one or other well-meaning persons may

have stumbled on this stone, and that the good which
they did, might have been done with more precaution
and prudence.          God then taught us by this opposi-
tion to give so much the more accurate attention to
every thing, and to be as much afraid (i . e. employ

more than the bad take in doing evil .                 We know
also that happy and unimpeded success in the objects

us, and soon imperceptibly awakens arrogance ; so
it may well be, that God saw this corruption in me
and in others, and that we might then abuse and de-
stroy the good we had done .              This then was most

object which lay at his heart, practical Christianity.

   The same mild spirit which discovers itself in such
and similar passages, Spener evinced in his actual
defences of himself (for he never commenced a con-

tends for the truth, and seeks sincerely the honor of

And though it might be said that the opposers of the

the person, in view, he could not be easily induced
to suppose any evil intention in his opponents ; but
excused them on the ground of their impetuosity,
which prevented their acknowledging the truth of
his doctrine, and misled them to look on every thing

   It is a sentiment which he frequently expresses,

with regard to the erring, that more is effected by
mildness and gentleness than by vehemence .                       " I
acknowledge readily," he says , " that I endeavour
to do every thing by love and gentleness, nor am I
disposed to change this plan, nay, rather would I
recommend it to others.  If nothing else is gained
by it, yet even this is much,                 that it influences
well-disposed persons, who in any degree acknow-
ledge the divine truth, whereas they may easily be
offended      by vehemence.And I firmly believe,
that as love is the characteristic of the children
of God and disciples of Christ, so gentleness, its
first fruit, is the chiefest which we have to learn
of the Lord ; whenever any thing occurs to me,

not conformable to it, I always think that I hear
the word of my Saviour ; Know ye not of what
spirit ye are ?"
   This mildness was deeply based upon Christian
humility, the only foundation upon which such un-
varying mildness, which harsh opposition did but
strengthen, could be laid .         When he met with praise,
says Knapp ', he was wont to say, (and in him it was

as many others, were wrongly ranked among his adherents.

generally ends with the wish that some one equal to the task

theran Church might be enabled to understand its real character.

has sometimes annoyed some of my enemies that in
treating of matters which are a thorn in their eyes ,
I so limit what I say by truth, that they cannot dis-
tort it as they would to an erroneous meaning .               I
recollect that on one occasion, a person complained
that he did not know how to deal with my writings,
in which he would gladly have found something to
censure ; for when he had hit upon a point, which he
thought would furnish ground of attack against me,
as holding heretical opinions, he always, upon con-
sidering every thing accurately, or reading further,
found something in connection with it which pre-
vented his attack .       This, however,       I consider no
disgrace, but rather thank God for having taught me
by the example of other pious men, (who, as being
inexperienced in the want of candour of others, have
sometimes written less guardedly, and have thereby
been exposed to the harsh sentences of uncandid
judges) as well as by that of my own first essays, to
write, as if one had to defend each page against
severe and unkind opponents.              Hence, I propose
indeed my doctrine firmly and plainly, with hearty
prayer to God for such prudence ; but since I know
that each word may be misinterpreted , and all sorts
of inferences deduced from it by bitter people who
would gladly find occasion to revile, I endeavour,
by further limitations of these expressions, to anti-
cipate all such consequences ."

   This caution and this mildness evinced itself not

only in actual controversy, but in all Spener's inter-
course with others. In all his earlier undertakings,
he uniformly acted in harmony and with the appro-
bation of his colleagues . His reformation of the

ill-conducted catechising in Francfort ', and the insti-
tution of the Collegia Privata, were not undertaken
until approved by them ; to them he submitted his
Pia Desideria before their publication , as he did his
book on the " spiritual priesthood ," to the whole mi-
nisterium¹.  To those fellow- labourers, over whom
he was placed, he was anxious to show all kindness,
and to avoid unnecessary interference, while he was
ready to share their duties : and when in his high
office at Dresden, he felt himself compelled to revive
by his own example, the duties of catechising, without
consulting the other clergy, it was, as he himself
wrote to a friend , only because he knew that their
prejudices against it could only be removed by ex-
perience, and that in such cases offence is better
avoided by undertaking what one knows to beof
importance without advice than against it.               Amid
the certainty also of his approaching end, he could
remind his colleagues ' of the love which he had borne
to them, of the mildness with which he                had em-
ployed his authority over them ; while he protested
that he had no feelings against his opponents, but
rather wished from his heart that they might far

   1 Hossb. ii. 3, 4.
    To the testimonies already given to the Pia Desideria, I will
add the judgment of the candid Knapp (1. c. 106. ). " This work
contained no general declamations, nor any thing offensive, as
did so many works of others on the same subject. With all its
openness there reigned in it modesty and mildness. He did not
content himself with pointing out failings and defects, but he
suggested improvements. The book was received by many theo-
logians and by statesmen also with the approbation it deserved.
In many places, the wants of the Church were perceived , and
attempts made to remedy them on Spener's plans. This could
not escape the attention of theologians . They, finding it more
commodious to retain the old system, began to complain of sus-
picious innovations ; at first, however, only privately, without
attacking him openly in printed works. The character of Spener
was too firmly established , he was too much valued by the higher
ranks, and even at courts : what he had written was expressed so
modestly and so cautiously, and yet for the most part so precisely
and evidently true, that it seemed dangerous openly to contest
the points with him. Yet even then many theologians of Witten-
berg, and others of the same character in Saxony, would not
have been silent, had not Spener had friends in the courts of
Electoral and Ducal Saxony, who approved his proposals of

    " Would," he says, Letzte Bed. iii . 119. " that the love
which I acknowledge that we owe to those who err in the faith ,
had never led any so far, as to enter into intimate communion
with them, and with the good intention of loving and valuing what
was good in them, gradually to adopt unperceived some of their
errors, and on the other hand ungratefully to undervalue the truth
which has been given us by God . Would that no one went astray
in separating himself from the good with the object of detaching
himself from the evil . Rather should every one learn to make
an accurate distinction between the condition of the church such
as we wish it to be ( and to which we ought, according to the
power which God has given us, and the state of life in which we
are placed, to endeavour to bring it, ) and that in which , after we
have done our best, it stands. We should then bear with pa-
tience what is grievous in it, not separating ourselves, but waiting
in obedience for that time, which the Lord himself has appointed
for the separation of the tares from the wheat. It is certain
also that all separation from a church, when the pure doctrine yet
remains, is much more dangerous than any other offences how-
ever great ; since this separation most offends the good , gives the
doctrine of holiness an evil name, and makes its enemies rejoice,
nay miserably hinders its course, which we have not so much
reason to dread from other sources of offence."

    " Did Spener well, (asks Knapp, 1. c. 102. ) to continue his
meetings, after a separatistic disposition had discovered itself
among some of his hearers ?" " I will not interfere," he says,
" with the judgment of others : but I may be allowed one remark.
Whenever a new revolution has taken place in the church, there
have arisen persons who, through weakness and an extravagant
zeal for the new truths, which they but half understand, become
fanatics. The light, which beams suddenly in the darkness, dazzles ;
and it is often long ere a weak eye becomes accustomed to its
brightness. How many enthusiasts arose in the first period of
the Christian Church ! How many fanatics at the time of the
Reformation ! Were the Apostles on that account to abandon
their preaching-Luther his reformation ! So thought Spener
also, thus he tranquillized himself ; and in the consciousness of
the goodness of his cause, and the blessing which had been
thereby realized, pursued his course tranquilly and undeterred."
    " Those members of the council, to whom the care of the
church was committed ." Hossb. i. 119.

in all cases they were to be under the direction of the
 ministry ' .     The object proposed by them was to in-

rest, who were not yet fitted to admit its warmth .
The tendency to division, which manifested itself,
was not the consequence of these meetings, but of
that spirit of undue excitement which before existed,
and which in a sounder state of the German church

    Nor is Spener's admirable prudence less manifest
 in his views on the practical reformation which he
 thought necessary for the church, or the mode in
 which it was to be effected .            He steadily objected
 to any sudden or precipitate innovation ; he was a
 stranger to that heated and over-anxious zeal, which
is not contented unless it sees realized in its own

days the great objects which it contemplates, which
is disinclined to wait for the slow and gradual pro-

gress which God seems generally to annex as a con-
dition to the introduction of permanent good, but
risks the destruction of the harvest by its endeavours
to accelerate the months of comparative inactivity,
during which the seed must first take root.                   The
germ, when thus unnaturally forced, often gives in-
deed a quicker and earlier show, but the plant be-
comes in proportion sickly and weak.                Spener dis-
covered merely a firmer and more enlightened faith,
the issue he committed cheerfully in all cases to
God, contenting himself to labour steadily and ear-
nestly in his appointed office.            He thus expresses
this feeling, in the contemplation of the great diffi-
culties with which he was surrounded in Saxony,

      It was accordingly his advice to those clergy who
were animated by a warm zeal for the Church, to
employ the utmost prudence in all things relating to
it,    not always to consider the boldest proposals as
the best,-not to steer against the wind , but to tack,
that their vessel might not sink : where they could
not obtain every thing , yet to neglect nothing which

deferring this last till a later time, when he might

   The same prudence evinces itself in what Spener

while the heart was yet filled with love to the world
and its vanities, could neither please God nor con-
tribute at all to true Christianity."          Similar wisdom
and good practical judgment also discovers itself in
his opinion upon a question, which afterwards be-
came the criterion of a party, -the supposed necessity
of a severe and (as generally described ) a desponding
struggle before real repentance took place .             Spener
showed a more real knowledge of God's ways and
of human nature . He denies not 2 " that this might
sometimes       take    place, although he thought, not
generally ; that it always must, appeared (he says)
neither from God's word nor from experience. God's
ways, though directed to one end, are various ; some
he draws with          gentle cords,    others with greater
pain ; in neither case by chance, but according to
the counsels of his holy wisdom .           We are not then
with Peter (John xxi. 21. ) to call Him to account,
that we may not be answered , ' If I will that he tarry
till I come (i . e. that he be brought by an easier way,
by fewer sufferings,) what is that to thee ? follow
thou me,' and be content with the orderings of my

                Ap Hossb. ii. 122.
            2 Bedenk. iii . 588. ap Hossbach i . 183 .


344


 wisdom.      A Paul, the Lord strikes, as it were, with
lightning to the ground, and he must abide three
 days in blindness and fasting, and it may not have
been without the most sensible pain that such a
noble child of God was born . In others there arose
(Acts ii. ) out of one sermon a single earnest painful
penitence, which penetrated the heart, and thus on
the same day they were admitted to mercy and the
sacrament of regeneration. With the chamberlain

and the jailor (Acts viii . and xvi .) , this took place yet
more rapidly ;—and yet were they all placed in that
state in which they could be saved, which could not
be without true divine faith, and accordingly without
the heavenly light and power having penetrated into
their souls, and consequently without real regenera-
tion. And yet all these did not feel that, by which
He has led others to Himself ; as also in other parts
of His dealings with those who are His, both within
and without, there is considerable variety, so that a
difference of this sort must not so much surprise us :
nor must what we observe in others, or even what
we experience in ourselves, be regarded, or be
given out by us as the general rule .    This alone re-
mains true and certain , that in Christ and Christianity
there is aλna , a truth, and so not a mere conception

its powers, understanding, will, and affections : hence

what he was before, and from what the unregenerate
are     and from this he learns that there is something


345


essential , which is now the inward principle in him,
from which what is good in him is properly derived,
as on other occasions he feels from the indwelling

he speaks of the " misfortune arising from the pub-

renovation, in that it was often not specified , that such
was indeed the course of God with these persons, but

that they could not tell whether it would be so with
others ; on the contrary, it was rather implied that
what had happened to them was the rule for others
also .   Hence it had often happened, that real Chris-
tians, because they had not gone              through this
course, doubted of the truth of the good work which
was really begun in them, and were sorely troubled.
Thereupon they endeavour to force themselves to
this or that, which God does not work in them.
Yet, although there is but one way, in which all the
children of God are led to their salvation , there are
many paths along this one way, by which he leads
different persons according to his wisdom.             They,
on the contrary, who from observing the example of
others, endeavour to force themselves into a similar

path     assuredly   rather   hinder than promote the
work of God in them."          These passages are so cha-
racteristic, and a contrary conduct is often even now
producing so much pain and disquiet to conscientious
Christians, that it is hoped the length of these quo-
tations will be excused.

   There is one more imputation which is very fre-
quently cast upon Spener, and which may contribute
to the impression that he is no fair judge of the
state of the Lutheran Church and Lutheran theo-
logy, his supposed disparagement of human learn-
ing.   It would perhaps have been natural, that a
man of less enlarged views should have been induced
by the character of the learning of his day, which
through misapplication , or a too exclusive cultivation,
was frequently unprofitable, and sometimes perni-
cious, proportionably to depreciate it.      Still the same
construction, which is put on the warnings of St. Paul
against vain philosophy, or on Luther's condemna-
tion of that of his day, ought to have been employed
here also , and such depreciations limited to the sub-
ject in question,-ill- timed and mis- placed learning.
Yet in this instance also, the deviation of the later
Pietists from Spener's views has been confounded with
the principles of Spener.       This great man was, as
has been already stated ' , possessed of considerable
learning ; in his early years he had refused important
engagements which interfered with the pursuit of
the studies in which he thought his calling lay' ;
his defence of his church against the Romanists is
said to be a monument of well-applied learning
doctrinal theology, ecclesiastical history, and scrip-
tural interpretation, and to have been one of the
most important controversial works on the subject,
embracing nearly the whole           system of Lutheran

           1
             Enquiry, p. 67 , note 1 .
           2 Hossbach i. 90, 93. ap p. 94.

theology ' ; and his defence of the scriptural doctrines
against the Socinians, the last work of advanced
and declining life, was universally approved of by

   Spener's object accordingly was not to displace
theological learning, but to recall it from some of

   Spener may not then perhaps have fully felt the
benefits of an enlarged preliminary education for all
persons, however small their natural endowments.
Yet what he wished to substitute was far better
than that which already existed .              " I know not,"

(he says , with reference to theological students)

of real use to a student, if it is pursued without
neglecting what is essential, and if rightly applied. "

misapplied philosophy had brought into the church ;
he doubted whether the introduction of the Platonic
philosophy by the Alexandrian school had been gener-
ally beneficial , deeming it the source of many later er-

of the Aristotelian philosophy, and the scholastic theo-

sophy , but such a study of it, as should obviate
the blind following of a particular school.      Hence,
besides Aristotle's logic, whose value he acknowledged ,
he recommended the study of Cicero, Plutarch, Dio-

good view of philosophy ; he wished especially, if

Spener especially wished to be studied, the first as
increasing the knowledge of God, the second as the
best means of improving the understanding .        For a
class of students of inferior powers, he considered
a mere historical study of philosophy sufficient, with-
out a deep acquaintance with philosophy ; the lowest
class, he thought, had better abstain from it alto-
gether.   As philosophy appeared to him on the one
hand exalted to too great importance, so he thought
philology far too much neglected ; and to the ne-
glected and very deficient study of the sacred lan-
guages he attributed (his biographer adds       rightly')
the melancholy condition of theological learning.
The whole of divinity, he said, in the words of Chem-
nitz and Luther, is a knowledge of grammar and

of language, i . e. is engaged in the knowledge of
Scripture.   The first object must therefore be to
acquire the means of obtaining this knowledge, the
Hebrew and Greek languages.        This study accord-
ingly could not be dispensed with even in those, who
might well do without philosophy ; they must at
least advance so far as to be able to read Holy
Scripture in the original, and to employ to good
                                          Spener

did indeed trace too narrow a course for the acqui-

language could only be thoroughly learnt by varied
and extensive reading ofthe different works contained
in it, and that the study of the profane authors fur-
thered the right understanding of Scripture writers,
he still wished the principal attention at schools to be
bestowed upon that which was necessary to all , the

knowledge of the sacred writers ; leaving those ca-
pable of more enlarged studies to supply the rest at

by the nature of the evil which he wished to remedy,
-the too exclusive value attached to Latin and to

heathen science, producing an entire ignorance of
Scripture.      It was on the same ground, and not
from undervaluing the uses of history, (his own favo-
rite study, to make any real progress             in which, he
said, the whole man must for some time be given up
to it,) that he proposed to confine the preparatory
studies of this science in the school, to some one

author, who should present the whole succession of
the most important events.   It must be recollected
                                        1
also, that in Spener's time six or seven years was
the usual period employed at the Universities, so that
considerable time would still be allowed for the pur-

suit of preparatory studies before those of proper theo-
logy commenced .
   Nor was any neglect of science apparent in the
outline which Spener drew for the study of theology
itself.   His main object was, not to diminish the in-
fluence of science, not to make theology less scien-
tific, but to give to the science itself a more practical
direction .   Of the theological sciences, his great

aim was to restore to Scriptural Interpretation the
rank which the Reformers had assigned it.       He
termed it the architect which was to arrange all the
rest ; it was the mine, from which almost all their

materials were to be drawn.          Upon this he wished
Doctrinal Theology to be formed , that it might thus
be restored to its primitive sacred and divine simpli-
city.   He did not, however, unduly depreciate even
the existing systems of Doctrinal Theology, but re-
commended the employment of the better amongst
them, in order to obviate the labor of arranging the
doctrines of Scripture, only warning the students
against a blind belief in authority, and against indo-
lence in not searching the Scriptures for themselves.
He did not even reject the scholastic terminology,
deeming it, under existing circumstances, indispens-
able to enable persons thoroughly to understand
theological works, to express themselves with the
necessary caution on theological matters, and so to
avoid all appearance of heterodoxy.              The advice
which he gives with respect to the method of study-
ing doctrinal theology was also very judicious.             He
wished that some one compendium ' should be laid
 as the basis, by means of which the close connection
of the Christian doctrines, and the analogy of faith

    The practical mode of studying controversial theology at
Halle, has been already touched upon in the Enquiry. A similar

356


writings, to charge them with nothing which they
did not maintain, and to employ only sure and strong
grounds, not by the multiplication of such as were
weak, to give them so many vulnerable points.         In
ecclesiastical history again, Spener wished the best of
his three classes to become deeply acquainted with
the Fathers   for the second he recommended a dili-
gent study of ecclesiastical history, and an historical
acquaintance with those great men : even for the
lowest, he held a comprehensive survey of the whole,
and of the chief points, to be necessary in their
earlier years ; and this knowledge he wished to be

afterwards perfected .   The advice of Spener on the
study of the Bible manifests the same judgment.
Instead of the " unfruitful modes which then existed,

where any exposition of the Bible did exist at the
Universities, a philological namely, or polemical dis-
cussion of certain difficult or controverted passages,"
he wished that entire     books should be explained
verse by verse in their whole connection, and with
a concise notice of the theoretical application .   Such
a study, he thought, of one book under an able
teacher might be a guide to the right exposition of
all the rest. Should there be no opportunity for

doing this with an instructor, he wished that the
several books should first be studied as a whole, in
order that the student might become acquainted with
their general subject, its division, and the mode of
treating it ; that he should then, in a more labo-
rious and minuter study, weigh each verse and each
word, and compare passages of the same general
meaning, not exhausting his powers,         however, in


357


the first instance, upon passages of the greatest diffi-
culty, but leaving these for subsequent study. The
advice also that the word of God should first be
studied without any aid but itself, ( after which com-
mentaries were to be consulted for confirmation or
correction, or further examination of the results thus
obtained, ) had a tendency to promote the independent
understanding of Scripture, while the employment
of human aids was recommended where it would be

most efficacious.       Nor is any depreciation of human
learning implied by the great maxim of Spener,
that an unregenerate or ungodly theologian did not
possess the true theology, -that since it was a prac-
tical science, he who did not understand it practically,
would understand it very imperfectly ' . " A Christian
student ," in Spener's words " , " prays as earnestly for
divine illumination as if he required no diligence of
his own     but he studies also with the same diligence
as if his labours were to effect every thing .               For it
were a presumption and a tempting of God only to
pray, and then to await the divine illumination with-
out his own exertions."

  These extracts may suffice perhaps to give some,
though comparatively a faint, idea of the beautiful
Christian mildness and temper, as well as of the
great judgment, wisdom, prudence, and practical
character of this admirable man. Much additional

light would be thrown both upon his qualifications as a
practical reformer and a Christian teacher, and upon


  1 See Enquiry.
  2 Gestalt. e. würd . Stud . S. 1147. ap Knapp. I. c . ii . 50 .


358


the state of theology in his age, by surveying him in
the controversies in which he was involved by the
ultra-orthodox party.     The   discussion,   however,
would lead into topics too manifold for the present
occasion.    A knowledge of these controversies, as
they were carried on against him and his successors,
is indeed indispensable to the full understanding of
the subsequent fearful revolution.   They give painful
but ample proof of the yet further decline of the
scientific theology of those, who would fain be re-
garded as the sole representatives of the Lutheran
Church : some important truths, which had been ac-
knowledged by earlier divines, even of the 17th cen-
tury, were now regarded as criteria of Pietism and
heterodoxy    even those disputes , which were in fact
logomachies, and in which Spener and his        school
held the same thing as those who censured them,
tend to show, more perhaps even than the rest, how
little prepared the Lutheran Church was for the con-
flict which followed.   In that period, as has often
been already remarked, there were two parties, who
proposed to themselves objects entirely distinct ; the
one, the reformation of the faulty parts of the ex-
isting system, the other, the destruction of the whole.
These parties, as was natural, melted by an infinite
variety of shades into each other : that party which
was animated by an unholy enmity to Christianity,
appears to me to have been in itself too weak, had
it been opposed to a genuine Christianity, to have
effected much.   It prevailed, I think, through the
weakness of its opponents, and the miserable reli-
gious condition of the age, not by its own strength .


359


The other party, who in consequence of the unfor-
tunate state of things, unintentionally furnished arms
for the destruction of what they themselves valued,
would probably, under happier auspices, have really
contributed to its defence. It was of the utmost

importance to detach these two parties ; not un-
doubtedly      by any unworthy compromise, not of
course by any of those shallow conciliations, which
first sacrifice truth for peace, and in the end lose
both ; not merely because they were parties,-but
because the requisitions made by the one of them
were just, and the inconsiderate and hostile rejection
of these tended to confound in the ordinary mind
the difference between these better-disposed men,
and the fanatic enemies of the Gospel.
   The two opposite evil consequences which result
from undue imputations of enmity to the Gospel
truth, according to which persons of opposite cha-
racter are led to suspect the innocent undeservingly,
or to feel less strongly the unhappiness of the errors
themselves, are both noticed by Spener, in answer
to a clergyman of high rank, who had sent him a
                                   66
plan of Anti-Atheistic Lectures '.    Many will re-

                         6
Atheism .  For since the   qualiscumque imputatio
Atheismi' is the most grievous with which one, in
other respects an honest man, can be assailed, and
one which, when his name has been once stained with

it, cannot be easily effaced, Christian charity requires,
that we should not incautiously give occasion to its
being fastened upon an innocent person .        You will

always able to discern, how far the imputation is ex-
tended ; and it is not only matter of apprehension ,
but almost certain, that if great precaution be not
taken, people who are nothing less than Atheists ,
and have an utter abhorrence of such ungodliness ,
will be regarded as Atheists by the hearers, when they
shall afterwards meet in them with any thing which has
been charged with this indirect Atheism. All opinions
which are, or are supposed to be, at variance with
the letter of Scripture, are not on that account to
be charged with Atheism, since a difference has always
been made between heterodoxy and atheism ; nothing,
therefore, but the most urgent necessity must induce


361


us to proceed to this last imputation.   Nay, should
we give occasion, that the name of atheism should
be applied to so many, and indeed almost every sort
of error, since many will not be persuaded that the
imputation is well founded, I fear that the horror
with which people now rightly shrink when they
hear of atheists, will be rather diminished, and
the cause of atheism unintentionally advanced.”


CHAPTER XI.


Explanation of the Theory on the Causes of Rationalism in
  German Theology- Why the mere external Causes were not
  thought sufficient-Rationalism not attributed to one Cause
  only-Objections solved- Subscription to Symbolical Books
  not relaxed previously to the times of Rationalism— Case of
  the Reformed Church in Germany- The above Theory not one
  of the Rationalists- Justified from Presumption- Present
  state of Germany- Case of our own Country.


HAVING now, I imagine, sufficiently vindicated my
statements of facts as far as it was possible without
entering into too large a field, I must here re-state
briefly the general views of my Essay as to the
causes of Rationalism in German theology, and clear
them also from the misconceptions to which they
have been exposed.

  In all great revolutions, probably, whether in the
civil or religious world, the causes which lie nearest
to the event itself, are rather the occasions of the
crisis, than its ultimate and principal causes.        The

spark which, falling upon inflammable matter, creates
the widely-wasting explosion, would have expired
harmlessly, had not the noxious vapors, through
which it acts, been long in silence collected .        The
causes of the French revolution must          at least be
traced up to the reign of Louis XIV. , some of them
much further     those of our own Reformation lay


363


not in any event of the reign of our eighth Henry :
nor was the abuse which first awoke the opposition
of Luther, or even the existence of this great instru-
ment of God, the real cause which restored genuine
religion to Germany.     Had not other causes coin-
cided, had not a deep longing for a renewal of the
pure Gospel been secretly nourished, the electric

flash could not have passed so rapidly from one end
of Germany to the other : had Luther never existed,
the Reformation would indeed probably have been
delayed ; but it would have been suspended only,
until some other circumstances should give unity and
direction to the powers, which, though confined,
were already in being.   Nor can it of course diminish
our gratitude to God, or to those great men, whom
he employed as his agents, that they were not the
sole instruments of this his mercy, or that he had
prepared the way by which their efforts might be-
come more speedily and more extensively blessed.
It is then probable, upon general principles, that
any great revolution , whether for temporary good or
evil, should proceed ultimately and principally from
causes considerably antecedent to itself, especially in
the kingdom of Christ, in which there is a higher
unity than in ordinary earthly affairs.
  Such appeared to me to be the case in the late
fearful revolution in Germany, the results of which
we can indeed at present only imperfectly see, and
which, consequently, we can as yet only partially
understand .   Without arguing, however, from pro-
babilities, which can only suggest to us where to look
for the real causes of such stupendous events, not


364


determine where they are to be found, the above
principles seemed to me to be verified by this history
itself.     I found, namely, in the period immediately
preceding or contemporary with rationalism, several
agencies calculated to produce unbelief in some de-
gree.       They appeared to me, however, incapable in
themselves of producing effects so immense, and had
already, in other cases, failed of so doing.                    These
causes then         I mentioned and dwelt upon in my
Essay, but could not regard them as the sole or as
the deepest sources of unbelief.                Such were the in-
fluence of an able and popular , but unbelieving mo-
narch ' ; the circulation of the works of foreign² un-
believers ; the Wolfian ; and in a different way the
                 4
shallow popular philosophy which displaced it ; the
extraordinary despotism exercised by the review of
Nicolai      ; the     excitement of mind caused by the
sudden action in all departments of literature, acting
through the bad moral and intellectual condition of
the age.        It was moreover a striking phenomenon,
that much of the calamity in the first instance arose
from men whose endeavours were directed against
unsound parts only of the existing system, but who
contributed very considerably to the fall ofthe whole ;
and again that enquiries in philology, or the study
of history should in Germany have exercised a pre-
judicial influence, whereas in other countries, and in
this, their cultivation had been beneficial.


  1                                       2 Ibid . 127-34.
      Enquiry, p. 123.
      Ibid. p. 114-18 .           ⚫ Ib. p. 128 , 9.             P. 128.
      See Prof. Sack's letter, p . viii. ix . Enquiry, p. 7 .


365


  To enter a little more into detail upon one or two
points.
  We had ourselves in Charles II . a monarch pro-
fligate and unbelieving, but clever, who encouraged
licentiousness as well as the superficial and frivolous
mockery of religion, in those immediately within
his sphere     among them his example and other
causes acted widely ; doubts upon religion became
part of the qualifications of a gentleman or a man
of fashion ; but upon the clergy and upon the more
stable part of the nation no effect was produced..
Again, in Denmark the unhappy Count Struensee
exerted the whole        influence of his great talents
and almost royal eminence to overthrow both the
belief and the moral earnestness of the people : but
his attempt is     said not only to have failed for the
time, but to have caused his fall.    " The primitive
and sturdy principles of the Danes, aided by the
purity of their national religion," says Mr. Rennell¹ ,
" withstood the infection." Frederic on the con-
trary, although, unhappily for himself and his coun-
try, he knew not how entirely the morality, which he
wished to preserve, depends upon the religion whose
overthrow he countenanced by his encouragement of
French unbelievers and French literature, was still,
as I have understood, anxious for the moral improve-
ment of his country, and paid great attention to
those institutions which he thought calculated to pre-
serve it.

   Again, it was probable that a struggle, perhaps a


  1 Introduction to the account of Struensee's conversion, p. x.


366


severe one, would be caused by the writings            of
English and French unbelievers,       with which Ger-
many was inundated, and by the imitation of these
efforts by persons similarly disposed among the Ger-
man laity but these writings were, as long as their
direct influence lasted, opposed by the theologians .
The deists of this country, who alone were of a
character to produce any considerable effect upon
more thinking minds, had here long lost all their
influence ; after the first contest they had become
almost entirely unknown. It was therefore still a
problem, why the better among them should ulti-
mately have produced so much effect in Germany.
True Christianity is in any country, not only ulti-
mately but speedily, victorious over all the machin-
ations which may assault it.    It was yet more per-
plexing, without going yet further back to the different
state of the two churches, that the translations of
the works of our defenders of revelation should, in
Germany, have even increased the evil.
  It seemed to me still more manifest, that the other
causes which I mentioned as contributing to this
revolution, were either secondary only, or acquired
their influence from the previous state of religion .
Even the reception of the Wolfian philosophy into
theology was inexplicable without a knowledge of
the previous scholastic character of doctrinal theo-
logy while its demonstrative form and its abstract
character, its confusion of evidence, its neglect or per-
version of Scripture, and its naturalist tendencies,
which at all times and      under any circumstances
would have been pernicious, received their highest


367


power to hurt from corresponding points in the state
of theology or religion.         A faulty character of the
age is again presupposed by the absolute dominion of

such a publication as the review of Nicolai, which had
no merit except that of acuteness, and which from its
nature could not thoroughly discuss the                subjects
upon which its opinions were received as oracles.
I need scarcely observe that the moral or intellec-
tual state of a people cannot be adequately under-
stood without going back to preceding periods, in
which the foundation for this frame of mind was
laid.

   It thus being necessary to recur to the previous
history, there was no point short of the last great
era, the Reformation, from which the course of
events in that period could be adequately traced.
It would require a considerable volume to exhibit,
with the requisite fulness and vividness, the history
of this period, of which I attempted only to trace
the outline.     I may now only recapitulate yet more
concisely the results to which I came.              They were
these.    United with the immortal heroes, the mighty
agents of the Reformation, were others, who were
actuated by less perfect motives , who sought deliver-
ance only or chiefly from papal oppression ' , and but

   ¹ I find the same fact stated in Andrea's Menippus, Dial. 26 .

                          5


368


imperfectly understood the religion in which they
found refuge.   A liability to discord and degeneracy
was thus engendered ; this was promoted by prin-
ciples which were naturally brought over from the
religion which these persons had left, by other mis-
conceptions incidental to man, and by the political
divisions between the two branches of the house of
Saxony.   Luther during his life, by his extraordi-
nary powers, held together the discordant materials.
Soon after his death there appear two opposed par-
ties ; the one under Melanchthon , which held fast to
the essential principles and doctrines of Protestan-
tism, but which wished tacitly to drop those subtle-
ties in the Lutheran system, which the human mind
ought never to have entertained ; the other,       and
more popular party, the indiscriminate admirers of
every letter which had fallen from Luther.       Inju-
dicious conduct in the school of Melanchthon, after
their master had been removed from the troubles of

these distracting controversies, precipitated its over-
throw ; and the reception of the Formula of Concord
completed the destruction of the followers, the prin-
ciples and the tenets of Melanchthon.       After this
step no choice was left but to proceed in the groove
which had been formed.         By a strange yet well-
established analogy with the phenomena of the na-
tural world, a period, in which all the powers of the
mind appear to have been put forth to an unusual
degree is generally followed by one of comparative
exhaustion.  With the emergencies which called

forth the energies of the giants of the Reformation,
the race itself ceased ; and was succeeded towards


369


the end of the 16th century by a generation of very
inferior strength.     Upon these, the hereditary con-
troversies   exerted    a   more    pernicious   influence.

Minor questions acquired more and more, in minds
such as these, an intrinsic importance ; a large human
system was introduced, beyond what ever existed in
any portion of the Reformed Church.              The habit

thus generated in the Lutheran theologian, of re-
garding the defence of these abstract points as his
main duty, had an obvious tendency to turn his
mind from the more practical parts of Christianity ;
to make religion a matter of speculation rather than
of practical interest, -an occupation of the intellect
rather than of the heart.    These evils were I thought
further increased by the re-introduction of the scho-
lastic theology, whose       characteristic was abstract
speculation ; an event which took place partly in
consequence of the contests with the Romanists,
partly in the defence or attack of the matters of high
speculation, which had been admitted into the Lu-
theran system itself. The excessive importance again,
which had been attached to these controverted points,
occasioned the comparative neglect, or the perverted
pursuit, of those subjects which might have coun-
teracted     these prevailing      tendencies,   the   study
namely of the Scriptures for their own sake, of his-
tory, and of the practical application of theology in
the ministerial functions.         Opposition was indeed
made to this system on the scientific side by Calix-
tus and his school, on the practical side by Arndt,
J. V. Andrea, &c. and subsequently by Spener.
The effect, however, was, I observed, to diminish
                       Bb


370


the extent rather than to change the character of
the orthodox school.     The contests with the Pietists
were beyond all others pernicious to their opponents,
to themselves, and to the people.        To the people,
through the distraction arising from the constant
warnings against Pietism ; to themselves, by gra-
dually producing among many of them as narrow
and formal a spirit as that which their excellent pre-
decessors had opposed ; to their opponents, by ren-
dering them still more cold and stiff : practical doc-
trines, which had hitherto been held by that party,
were now abandoned as favouring Pietism.        On

this later pseudo- orthodox school was engrafted the
Wolfian philosophy ;    and science received its last
evil direction among them, at the same time that it
was being more and more discarded by the dege-
nerate Pietists. I need not again dwell on the
different modes in which the declension of the Pie-
tists took place, although the accounts may be use-
ful to all , and not least to these times.
   My conclusion then was, that the principal cause
of the extension of unbelief in Germany was the pre-

vious state of theology and of religion ; and that this
in both cases acted positively as well as negatively,
by increasing the ill while it diminished the power
of resistance. Indisposition to the Lutheran system
as a whole was created by the wearisomeness of its
controversies, its unpractical character, the manifest
untenableness of parts, the offensiveness of others,
which persons were yet unable to separate from the
sounder portions.     Hostility or indifference to reli-
gion was engendered by the different exhibitions of


371


it in a degraded form, as a lifeless and spurious
orthodoxy, a formal Pietism , or a wild fanaticism,
which had been confounded in the public mind with
genuine Pietism , or all earnest endeavours after holi-
ness.    Rationalism, however, was rather the indirect

result than the natural offspring of this struggle,
                                                                   I
rather a composition with the            invaders than the
acceptance of the terms which they originally dic-
tated.   The theologians endeavoured to              maintain ":
what they could of the high import of the Christian
doctrines ; but their purely intellectual conviction-
sunk before the increasing difficulties with which
they were surrounded .  Positive views of Christian
doctrine, where there is not a deep practical religion,
must always be at best superficial.             As they ran
along this waste, they became more and more shal-
low, until they were gradually absorbed, or at most
had little more of the freshness of the original foun-
tain, than the barren sand along which they flowed.
The unhappy state of German theology was produced
then , I still think, partly by reaction in engendering
dislike and opposition to it, partly by its own further
degeneracy, in that the doctrines of revelation were
gradually more and more debased until the gold
became dross.

these furnished a well-prepared soil for the seed of
unbelief, under whatever immediate circumstances
it might be planted."        The previous condition , then,
I regard as a disordered state of the constitution , cal-
culated to engender or to admit disease, where a
sounder frame of health might have resisted it : and
to give way before it when admitted, converting into
poisons what might, to a firmer constitution , have
been remedies.  The causes which at the commence-

ment of the struggle shewed themselves upon the
surface, I regard as the means which called the hid-
den complaint into action, and determined its cha-
racter. The body, as I said, might have recovered
its tone of health , had not these incidental causes
occurred, and those physicians been rejected, who
might have gradually restored it to a firmer state.

those further inferences which could not at the time

He had accordingly so ordered all the words of

Mosheim in the passage cited (ibid. p. 19.) to shew that subscrip-

any thing, would show that it was abandoned in the 17th cen-

formula, which was not enacted until 1675, was supplemental
only to those which already existed , being directed against the
innovations of Capell, Amyrault, and De la Place. Against the
first it asserts the inspiration of the Hebrew points, the perfect
integrity of the text, and rejects the use of the versions in cor-
recting that text ; against Amyrault it maintains in strong terms.
the particularity of divine grace ; against De la Place, the direct
imputation of Adam's individual sin, beyond the displeasure of
God at our inherent corruption. This formula soon afterwards,
in different parts of Switzerland , sunk in different ways into
neglect ; but the controversies about it did not affect the estima-
tion of the other Formulæ. The Formula itself is in Augusti's
collection, p . 443, sqq.; a full history of it is given by Schröckh ,
