                             cc 2


388


much sunk, adds, " they have been considered unfit
for the end proposed , and some went so far as almost
to propose their total abolition ; but since these were
and still are only the sentiments of a small por-
tion of this Church, no regard can be paid to them :
and the less, since the exercise of religion , conceded
in so many countries by the laws of the empire and
by pacification, was granted on the supposition , that
she would remain faithful to the system of her sym-
bolical books."

      In fact, however, this whole objection has nothing
to do with my theory.       I maintained that the faulty
symbolical     books   contributed   to   perpetuate the
faulty system of the Lutheran Church : that they
fostered the habits of mind in which they themselves
had originated ; that they excluded the influence of
the milder and more practical school by which that
system might have been gradually modified.        Now,
although the continuation of these restrictions would
probably, while circumstances remained the same,
perpetuate the system, it does not follow, nor is it
probable, that a mere incidental relaxation of them
on the part of the authorities, would have modified
it.    While the predominant character of the Ger-
man Church was yet unformed , while it was in its
first fresh life and vigour, it might probably under
better guidance have       assumed a character more
favourable to the pure announcement of doctrines
committed to it : but when the tree had acquired
its full growth, when above a century had passed
over it, fixing, strengthening , and indurating it in
the direction which had been originally given, it is


389


not probable that the mere removal of those instru-
ments which had bent its yet pliant form, should
now have any effect upon the aged and hardened
trunk. The confirmed habits of an hundred years

have, unless some mightier agency interfere, in them-
selves a principle of self-existence. It is the first
tendency of an established system to maintain itself :
the second, if parts of it are unsound, to produce op-
position to it under favouring circumstances as a
whole .    Hence one may perhaps account for the
extraordinary phenomenon that a faulty system (as
e. g. that of the extreme allegorical interpretation)
may at one time universally prevail, and be admitted
even by men who have no superiors in any age ; at
another,   may produce    a    system   equally faulty,
though altogether opposed to itself ; —that argu-
ments, which at one time seem to ground belief,
and of whose validity the most comprehensive minds
have been persuaded, may at another have a ten-
dency to generate unbelief.     A traditionary system
will long maintain itself, (whatever be the subject,)
even when unsupported         by internal means : the
history of every church will prove that a theological
scheme will long stand unaided .    The case is much
stronger, where,   as in the German Church, this
scheme is inculcated in a long theological education.
  Could indeed the majority or the more influential
portion of the Church, which held the tenets at first
objected to, have been induced to regard them as
unessential, the indisposition to the system, consi-
dered scientifically, would of course in that propor-
tion have been diminished : its defenders would have


390


been the better able to maintain it : the change
might have diminished also that feeling of excite-
ment at having burst these bands, and that supposed
superiority to former times, which acted so prejudi-
cially upon this age.     Even thus, however, a por-
tion only of the existing evil would have been re-
moved : it was not the mere lopping off this or that
portion of the system, which had sufficed to secure
the rest ; but a substitution of the practical convic-
tion of the main truths of Christianity , for the mere
intellectual conception or treatment of them.       The
doctrines of Christianity may, when unattacked, pre-
serve their hold over the intellect, even when they
have comparatively lost their dominion          over the
heart ; but it rarely happens that they can maintain
even that tenure in times when they are assailed
from without also .  Repeated experience has shewn,
that in the contest with unbelief their character is

gradually changed ; the fullness of their meaning
gradually lost, until they become, even if their names
are retained, scarcely different from some of the
corresponding ideas, which, under the title of natu-
ral religion, were at first opposed to them .   Because
they had no root, they wither more and more, until
they become as dry and lean as the steriles avenæ
sown among them.
  It is impossible, however, to conjecture, and it is
idle to argue what might have been the course of
things, had some among different complicated causes
been withdrawn .  The external sources of unbelief
connected with the reign of Frederic the IId. ,      de-
rived, I conceive, their tremendous power, not from


391


the one or other cause only, but from the combined
effect of the religious and scientific, of the intellec-
tual and moral condition of the age ; the influence
of the removal of one of these causes must have

been, at all events, very questionable.       The diminu-
tion at an earlier period of the number of symbo-
lical books, or the limitation of the subscription to
the essential truths contained in them, (those truths
being specified, ) with the abolition of the Formula
of Concord, might gradually have produced a bene-
ficial effect   a mere vague relaxation of them at so
late a period, when the struggle had commenced,
could certainly only have had the effect of precipitat-
ing the evil.
   II. The next objection derived from the similar
fate of the German Reformed Church, is founded
upon the same mistaken supposition , that I attri-
buted the whole effect to the symbolical books .
Since these Churches had no common symbol ex-
cept the Heidelberg Catechism (so that historians
generally speak of that symbol as the sole symbo-
lical book of those Churches, ) it is urged ' , that I
" cannot contend that mere              dead orthodoxism ,'
 adherence to a deadening formularism ,' &c. were
the causes of Rationalism in the Reformed Church."

Happy, too      happy race of men, were the evils of
dead orthodoxism,      which      assumes the garb and
mimics the appearance, and desecrates the name of
a living and genuine orthodoxy, without possessing
one of its real qualities, confined to one state of


                       ¹ Letter, p . 144.


392


things alone.   Happy race, if we abused in one way
alone the truths which God has given us, if in one
form only we held the semblance without the sub-
stance, the form of truth without its power, the
letter without the spirit. It then were easy to cast
this subtle enemy from us.    The fatal disease cleaves
to our race, however, in every form       in every age
of decayed piety it re-appears : from the days of the
Pharisees to those of the degenerate Pietists, it has
robbed men of their real treasure, and substituted
counterfeits.

  Although faulty symbolical books were obviously
calculated to continue and to promote that spirit, to
direct men to the minor speculations of unbiblical
controversy, in place of the more influential doc-
trines of Christianity, the spirit must to a certain
degree exist before such books are formed .       The
same formularism again has appeared, and, in a
sound Church, will always much more appear, in

the smaller separatist parties, although among them
no symbolical books have existed, or have exerted no
influence.   The formularism of the degenerate Pie-
tists was even more pernicious than that of the great
body, which their excellent predecessors had endea-
voured to unformularize.      Spurious   orthodoxy or
formularism is uniform in one quality alone, that it
substitutes a barren intellectual adherence to certain

truths, or certain modes of stating those truths, or
again of a set of opinions, or even of a received tech-
nical language, for the effects which those truths
were intended to produce upon the heart and life .
In other respects it varies endlessly according to the


393


circumstances of its birth : by no forms, however
deep their piety, can it be wholly prevented , although
some may be calculated to retard, others to promote
it ; constant vigilance, under God's grace, can alone
check the spreading of a malady, of which the seeds
lurk in every human heart.
   With regard to the German Reformed Church, I
have already stated my opinion that its theology re-
mained free from formularism to a much later period
than the Lutheran, and that unbelief consequently
found a slower access in it.           The data, however,
furnished by ecclesiastical historians ' are very meagre
or indefinite for the most part they relate only to
its numerical increase or decrease, or to its oppres-
sions at the hands of the Romanists : its              internal
condition is left at furthest to be inferred from sum-
mary descriptions of that of all those bodies which
bear the common name of the Reformed Church .

Although then I am satisfied that my general state-
ment is correct, the subject is very difficult to a

the Wolfian philosophy was introduced into the

truths," and stated that " these evils acted on prac-

   Into that other portion of the calculation of the
" thousands who died in indifference and in unbelief,
in sin and in misery," I did not enter, nor do I think
that man has a right to do so.    The thought of a sin-
gle human being, who is lost, must fill the heart with
unutterable dismay : but the question 66 are there
few who shall be saved," is one upon which the human
mind must not dwell, except in hope.      This only we
know, that each will be judged according to that
measure which was given him ; of the results we are
wholly ignorant.    What proportion did , in the evil
times, when Rationalism was predominant in German
theology, act up to the light which they had , is as
much unknown to us as that proportion, who before
broke through the " deadening formularism ;" or how
many of those who in our crowded cities, being edu-
cated only to vice and crime and sin, and never hav-
ing heard the name of their Maker, except in some
form of blasphemy, still avail themselves of the single
talent given them, so as to be accepted for the sake
of that Saviour, whom they never knew.        We know
nothing, and therefore we may hope every thing.
All upon which man can and may decide, is the

general apparent character of any age : he will
mourn over the apparent obstructions to the efficacy
of the Gospel at one time ; he will exult in their
removal at another, will rejoice that God has called
light out of darkness ; but he will not presume to
pronounce upon the fate of those who lived either


406


under the darkness or the light.   The history of the
visible Church alone is open to us ; and in this we
may and must look for warning or for comfort, with-
out attempting to pry into what that day only shall
disclose, which will reveal the secrets of all hearts.
With this limitation, there is nothing presumptuous

in attempting, as soon as the events are sufficiently
developed, to trace God's designs in the varying
fortunes of His Church. With this restriction, it is not
unbecoming to endeavour to show either how some of
the grievous errors of the Romish Church contributed
to the glorious bursting of the light, which shone at
the Reformation ; or again, how when by man's
fault this light had been anew obscured in Germany,
God allowed the chill dark night of Rationalism to
prepare for the living rays of the sun of righteous-
ness.  In neither case must one presume to say that
such transition was necessary : in neither must one
say, that there were no other means by which God
might have brought about this new state of things :
but when that new and better state is being brought
to pass, or is wholly realised, one may infer that such
was the end, which these temporary inflictions were
to answer . Such was the feeling which supported

Spener and other pious men amid the evil times in
which they lived, and the constant apprehension of
times yet more evil, and chastisements yet more
severe. It was their constant hope that these im-

pending evils (whether the infliction should consist
in the increased dominion of Rome or in any other
means) would be but " the pangs of travail, which
should bring after them a blessed and a joyous birth."


407


In this assurance also did the pious Reinhard find
support in the evil days which Spener and the rest
anticipated ; and this assurance did he endeavour,
when the political, moral, and religious condition of
his nation was at its farthest ebb, to impart to others
as a stay and as a principle of action .      In his words ¹
I will convey my own sentiments.             " In a world
where, from time to time, dreadful disorders take
place, where amid the weight of universal distress,
thousands    find   their   death, -happy       is the col-
lected, thoughtful man, who loses not his courage,
and in the      midst   of the    direful confusion,      sees

the   preparation     for something better, the com-
mencement of a happier future.           In nature it is a
law, we all know, that the wild storm is followed by
a refreshing calm , that the darkest night alternates
with a cheerful dawn, that         out of the struggle of
the elements a fairer order of things proceeds : in
the most mournful dissolution , there lie the germs of
a new life, the materials of a more joyous creation .
The moral world also has its laws, and they are not
less beneficial.    It is true that here also, it often
becomes dark ; there often arises suddenly a power,

which threatens to cast every thing into fetters ; dis-
orders manifest themselves which cannot end other-
wise than in the most lamentable confusion ; all
nobler powers       are often so      mutilated,    so   over-
whelmed, that the hapless race of man seems to



  ¹ The following passage is the introduction to a sermon deli-

408


stand still exhausted , or even to sink back into the
state of ancient barbarism .    He who feels for our

race, my hearers, must often be filled with sorrow
and dismay at such a sight.     But wholly to lose one's
courage, to despair of a happy termination, is neither
rational nor Christian. They who believe in a God
who rules the world, and cannot neglect a race to
whom He has given His Son, -they never despond,
for they see in the midst of the night the first glim-
merings of a new day, in the midst of slavery the
movements towards a greater freedom, in the midst
of confusion the preparations for a fairer order, and
in the dullest stand-still the exertions for a speedier
progress ; to them it is certain that the providence of
God is never more active than when it seems to do
nothing ; never does it prepare itself for greater vic-
tory than when every thing appears to be lost.
  " We celebrate to-day the memory of one of the
greatest and most beneficial changes which ever took
place on earth ; for to the Reformation of the six-
teenth century we owe the light in which we live,
the freedom which we enjoy, the order which is
given to our most important concerns, and a great
portion of that progress which our race since that
time has in every way made.     But look back beyond
that event, and form to yourselves an image of the
condition which preceded it.    Was it not a darkness
full of horrors, a slavery which would fain enchain the
spirit itself, a confusion, in which all bounds were
removed, a stand-still, in which we advanced not
one step on the path to perfection ?     And yet that
happy change, which we this day recall to mind,


409


did not merely follow upon this terrible condition ;
it was developed out of it¹ :             it was, with all its
blessings, a fruit and effect of that mournful past.
   " It is much which I here maintain, and it may

at first appear rather surprising and strange, than
true and well-founded.    But hear my proofs . I
think that I can show that the great event which
we this day celebrate, arose out of the preceding
circumstances ; that the evils which before prevailed
must of necessity, under God's guidance, produce
it.   No mode of considering these effects can un-
der the present circumstances , be more instructive .
For we cannot conceal from ourselves that we live in
times of new corruption ; in times, in which unbelief
threatens to spread its darkness over every thing ;
the lust of dominion, to oppress every thing ; selfish-
ness to devour every thing, and indifference to all
which is above the cognizance of the senses, or di-
vine, to annihilate anew the education of our race
for its high destinies .      If we would not, under such
circumstances, be hurried along with the stream, or

we see in the events of this festival, with our own
eyes, that the purposes of God, even in the most un-
happy times, did not fail of their completion ; that

ourselves are dubious, when our fate becomes conti-

 " Let us then, on this very ground, hope for a joyful
developement out of the present corruption.        Who
can refute me when I call the present state of the
world a   corrupt state ?' When I compare our
situation with that which preceded the Reformation ?
when I maintain that , as the Church then required
a reformation in its head and members, so it does
now?      But should we despair, because we must con-
fess to ourselves that this alarming corruption in-
creases, with a fearful rapidity, and becomes more
and more threatening ? We must lament, my
brethren, we must mourn with tears every unhappy
being who becomes a prey to unbelief, who falls a
sacrifice to oppression, who perishes in the universal
confusion ; but let us not fear aught for the cause of
God and Christ, for the good of our race gener-
ally.     Let the darkness    of unbelief be never so
alarming, the Gospel of Jesus Christ will maintain
itself,   and   come forth    out of it with increased

splendor.  Let the fall of every thing true and good
upon earth appear to extend irresistibly, so much the

more powerfully will He interpose, who must rule,
until He put all His enemies under His feet.       If we
are genuine disciples of the Reformation , my brethren,
if we have the faith and the hope which the Gospel
restored by that Reformation inspires, we must firmly
believe, that the hand of God will develope a better
state of things out of the present corruption. "


412


  The confident expectation which the pious Rein-
hard derived from a firm faith in his God and Sa-
viour, is now, in some measure, realized.       He, in
the midst of the      night,   was fully assured    that,
(whether sooner or later man might not conjec-
ture,)   a brighter   morning     would come,   a   new
spring would bring with it a fresh resurrection of
spiritual nature ; and that the present dreary winter
was permitted in order to prepare for it.       He felt
fully convinced from the survey of God's previous
dealings with his Church that this would be : and
may not we, who, besides this experience, witness not
only the first genial days of spring, but also the fair
blossoms and much of the promise of the future
abundant fruit,-may not we venture to trace here
the   hand   of   God,   who is    the author both of
' cold and heat, summer and winter, day and night,'
without being supposed to love the darkness of the
night, or the chillness of the winter ?

  The anticipated day has now for some time dawn-
ed, the vital warmth of spring is now returning :
the spiritual world in Germany is putting off the
dreary garb of winter ; it is starting up in the fresh
vigour of renovated life.      It were vain indeed to
attempt to disguise from one's self, that there are
yet many vestiges of the rigour of the past season :
that in different portions of that desolated country,
the restoration varies very considerably : that there
are, who prefer the winter's frost, with all its wild
and fantastic imagery, to the calmer though fresher
face of vernal nature. Still the crisis is past there

must be, and is, very different progress towards a
                         5


413


better state ; yet still there is every where progress :
the stern dominion of Rationalism is broken and
gone.                                            .
         Even among those who have not yet arrived
at genuine Christianity, the contented confidence in
the past negative system is disappearing : Ration-
alism is no longer regarded even among them as a
proof of superiority of intellect : the empty bubble
hopes of perfecting Christianity, of improving the
form in which our Saviour and God's immediate in-
struments     gave it to us,        have    burst.    However
various be the forms in which the reviving spirit
may display itself, however imperfect the revival,
still there is extensive re-animation , still the direc-
tion is towards pure Christianity : be the course
straight or devious, slow or rapid, still the eyes are
turned homewards, still the heart of the wanderer is
 dissatisfied with his present condition , and longing
 for the rest and peace of his home, for the joy of his
 Father's countenance .         Exceptions there are, and
 must be, yet the growing and predominant feeling is
 one congenial to Christianity.  It is not the Ra-
 tionalist on his cold and barren eminence, but he
 who believes in Christianity, as a power of God unto
 salvation, whose intellectual as well as religious su-
 periority is the object of respect and of admiration ' .
    The time of the final victory God only knoweth :
 but    the   victory (be      it   spoken     not   with    pre-

the affections and the feeling ; when objects of this
present scene usurp the dominion over things invi-
sible, and render the mind dull and insensible to
them ;   it matters not whether they be objects of
actual sense or of intellect ; it matters not whether
man forms his idol of the things around him, or
idolizes himself and his own intellect ; -in either
case, he will make God after his own imagined like-
ness, instead of gradually conforming himself to the
likeness which God has exhibited to us of himself ;
in either case he will degrade God into the simili-
tude of corruptible things, instead of correcting his
own corruption through the means which God has
given us.    Small is the expenditure of intellect re-
quired for such an undertaking.        Nothing could ,
even intellectually, have been more miserable than
the shallow empiricism with which the reign of un-
belief commenced ; nor do I know of any one pure
Rationalist who was endowed with a mind of extra-
ordinary calibre .   Many possessed acuteness, powers
of combination , quickness ; but none, as far as I know,
a more than ordinary depth or comprehensiveness
of mind ; while those Germans, whose minds were of
a higher order, (however defective, and consequently
unfavourable to true Christianity, parts of their
system may have been) invariably contributed more
or less to the restoration of the tone of feeling, of


415


thought, and of principle, upon which Christianity
might more favourably be ingrafted . Belief and un-
belief have both a natural hold upon the mind, and

it is not to be hoped that the conflict will entirely
cease. Yet the icy thraldom with which Rationalism
for a while ruled in Germany has given way ; against
a rationalistic Christianity Rationalism may triumph ;
when contrasted , —not with a mere negative supra-

naturalism, which distorts the      proportions of the
Christian system ,-but with a living and active Chris-
tian faith, which, grateful for the means of proof
afforded of the divinity of that revelation, still re-
joices principally in the holy and purifying character
of its doctrines, Rationalism can as little boast of any
intellectual superiority, as of any superior efficacy in
elevating man's spiritual capacities.   It is fitted only
for a degraded state of intellectual powers, as well
as of moral sense and religious feeling.
   The most sanguine must confess that the present
religious state of Germany is very imperfect ; but it
is no longer a stand-still .   Many I heard of, others
I saw in Germany, who had formerly been cold
Rationalists, but who were now in different degrees
approximating to the fullness of Christianity.    From
the stage, in which the one great miracle of our
Saviour's resurrection was held, as the basis of the
Christian revelation, from this stage onwards there
was progress ; there was a deep moral earnestness
of mind, which becomes at last too strong for the
shallow compromises of any system of Rationalism :
others, who had the mental habits of fewer years to


416


conquer, were become zealous, pious, and humble
Christians.   Among many theological students (I
can speak of course but of comparatively few from
my own knowledge, ) I witnessed an improving Chris-
tian spirit ; and since I have left that country, I have
received from various persons, on whom the fullest
confidence may be reposed, the gladdening tidings
of the improved state of mind at many of the Uni-
versities, and increased susceptibility for Christian
truth.

  In every complex state of things, two opposite
pictures may very easily be drawn, each to a certain

degree consistent with truth, each becoming untrue, if
presented too generally. The same facts also assume
a very different light when viewed from different
points.  A state of health, which compared to per-
fect soundness would be sickness and debility, may,

when contrasted with yet unsounder condition , mi-
nister joy the same state of weakness, which would
be a source of trouble and anxiety in one case, may

in another be a gladdening symptom of convales-
cence, a life from the dead.     The same imperfect
state of belief may,   ( according as the wayfarer is
going or returning, ) be a point of transition either
to total unbelief or to perfect faith.    Though, ac-

cordingly, it was very difficult for me, as a stranger,
to appreciate the relative character of many systems,
differing widely from my own and that of my Church,
or to judge how near those who held them ap-
proached to perfect Christianity ; this at least is
absolutely certain, that the tide has decidedly turned ,


417


that it is at present setting strongly towards full
Christian belief, that the dreary sand of Rationalism
is more and more disappearing.
  Whether, now that the conflict in Germany is
apparently drawing to its conclusion , the struggle
will again pass to ourselves ; whether, as unbelief,
when driven from our land , fled to Germany, so now,
when cast out thence, it will again attempt to re-
cover its dominion amongst us, God only knows.
There are signs, which may serve to shew that our
utmost vigilance and exertion are necessary ; that we
must, even for this end alone, brighten the whole
armoury which God has given us, " of truth , of
righteousness , of faith, and of the word of God ;"

but there are other signs also , which promise that if
we be but prepared , the conflict will be neither long
nor perilous.      There is enough of fear to rouse all
our energies, enough of hope to enable us to look
with confidence to the result.        On the   one hand
there is much of the feverish and disturbed excite-

ment, which ushered in the change in Germany :
there is, and that I fear continually spreading, a
craving appetite for a desultory and superficial, and
therefore inflating knowledge, instead of the sound
cultivation of the mind which prevailed among our

forefathers ; a chilling love of intellectual enjoyment,
instead of the sterner but more wholesome school

of mental discipline ; -a change , which, by placing
constantly before the      mind what it      does rather
than what it does not know, tends alike to enervate
and to swell it.     Superficial unbelief, since it is the
proudest and most confident, is also the most dan-
                             E e


418


gerous to its victims ; and on that account the ten-

dency of the present day towards a widely-spread
but shallow knowledge, the emptiness of the objects
for which that knowledge is too often acquired, and
the consequent boastful and conceited character of

an unsound but large portion of the community,
cannot be contemplated without misgiving .        The
independent character of our nation has been also in
some measure weakened : the despotic         influence
which the general desire of this compendious unreal
knowledge has given to the periodical press, evinces
a diseased state of the public mind, similar to that
which prevailed in Germany, and is at the same time
calculated to increase it. Ill will it also be, if the
public mind continue to be directed, as it now is, so
exclusively to mere useful knowledge, to the things
of daily life, and to the natural sciences (often with
but little   reference to God, even as the God of
nature, ) as if in these were contained the sum of
human wants, the range of human knowledge : while
the more beneficial, because more humbling lessons
of practical history are neglected ; and even those
human sciences are passed by, which, while they
direct man to higher objects than those of sense,
shew him the limited extent both of his moral and

intellectual powers , his weakness and insufficiency, —
Ethics, namely, and genuine Metaphysics. Little good
is augured also, by our spurious and morbid liberalism,
in part perhaps the relics of the French revolution, in
part generated by present ignorance and consequent
indifference ;   a far more dangerous extreme than
the occasional harshness of judgment of former times,
                  12


419


which though it appear sometimes to exceed the
limits of Christian charity, and was sometimes misap-
plied, was still based on a deep sense of the inex-
pressible value of the Christian doctrines, and a
conscientious dread lest man's perverseness should
in any way compromise them.       Nor can it be con-
cealed, that while an unhealthy stimulus has been
applied at home, the renewed intercourse with the
Continent, without an object, or with worse than
none in itself, and ill directed in the selection of the
people generally visited, has impaired in some classes
the genuine stability, straight-forwardness, firmness,
and high moral principle which were wont to distin-
guish us.
  While these and some other phenomena, give
ground for apprehension, there is on the other hand
great reason for comfort and thankfulness .    It is not
namely the strength of the opponent, but our own
weakness, from which in any case aught is to be
dreaded . Much less is it the power of an enemy

already defeated, which we need apprehend, but the
treason of our own heart, our own readiness to sur-
render, our own secret unfaithfulness .       We shall
scarcely allow ourselves to be      entangled in the
snares, which another nation began to break through,
as soon as it ceased to be paralyzed ; we shall
scarcely build ourselves a palace of ice, when that of
our neighbour melted      with the first rays of the
vernal sun ; we shall scarcely deck ourselves with
the tattered garments which they are casting aside ,
as unfit for any of the purposes of raiment, for pro-
priety,   warmth,   or beauty.   German Rationalism
                         E e 2


4.20


then will not probably produce any direct mischief
amongst us.    Each nation has its own distinct trials,
its own separate struggles, as it has a separate exis-
tence and a distinct character.   The combat in each
will have something peculiar, corresponding to the
peculiar habits of each ; something in common, in con-
sequence of the general uniformity of human nature,
or of the ineffaceable, though distant likeness , de-
rived from a common national origin.            Indirectly,
however, German Rationalism, or French Material-
ism, may operate upon us, if the cold degeneracy of
the intellect, or the degraded state of the moral
sense, in which these originated, be allowed to find
place among us.    Yet with the increase , or perhaps
only the increased manifestation of evil, there has
been, I trust, a more than proportionate increase of
good.   There is increased domestic piety, invigorat-
ed warmth, enlarged zeal ; at least, the descriptions
of practical Christianity amongst us in some past
decennia, would not justly characterize the present.
There still remains in the English nation , notwith-
standing some drawbacks, a solid practical character,
which would guard us against many of the aberrations
that took place in Germany : we shall hardly allow
ourselves long to be deceived by that vague and mystic

jargon of a spurious philosophy, which has already
commenced among       us,    which,   without    any   real
depth, captivates the unwary by apparent paradox
or novelty, but which requires only that the fantas-
tic garb be withdrawn to discover its real emptiness,
and the dulness of an art which consists but in a
translation of common-place sentiment into unmean-


421


ing bombast.       The physical expositions of the Chris-
tian miracles again, which held in Germany a very
pernicious, although short reign, are too preposterous
to be received where the mind has not been already
weakened ; too degrading, where the exalted charac-
ter of our Saviour has in any degree been duly
estimated, where a knowledge of it has grown with
our own growth, and with our endeavours to imitate
it.    A knowledge indeed, and an increasing (how-
ever inadequate ) appreciation of that super-human
character is a panoply of proof against every diffi-
culty which may assault the intellect.   Again, not
to mention more cases , that other favourite weapon

of modern unbelief, the parallelism between certain
points of the Christian doctrines , and those of dif-
ferent systems of heathen           religion has no force
against the shield of Christian faith : on a growing
practical acquaintance       with    revealed truth, these
comparisons, which have been so endlessly accumu-
lated, serve to shew not the resemblance, but the
difference between the genuine lineaments of the
religion of God, and the spurious devices of the
human      mind.    To the    inexperienced eye or the
hasty glance, there may be resemblance ; he who
has learnt to know the value of the Christian doc-
trines in the hours of temptation, of weakness, of
struggle, of distress, when " without were fightings,
and within were fears," will " know of the doctrine
whether it be of God."

      The history then of the late revolutions furnishes
both warning and comfort.   It were indeed but a
distant consolation for us who are at present in


422


peace under the healing shade of the Gospel, that
this tree so small in its beginnings, will continue
to refresh with its shadow our sons' sons and their
posterity, although many in our own days should
prefer the parching of the noon-day heat, or even
ungratefully strip the branches stretched out for
their peace.   Comfort, however, it would still be.
Yet our immediate comfort and our warning is the
same   twice has it been proved, in the decay as
in the revival of Christianity in Germany, that it
is only a dead faith, a superficial belief, that un-
belief can overpower : be our faith re-kindled , more
energetic, more warm, more purifying , and the gates
of hell will not prevail even for a time against this
our portion of the Church of God.


ADDITIONAL NOTES.


ADDITIONAL NOTES.




                     [Note to p. 21.]


IN the chapter on Episcopacy I have been considering
the grounds upon which Mr. Rose seemed to me to argue,
that Episcopacy would have hindered German Rational-
ism. I ought, however, to state that it has been suggested
to me, by one whose opinion I much respect, that an
adherence to episcopacy might in another way have af-
fected the fortunes of the German Church. He observes
very justly that " a strict and minute dogmatism is ob-
viously less necessary to preserve the framework of an
Episcopal Church than of any other," and that thus that
spirit, to which I myself in part attributed the prevalence
of Rationalism might in some measure have been the fruit
of the absence of Episcopacy. I am not prepared to say
that such might not have been the case : although that
minute speculative spirit appeared to me, when studying
the history of those times, to have originated in circum-
stances attending the Reformation, and in the character
of the leading opponents of Melanchthon, not in the form
of church government.    As this party formed the great
majority of the German Church, were at all times the
most influential body, and after the death of Melanchthon,
possessed nearly the whole sway, I do not think that these
men would less have fixed the character of the German
Church, or that its fate would have been altered, had they


426


possessed episcopal authority.   Without venturing to
speak positively upon this question, I may observe that
this supposition assigns only an additional cause of the
spurious dogmatism of the succeeding age : the absence
of episcopacy thus becomes but a cause of a cause of Ra-
tionalism. At all events then, the omission of this, as being
an ultimate cause only, would scarcely tend to establish
the grave charge of indifference to the form of government,
whose blessings I, in common with my country are enjoy-
ing, while it leaves the immediate causes of Rationalism
the same as before.



                      [Note to p. 43.]


THAT I may not be misunderstood, I would observe that
I do not of course object to the term " satisfaction" when
used in its literal sense that " God was satisfied, orjudged
it enough, that Christ should die instead of our suffering
that punishment ;" nor do I object to its being argued a
posteriori, that since this tremendous exhibition of God's
holiness took place, it must have been necessary. All
which I do object to, is the attempt to explain, by a priori
arguments, a doctrine of unsearchable depth ; -an attempt
which, like all others of the same sort, has ended in spe-
culations, at least unscriptural, often presumptuous ; and
which has frequently compromised the great doctrine ofthe
Atonement, which it was called in to support. The term
" satisfaction," in the above sense, asserts nothing more
than those of Atonement, Reconciliation, & c. and is em-
ployed in our Communion Service, and in our 31st Article ;
the object in both places being not to explain the doctrine
of the Atonement, but to accumulate the various words,
which had been used to express this doctrine, and to assert
that they belonged to Christ alone, not to any pretended
sacrifices of the Church of Rome. It is also employed in


427

the same general sense, in our collect for the 4th Sun-
day after Advent.


                [Addition to Note 1 , p . 48. ]

I OUGHT perhaps in addition to remark, that " the Ortho-
dox " was the self-appropriated name of the predominant
party in the Lutheran Church, of whom I could not but
speak unfavourably. It became (as I mentioned in the
Enquiry) as much an historical title as that of " Pie-
tist."I thought it necessary to speak of the peculiarity
which characterised that party, the exclusive claim of
Orthodoxy, while they did in fact, as a body, only adhere
to the letter of a faulty system. As I could not but de-
precate the perversion of what was in itself good, I thought
that the abuse would be more separated from what was
really right and valuable, if I invented a new term (" Or-
thodoxism”) than if I were to join the word " Orthodoxy"
with epithets implying blame.


                     [Note to p . 152. ]

THE following vindication of my employment of some
authorities which I have been alleged to have misquoted,
I think it right to insert, that I may not appear to have
glossed over any thing. For the reasons stated in p. 152.
it was necessary to reserve it to this place.
  I. Baier and Quenstedt.- On these authors I quoted, in
a note ', Twesten's observations, that " they were the best
calculated to give an image of the doctrinal theology of
that day," and that they were " destitute, not indeed of
diligence, acuteness, order, and precision, but of unpre-
judiced scriptural interpretation, philosophical depth, and
religious warmth." Upon this Mr. Rose observes, " Mr.


                    ¹ Enquiry, p. 38. note.


428


Pusey gives Twesten's severe judgment of Baier, but he
omits what Twesten adds, and what takes away the sting
of his remark, viz . that on a nearer view of the work,
much of the disgusting effect of the first impression wears
away '."   If Mr. Rose looks again at the passage more
accurately, he will find that the softening clause precedes
the very severe judgment which I quoted, and relates to
                                                  2
an entirely different subject. Twesten had been describ-
ing and condemning the external form of works on doc-
trinal theology ; he then mentions these two, as I quoted
him, "that the works of Quenstedt and Baier are best cal-
culated to give a knowledge of the doctrinal theology of
that period ;" says (as above) that they are not destitute of
diligence, &c. and (in reference to what had preceded) that
" on a closer study much which is calculated, at first sight,
to deter (abschreckend) vanishes ;" " but (he adds) they
are wanting in unprejudiced scriptural interpretation, phi-
losophical depth, and religious warmth." I might have
added a yet severer sentence : " Doctrinal theology is a
piece of mechanism of the logical understanding. " It is
then apparent that I had no occasion to quote a sentence
which the context, position, and punctuation shewed to
have no reference to what I did cite, and which belonged
to a very subordinate part, of which I was not speaking.
   2. Hutter. The whole of my note upon Hutter³ is too
long to be here extracted. The substance is , that after
giving instances of his polemical spirit towards the Re-
formed Church and the school of Melanchthon, I said,
" the work of Hutter, as a system of theology, is not suffi-
ciently connected , is unpractical and meagre ; as a com-
pendium it is overburthened with refined discussions ;
twenty pages, for example, being devoted to the develope-
ment of the doctrine of the union of the two natures in
Christ, and the communicatio idiomatum."       I said again,

that " its principal object was to combat the errors of the
Romanists and the Reformed." Mr. Rose observes, " In

however, Twesten's " very favourable opinion" has no

  3. Omission of Höpfner and Hollaz .-The passage upon

Enquiry. After having spoken of the matter of the doc-

Twesten's description of its form ; first the causal as Ca-

I said, " not the obscurest nor the most abounding in meta-
physical terminology is the    Systema Theologiæ 29 defi-
nitionibus absolutum' of Scherzer, in which the definition
concerning Christ occupies three 4to pages in a single
period." Having extracted part of this definition from
Schröckh, I added ; " Yet these books satisfied all the
wants of that age ; an acquaintance with the scholastic
terminology and the topics of controversy, joined to a
copious collection of Biblical passages, whose relevance
was a point of inferior importance to their numbers, was
all which the ordinary theologian required ; and the favo-

430


rite class-book of the age, the most frequently commented

                                              66 matter then
Scherzer's belongs ; and since there was no

I deemed an historical fact. Of the two authors whom

considered so little worthy of notice, that Buddeus, though

431


enough the oppressive, dry, and prolix character of the

views. For he belonged to one of the very few Univer-

have been only a popular treatise.   It met indeed,
Schröckh informs us, with some approbation from the
opposite party, and was used as a lecture-book in Bruns-
wick, but this again was a district in which the Formula
of Concord was not introduced.

says is this,      Paucis quidem et nervose, multa auctor
complexus est ; sed per nimium brevitatis et aкpißriaç
studium effecit ut sceleton aliquod exhiberet , succo omni
et sanguine destitutum.' Surely it would be mere justice
to give the praise with the blame ; and in mentioning the
fault, not to omit the reason which in some degree pal-
liates it."
   My object, however, here, as in other cases, was, not to
praise or blame König, but to give an idea of the charac-
ter of the works which were admired in the 17th century,
being, as they were, the result of a previous and existing
state of things, and tending to continue or increase it.
As one of these books, I mentioned König ; and even had
the words of Buddeus conveyed praise, I still do not see,
that since I was speaking of it not in reference to the
character of the author, but of the reception with which
it met, and the effect which it was calculated to produce,
there was any unfairness in mentioning as an illustration
in a note, Buddeus ' expression, that it was " sceleton
quoddam sine succo et sanguine," without entering into
the causes which contributed to render it so. I do, how-
ever, much doubt whether the expression of aкpißɛía really
convey praise from the context ' in this passage, as well
as from Buddeus's use of it elsewhere, I should rather

  1 This context, of which I mentioned part in my Enquiry, (p. 37,
note, ) is, " Terminos quos vocant, metaphysicos, sine necessitate
cumulat, interdum nec recte adplicat ; hinc litibus et quæstionibus
nihil commodi adferentibus occasionem præbet ; in probationibus sub-
inde deficit, dum dicta scripturæ, quæ nihil ad rem faciunt, adducit.
Celebritatem itaque quam olim hoc compendium nactum est, non aliunde
factam, quam quod eo tempore theologia scholastica in ecclesia nostra
hinc inde regnaret." The word is employed in a similar sense by Pfaff,
(a writer of the same school , ) who speaks of " argutiæ illæ scholastica
in quibus dкpiẞear theologicam quandoque miro instituto ponere,
nescio quâ rerum venustate ducti, solemus. " Progr. Invitator. ad
Colleg . Theol. Dogm. p . 6.

think that it described that painful and minute character
which belonged to the scholastic theology, and which, by
its interminable anatomizing, is likely to render a system
of doctrine a pale and lifeless skeleton ; " sceleton aliquod
exhiberet, succo omni et sanguine destitutum." Stäudlin '
also says, without any qualification, that " König pressed
together the whole of the existing scholastic Protestant
theology into a skeleton-like compendium."
